#6937. 7 Peter Millar Staples That Work Wherever the Day Takes You

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Good clothes, fewer decisions. What’s not to like?

Getting dressed for just about anything is a whole lot easier when you have the right essentials in your closet. Whether you’re heading into the office, catching a flight, or packing for a weekend away, a few well-chosen pieces can take you from one plan to the next without having to give your outfit much thought.

That’s the appeal of Peter Millar’s Crown Collection, which brings together classic menswear with thoughtful details, quality fabrics, and a relaxed approach to dressing well. There’s an emphasis on comfort and practicality, but never at the expense of looking polished. From tailored trousers and half-zips to quilted layers and other everyday favorites, these are pieces you'll find yourself reaching for again and again.

Ahead, seven picks from the collection, available at Bloomingdale’s, that deserve a spot in your closet. Because looking good shouldn’t require a whole lot of effort.

A chore coat is a great casual Friday-to-weekend staple when you want to look put together without feeling too dressed up. This navy style has a relaxed, cardigan-like feel, with a classic collar and patch pockets that work just as well with a T-shirt and jeans as they do with a button-down and chinos.

The Essex Vest is an iconic piece within Peter Millar’s Crown Collection and is designed as a versatile layering option for fall that works across a range of occasions. This black diamond-quilted style offers an extra layer of warmth without the bulk of a jacket, making it just as useful for your morning commute as it is for a round of golf or an afternoon apple picking.

Few pieces strike the balance between comfort and looking sharp quite like a good half-zip. This navy pullover is comfortable enough for a long flight but polished enough to wear over a collared shirt with tailored trousers. Prediction: You’ll want it in multiple colors.

A quilted coat is timeless, especially in a rich, dark olive shade. This one has a classic countryside feel, but its clean shape looks just as at home on city streets with jeans and boots or layered over a button-down and trousers. A bottle opener tucked inside the left interior pocket adds a handy signature touch. It’s a smart choice for chilly days that don’t call for a heavy overcoat.

Utilitarian pants are a wardrobe icon, and this five-pocket design offers a slightly more refined take on the classic. The smooth sateen fabric and rich blue color give them a polished look that works with everything from a polo at the office to a crisp shirt and loafers for dinner.

Every man’s shoe collection should include a pair of driving loafers, particularly when comfort and looking elegant are equally important. These walnut-brown suede drivers feature classic penny-loafer detailing and flexible, rubber-studded soles, making them a natural choice with chinos, tailored trousers, or jeans.

A checked button-down is a classic for a reason, but how about one with a little more personality? This soft twill style features a small-scale pattern and subtle grapeseed color that look particularly great with navy, gray, or khaki. Designed to be tucked in, it pairs easily with tailored trousers for work or sateen five pocket pants for a more relaxed look.

#6932. Iowa Governor-Elect Rob Sand Was Also a Prada Model

On November 3, as part of the blue-vote tsunami, Iowa elected Democratic Governor Rob Sand. The 44 year-old graduated with a political science degree from Brown University and a J.D. degree from University of Iowa College of Law; he even got a Truman scholarship and an honors thesis. And his 2026 campaign was centered on anti-corruption, bipartisanship, affordable living, and public education. But there’s more—he’s also a pretty face.

As it turns out, the governor-elect helped pay for his prestigious college degrees by walking runways in Milan and Paris. At six-feet, three inches, the young, aspiring politician modeled in shows for Giuliano Fujiwara and Number (N)ine. He even made it into a Prada runway, Newsweek reports. It was the early 2000s, and he was reportedly signed to none other than IMG Models—though his profile seems to have been taken down since.

For whatever reason, his modeling past is not something the chiseled governor-elect likes to talk about, the New York Times noted, but it sure makes for quite the resume.

On January 19, 2004, Sand even missed the Iowa caucus in Decorah because he was modeling menswear in Milan. “It had to be one or the other,” he told Newsweek. “And I chose to go to Europe.” (Who wouldn’t?) While Sand did not win that time around, look at him now, years later, up at the top.

Sand took Iowa this week while in a sharp navy-blue suit and a sky-blue checkered button-down that matched his eyes. “Thank you, Iowa! I love you, Iowa!” he excitedly said after catwalking up to the podium for his victory speech.

Rob Sand’s team has not responded to Harper’s Bazaar’s request for comment.

#6929. Reviewing Ross Lynch’s Low-Key Relationship History Now That He’s a Married Man (!!)

Yes, his wife’s identity is still mostly a mystery.

If you missed the headlines this morning, Ross Lynch is now a married man…and if you’re thinking, “Um, I didn’t even know he was dating anyone,” you’re not alone. Despite hard-launching his relationship status at the Heartland premiere—telling People, “Yes, yes I am,” when asked if he was married—the actor and musician has yet to even confirm his wife’s name, much less how they met or how long they’ve been together.

Luckily, I’ve done the work of piecing together Ross’s very low-key relationship history for you, from his Teen Beach Movie love triangle, to his six-year relationship with his Chilling Adventures of Sabrina costar, to his mysterious new wife (!!). Scroll ahead for everything I’ve uncovered.

Okay, it’s unclear if these two actually dated, but Ross did tell Cosmo (hi!) during an April 2019 interview that he was involved in “a love triangle” on the set of Disney’s Teen Beach Movie.

“I was 16 at the time of the first movie, but it was basically a bunch of high school students or college students,” he told Cosmo (via Seventeen). “And they just put us on an island in the Caribbean for two months.”

He never named who was involved in the love triangle, but this flirty Instagram pic ^^ that he posted with backup dancer Morgan Larson in July 2013 led fans to believe that something possibly went down between the two.

Again, this one was only ever rumored, but fans suspect that Ross’s Teen Beach Movie costar Maia was possibly the other person involved in the supposed “love triangle.” The reason? While chatting with Hollywood Life in June 2015, Ross shared that he and Maia had an unscripted kiss in the sequel film.

“Maia and I did a kiss spontaneously,” he said. “We thought that Mac and Brady should have an onscreen kiss. It wasn’t scripted and the director didn’t tell us to do it and we just did it and hopefully it makes it in the movie!” Oh, and he also added that he thought Maia was a good kisser. Moving on!

In September 2015, Ross became Instagram-official with Yellowjackets star Courtney after reportedly meeting on the set of the movie Status Update. The two went on to date for just about two years before going their separate ways, with Courtney being described as Ross’s “then-girlfriend” during a November 2017 interview with W.

A month later in December 2017, Ross and Courtney were spotted having lunch together, but it doesn’t seem like they ever officially reconciled.

Dating rumors about these two began shortly after Ross and Jaz met on the set of Chilling Adventures of Sabrina, but they really ramped up after Ross’s sister Rydel shared a Snapchat of Ross and Jaz kissing on New Year's Eve in 2018. However, when asked about their relationship by Entertainment Tonight later that month, Ross insisted that Jaz was just his “closest friend.” Mmkay.

Just a few months later in April 2019, Us Weekly caught the couple getting cozy in NYC, and at long last in January 2020, the costars finally made their red carpet debut together at the Balmain show in Paris.

The couple kept fans swooning for the next couple of years until June 2023, when a fan shared a video of Lynch discussing heartbreak during one of his live shows with his band The Driver Era, sparking breakup rumors. “The thing about heartbreak is the only thing that really helps is music,” he told the audience at the time.

Following his split from Jaz (and rumors of a brief relationship with a woman named Indie Wolper), Ross seemingly soft-launched a new romance on TikTok in September 2024, which sent fans into a frenzy.

A few months later, Ross hinted that there was someone new in his life during a January 2025 interview with radio show Smallzy’s Surgery. Talking about The Driver Era song “Same Old Story,” Ross said, “It’s like really recent—like, maybe, I’m talking like a month ago. It’s about this experience I’ve been having recently with this girl.”

Reddit sleuths got to work and ID’d the new mystery woman as Anna, claiming the two met in Portugal. By early 2026, fans took to Reddit to claim that—when asked about his ring at a meet-and-greet—Ross confirmed he’d been married since before Christmas 2025.

Then, after a nearly year-long hiatus from Instagram, Ross reappeared on social media in June 2026, with his first photo showing him wearing what appeared to be said wedding ring.

“Been a minute, what did I miss?” he captioned the post, and when a fan slid into his comments to write, “more like what did WE miss ? 🤨💍” Ross replied, “hahaha A LOT 🤫.”

Finally, Ross cleared things up at the premiere of his new movie Heartland in November 2026, telling People, “Yes, yes I am,” when asked if he was married.

“I’m not trying to shy away from that by any means, but I am trying to, you know, my wife’s not very fond of celebrity,” he said of keeping his relationship on the down-low. “So I am trying to be sensitive towards that and respectful to her. But everything’s going well.”

#6921. Will There Be Fewer Gowns at the Met Gala Next Year?

Announced today, the theme of the 2027 exhibition explores fashion’s obsession with everyday garments like T-shirts, jeans, and trench coats

Just over two months ago, the Met Costume Institute canceled its John Galliano retrospective, the original theme for the 2027 exhibition, amid donor and patrons outcry—today, it has announced the new direction, and it’s as far from Galliano’s fantastical fashions as possible.

The 2027 Costume exhibition and corresponding gala is titled “extraOrdinary: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday.” It will explore how designers reimagine quotidien ideas for the high-fashion world and how wearing, styling, and caring for these garments shapes their significance over time.

The exhibition will be divided into two larger themes. The first, “Familiar Forms,” will center archetypal garments like jeans, T-shirts, biker jackets, and trench coats, looking at how designers reinvent these pieces and play with the symbolism surrounding them. The other, “Common Matter,” will focus on disposable materials like paper, aluminum, and plastic, and how they have been elevated to fashion.

“The ordinary is never quite as simple as it appears,” said Andrew Bolton, Curator in Charge of the Costume Institute. “A pair of jeans, a white shirt, or a sweatshirt can hold traces of a life—the habits, memories, and attachments that make something our own. Designers work with that familiarity, revealing unexpected possibilities within forms and materials we think we already know. The exhibition invites us to discover how much imagination the everyday can hold, and how looking again might change what we value and care for.”

Bolton began where he left off last year with “Costume Art,” and the reality of placing the body at the center of your design process. He was particularly interested in moments like Jun Takahashi’s fall 2024 collection for Undercover, titled “Watching a Working Woman,” which was inspired in part by Wim Wenders’s 2023 film Perfect Days. He created ensembles that joined and layered familiar garments in new ways to subvert and complicate traditional perceptions of identity and form. The exhibition will open with pieces from this collection and expand to include over 200 items, including Matthieu Blazy’s trompe l’oeil leather jeans for Bottega Veneta, pieces from Marc Jacobs’s 1990s Perry Ellis grunge collection, Brooke Shields’s Calvin Klein jeans, Junya Watanabe’s leather biker jackets, Charles James’s 1930s couture puffer coat, and more.

The original theme, which would have centered on Galliano’s life and work, was initially announced in late July. After donors and critics spoke out against the designer’s past antisemitic and anti-Asian remarks, it was then recalled in late August. This decision was unprecedented; the Met had never made such a late-stage switch-up.

Galliano was known for his objectively extraordinary work subverting themes of historical excess and grandeur. His shows were more like theater productions. Now, it seems they’ve chosen a balm for the raucous in skewing in the complete opposite direction, promoting real life over fantasy. That said, this new theme is purported to examine familiar ideas through the extraordinary lens of fashion. There is certainly nothing ordinary about a Junya Watanabe biker jacket.

There is certainly much to explore here. The theme reminds us how much inspiration comes from the people walking down the street every day, the uniforms that have shaped our perceptions of society. Those ideas are ripe material for fashion designers’ creative minds —and can cause quite the stir among audiences. Just think of how shocked the industry was after Marc Jacobs elevated the way youth culture were dressing, what we call “grunge,” to the runway with his 1993 collection for Perry Ellis. Or the fascination with those optical illusion leather jeans Matthieu Blazy put on his Bottega Veneta runways a few years ago. After all, fashion is only fantastical relative to what we deem commonplace. We need a bit of the the familiar to help us digest something radical.

#6920. Designers Say These 6 Paint Colors Are the Secret to a More Vintage-Looking Home

If you have a brand-new home or just bought a builder-grade house, you may find that your walls are whispering—or maybe even begging—for some character. Or perhaps your home already has vintage bones and tapping into its history feels right. Whatever the case, picking a vintage-inspired paint color can help add a sense of heritage and character to the space before you even spend a dime on furniture or accessories.

Some colors have a modern feel, while others are distinctively old-fashioned. For that nostalgic touch, we consulted interior designers about their go-to paint colors that read as vintage, from charming yellow to dramatic burgundy.

Inspired by the English fine china brand, this soft, dusty shade of blue-gray feels like it’s been living in a room for decades. “It’s the kind of color you find in old European farmhouses or well-loved coastal cottages,” says Jessica Risko Smith, interior designer at Santa Barbara firm JRS ID. “It reads differently in every light: almost slate in the morning, almost soft denim by afternoon.” Her team used it throughout this kitchen renovation to ground the space and give brand-new cabinetry an immediate sense of age and permanence.

This creamy yellow hue skyrocketed in popularity over the last few years, but it’s always been a go-to for cozy, lived-in interiors. Andrew Suvalsky, a New York City-based interior designer, says, “Butter yellow is a color that is as strong as it is youthful and playful. It’s a bridge straight to the 1960s and is as relevant today as it was then.” Whether used on a ceiling or in a kitchen, it adds a sun-drenched character you would find in a home that's been loved for decades.

Rich, saturated tones like oxblood or deep plum instantly add a sense of history and depth to a space. This hue “brings a refined, almost heritage-inspired warmth that feels both dramatic and timeless,” says Iona Graham, interior design expert at Graham & Brown. “It’s particularly effective in smaller rooms or on accent walls, where it can create a cocooning, layered atmosphere reminiscent of classic interiors.” With its moody saturation, this vintage-inspired paint color is also perfect for color drenching.

White is one of those hues that comes in a surprising range of shades. Some lean blue and bright, others feel more golden. A warm parchment shade sits somewhere in the middle, offering a timeless, lived-in take on this go-to color family. “Nothing gives a new build an instant sense of history like a genuine warm parchment—not stark white, but something with yellow or blush undertones that softens edges and feels sun-faded in the best possible way,” Smith says.

For an even deeper infusion of warmth, a peach hue with rosy undertones will do the trick. This bold hue has shown up across the decades, with splashier iterations in the 1980s and softer, muted examples found across the midcentury. Suvalsky says, “This color is great because it is both fresh and cozy at the same time. It works in all seasons and across all eras of furnishings, bringing the romance of golden hour and a subtle retro feeling.”

If you’re thinking blue—but make it saturated—a paint inspired by the crisp night sky will do. It’s rooted in nature, and that’s one of the very reasons why it carries so much vintage appeal. “It is regal, historic, and architectural all at the same time,” Suvalsky explains. “It structures a room as though it’s been there forever, and knows why.” In this wet bar project, deep midnight blue contrasts against a gilded ceiling, warm wood tones, and brass fixtures.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6918. The Best Mini PCs to Buy in 2026

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These high-performing, small-footprint models will clean up your workspace.

Tiny computers are nothing new. I know you have one in your pocket right now—that is, if you’re not reading this on it already. The smartphone proved that computers can be anything, and anything can be a computer. Even putting aside the smartphone and the laptop, desktops have been getting smaller and changing form over time. The mini PC (sometimes also called a NUC) is a term that’s getting a lot of use lately, but it’s for something that’s more or less existed for decades: a small, prebuilt computer.

Traditionally, a PC runs Windows, but you can find Linux-based and MacOS-based mini PCs too. Of course, Apple makes its own in the form of the Mac Mini, probably the most popular one on the market. The original version debuted back in 2005, proving my point that these are nothing new, and has made its way into millions of homes without needing anyone to utter the words “mini PC.”

Point is, if you’re looking for something with the capability of a desktop without wanting a space-hogging tower, this is what you need to be looking at. Whether you need a machine for school, work, or gaming, these are the most capable desktops that come in shockingly small packages. These four machines are the best mini PCs to buy right now.

Starting off, it’s the 2026 Mac Mini. For the average person, you can start and stop the shopping journey here. If you want a compact home computer and you’re already deeply embedded in MacOS and Apple’s ecosystem, it’s hard to resist the simple appeal of the Mac Mini M6. It’s unassuming, smaller than a cable box and about the size of your internet modem. Ninety percent of the time, it’s as quiet as either of those too, despite being a fast mid-range computer optimized for AI workloads (more so than any generation before it). In terms of connections, you get a full stack; three Thunderbolt 4 ports, an HDMI port, and an Ethernet port in the back, as well as two USB-C ports and a headphone jack in front. More than plenty for all your accessories.

Like I said, this is a mid-range Mac. It’s just as suited for a home office as the main family computer in the living room. For something with professional level capabilities (we are all professionals, but I’m talking video editing, software development, any 3D model rendering), you’ll want the one with the M5 Pro chip instead. As always, with either of these, the biggest downside is Apple’s walled garden. You can’t upgrade the RAM or storage capacity like you can with other PCs, and Apple isn’t afraid to charge an arm and a leg for the versions that come with extra.

If you’re in both a time and money crunch, this is the kind of computer you want. For less than $400, it’s a Windows-capable machine with 16 gigs of RAM that can output to up to three 4K displays at a time. And unless you are pushing its processing power, it stays cool and relatively silent. It’s got all the ports you’d want—USB, HDMI, DPI, headphone jack—plus two high-speed USB-A ports in the back. These are decent for when you have large files to transfer, but they admittedly pale in terms of speed compared to a Thunderbolt port.

At the price it’s currently at, this is also cheaper than any gaming console out there. Not even as powerful as a Nintendo Switch (the old one), but it can run Minecraft and other casual or indie titles with little trouble.

If your job or home business requires running more and more local AI models (as many do), you might want a mini PC designed with these workflows in mind. Plus, it has every kind of port you’d want and more. With HDMI, USB, Thunderbolt, Ethernet, even an SD card slot and Copilot button, it’s fully kitted out for even the more niche uses.

It’s not a perfect solution. This machine will struggle with more intensive tasks and you’ll hear about it. For its size and light frame (it’s easily mountable to a monitor stand), though, it’s nothing to sneeze at.

Any machine can run some video games, but if you’re the type who considers gaming your hobby of choice, and you need something to play all the latest releases in 4K, well, then you need something as dedicated as you. For a reasonable price tag (trust me, it is), you’re getting a powerful PC with an RTX 5060 graphics card and 32 gigabytes of RAM. For the completely uninitiated, that’s a mid-range 2025 dedicated graphics card—sure to run any modern game, but when it comes to more demanding titles you just may have to choose to prioritize either frame rate or visuals.

Beyond gaming, this is a capable machine due to the decent GPU. And for those who don’t want to tinker at all, the G1 Pro offers three default modes to quickly swap between. Swap from Office to Gaming when it’s time to play, or go into Beast mode when you’re really trying to throttle the machine and get the most out of it.

The real reason to get something like this over a console or Valve’s Steam Machine is that it’s upgradeable. The storage and RAM can both be swapped out for newer or higher-capacity options. It’s not a full-on PC, though. With the CPU and GPU, you’re stuck with what is on board.

#6912. Please Just Tell Me Who the Next James Bond Is Already

Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, and Josh O’Connor are reportedly all up for the role. But I’m not having fun anymore.

Some movie announcements arrive as a complete surprise. Ryan Gosling as Ghost Rider? Very cool idea. Nathan Fielder secretly spending weeks staring into Elizabeth Holmes’s eyes for You Can See Everything? Amazing. In other instances, film studios will delay an announcement everyone knows is coming until their audience finds itself sucked up into the maelstrom and spun around until they’re so dizzy that they’re puking up that morning’s Cheerios. Maybe they wish they never even got excited in the first place.

That’s where I’m at with the current race for James Bond, which has reportedly come down to three actors: Jack Lowden (Slow Horses), Callum Turner (One Night Only), and Josh O’Connor (Disclosure Day). The three finalists were announced via Puck three weeks ago on October 22, along with a mysterious fourth actor whose identity has not yet been revealed, if he even exists.

Since then, there have been no official updates from Amazon/MGM—which acquired full control of the franchise in February 2025—but there have been dozens upon dozens of interviews from former Bond actors as well as anyone remotely associated with the prior 25 Bond films and novels, with each interviewee expressing that all three men up for the role would probably be great.

“All the men who are on the list now are actors I admire,” Pierce Brosnan recently told Vanity Fair. Maybe true but after a year of this guessing game, it just isn’t fun anymore. Even worse, no one seems concerned with even making it fun.

“It's an incredibly bizarre pinch-me moment... even being uttered in that same sentence and being considered for something like that,” Josh O’Connor told BBC News on Tuesday. “But I am afraid, there is nothing to say beyond that.”

Callum Turner was even more direct: “I’m not going to comment on it just because there’s no point in commenting on it in this moment,” he told Esquire UK in August.

Then, Lowden uttered “Oh, Christ” in an interview earlier this week when he finally addressed the rumors. “It’s all very flattering. It’s nice to be mentioned—for any role—and I’m just not really going to add to the noise.”

Are they not allowed to even say they want the role? Am I supposed to act excited when they finally announce if they don’t even seem excited themselves? Do we even want to see a film with Turner, Lowden, or O’Connor as James Bond?

Please add to the noise, guys. I’m begging you. It’s time to tell us.

#6911. These 7 Storage Solutions for Small Spaces Look Too Good to Hide

Display-worthy baskets, glass-fronted cabinets, and colorful tins can make organization part of your decor.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

From a Brooklyn studio to a narrow Baltimore rowhouse, two decades of small-space living have taught me how to make every square foot count. Add in my Type A Virgo tendencies and an appreciation for good design, and I have strong opinions about storage: Namely, it should be as good-looking as it is hard-working. My first stop when hunting for products that deliver just that is always IKEA. The Swedish retailer has an unmatched selection of storage furniture and decor. Here are seven pieces that offer both function and form.

I have never known the pleasure of a linen closet, but I’ve gotten so many compliments on this metal-and-glass cabinet in my bathroom that I no longer mind. The reeded glass offers an unexpected texture while obscuring what’s inside to reduce visual clutter, and each interior shelf is roomy enough to hold a stack of towels, plus a small bin for toiletries and medicine bottles. I like to use the bamboo UPPDATERA boxes for material contrast; the larger style works great on the lower shelves to tame my mess of serums, makeup, lotions, and the like.

There is no such thing as too many bins or baskets when you live in a small space. They are the unsung organizational heroes, giving you a way to stash all the miscellaneous things that would typically be tucked away in a closet, attic, basement, or even a spare room in a larger home. IKEA sells a huge variety, but the woven styles like this one are particularly lovely to keep on display. I use them on my EKET living room shelves for games, in my bedroom for scarves and bags, and in the kitchen for tea towels and napkins.

When square footage is at a premium, closets tend to get eliminated. A free-standing wardrobe is the solution to creating clothes storage in a small bedroom. This chic new design features sliding doors—a space-saver itself since you won’t need to account for door clearance—adjustable shelves, and two drawers.

If your front door, like mine, opens directly into your living space, you need to fake a foyer as best you can. The key to that is shoe storage—otherwise, guests will be staring at muddy boots the minute they walk into your home. This cabinet hides 14 pairs behind a traditional design, and sticks out from the wall less than a foot. Two drawers make space for keys and sunglasses while four hooks give bags and dog leashes a home.

All kitchens can benefit from more storage, but it’s the small cook spaces where you really need to get creative. A mobile cart offers that utility, and this one features three shelves for anything from spices and baking supplies to serveware or a coffee bar.

Another simple small kitchen storage idea: Keep your dry goods in stackable containers, like the BETTHAJ tins, to maximize your pantry shelf space. They’re far easier to handle than flimsy paper containers or plastic bags, plus, they look great on a countertop or open shelving. They come in five different sizes, from 19 oz. to 3.3 qt., so you can mix and match to suit your needs.

Why choose between extra seating or more storage when you can have both? This sturdy little pouf hides a compartment that I use for an extra throw blanket, but it could also fit a few games, your knitting supplies, or remote controls. The cushioned top is taut enough that it can also function as a side table, making this a small space triple threat.

Head to IKEA to find storage that maximizes space without sacrificing style.

#6908. A Straight Razor Will Give You the Best Shave of Your Life. Here Are 6 Great Options to Buy.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

If you get razor bumps and ingrowns, don’t be afraid to go old-school.

We’re 120 years past the invention of the safety razor and 50 years past the invention of the cartridge razor. So yes, we understand how annoying it is to even suggest that you should shave with the thing that’s even older—technically as old as shaving itself. Go ahead, make fun of the old-school dandy men’s magazine saying you should be straight-razor shaving with a shaving cream that requires a boar hair brush and smells of tobacco. But to turn shaving from a chore to something you look forward to, well, the straight razor is a one-way ticket to hobby town.

Using an electric razor or beard trimmer is great for getting rid of scruff, but if you ever go for a clean shave, a straight razor is the best tool—full stop. They are the best for your face, don’t give you as many ingrown hairs, and (unlike the five-bladed cartridge razors) don’t get clogged up with hairs after two shaves. Plus they add a sense of elegance to your everyday routine, not to mention a bit of danger, à la Sweeney Todd. Only joking, of course.

So sure, challenge our claim that straight-razor shaving is as good as it gets. Or better yet, try it for a little bit and then tell us we’re wrong. If you clean shave and care about grooming, consider picking up a straight razor and doing it the old-fashioned way.

Frankly, most of these straight razors aren’t even technically straight razors. They are shavettes; hybrids with disposable blades so you don’t have to worry about honing and sharpening (and all the hazards that come with failing to do so). You still get the straight razor vibe, but it’s safer and more convenient. For a starter shavette, you can pick up the Parker SRX straight edge. It’s compatible universally with any double edge blades, so you won’t ever run out.

Take care of this stainless steel beauty and it will retain that sheen for years without a sign of rust. This all makes it a great, affordable pick for intermediate shavers who prioritize performance. For a more gateway razor, I’d pick the Dovo shavette

Fundamentally, this is the same thing as the previous Parker razor. It’s a shavette that uses double-edge disposable blades, so the experience of using it and the accompanying learning curve are the exact same. The difference is the wood handle. It looks great, but if you get anything other than shaving cream on there, it’s certainly not going to be as easy to clean as stainless steel. I still wouldn’t recommend a novice pick this up, but that’s what the safety razor is for.

If you are genuinely just curious about this whole thing, you don’t need to drop even $30 on something you may use once and then put down forever. You can pick up a Classic Samurai razor from Amazon for less than 15 bucks. It’s not the best ever, and you won’t be showing off to your friends, but it’s a way to get your feet wet. Plus it comes with enough extra (safety-razor-compatible) blades that if you do find the knack for straight-razor shaving, you can upgrade to a Parker or Dovo and have plenty of blades to use.

The straight handle is your less flexible, slightly sturdier alternative to your folding shavette. This Feather razor is Japanese made with a straight grip meant to give more fine tuning and control to the average shaver. I only wish that, like Parker shavettes, it was compatible with all double-blade replacements—not just the Feather branded ones.

Whether straight handles are actually easier to control and safer than folding ones is up for debate. Barbers have their preferences and if you are looking to start shaving with a straight razor at home, so will you. If you’ve tried the folding shavettes and can’t quite get a handle (sorry for the pun) on them, this one is worth a shot.

As we exit the shavette category, I turn once again to Dovo. If you want a razor that looks and feels as traditional as it gets while still being brand new, you need a Dovo.

This isn’t for beginners or really even intermediate shavers. If anything, it’s more of a novelty. If you want to use it for real, you should probably be a real barber. If you have the skills (and the disposable income), this will be something fun to whip out for your favorite clients in need of the cleanest shave of their life.

There’s something uniquely satisfying about buying vintage. These come from the era when these were the only razors men used to shave. In a way, that’s how they know they are quality. Just old-fashioned stainless steel blades and a design that modern “retro” can’t come close to replicating.

You can buy these (one per package despite the website’s imagery) sealed or opened and cleaned Friedrich Schlemper straight razors for less than the price of a Dovo. These Sistrum razors are German-made carbon steel, likely dating back to sometime between the 1950s and the 1970s.

Classic Straight Razor: Traditionally, a straight razor has one steel (typically stainless or carbon) blade. In the old days, before the safety and cartridge razors we know and love, this was the only option. Barbers had to hone and sharpen their blades using a leather strap—and if you go classic this is still a requirement. This makes a straight razor an investment that can go south if you don’t take care of it properly. Serious professionals only—it’s not easy to learn how to shave safely with one of these. There’s a reason that as a society, we don’t use them anymore.

Shavettes: This modern hybrid of the safety razor and straight razor is what I recommend over the classic style. They are much more affordable, for starters, and the replaceable blades are much safer, especially for at-home experimentation. If you are a casual looking into straight razors, I cannot stress enough to start and end with a shavette.

Folding vs Straight Handle: In terms of shavettes, it all comes down to preference. A straight handle can be preferable to a lighter, airy folding blade handle due to better ergonomics and greater control. But others find a folding handle to allow them better control and stability. It’s all about whichever you feel allows you more precision while leading to fewer errors.

Esquire has been helping men look (and be) at their best since the heyday of the straight razor. We lived through it. If 90 years of institutional knowledge isn’t going to convince you, nothing will.

#6906. Here’s What We Know About ‘Nobody Wants This’ Season 4

We can safely say we do, in fact, want this.

Can Nobody Wants This still have that title after three seasons? The characters continue to prove it wrong with each passing episode. It might depend on which character you ask, but things continue to go well for Adam Brody and Kristen Bell’s characters. If you’ve already breezed through the new season on Netflix, and want to know what’s next, here’s what we know about the possibility of Nobody Wants This season 4.

Yes! As of November 6, Nobody Wants This has been renewed for season 4!!

As of season 3’s release, Netflix had not announced a fourth season. But this announcement tracks with how the news has been released in the past. Previous renewal announcements have come a few weeks after the previous season. For example, season 2 dropped in October of 2025 and they announced season 3 that November.

Season 3 introduced a few new characters to the opposites-attract romantic comedy including Keyla Monterroso Mejia, Avan Jogia, Sarah Silverman, Sadie Sandler, Poorna Jagannathan, and Andrew Rannells. One can imagine that, if the show continues, it will continue to fill out that roster.

Also, spoiler alert but Joanne and Noah get married at the end of season 3! You don’t need to be a sex and relationship podcaster to know that nobody’s love story ends at marriage. I’m not going to be corny and say it’s actually the beginning, but there are so many new challenges ahead for them–even though they moved in together at the beginning of season 3. Joys as well! Plus, their extended family’s lives get more complicated and messy by the day. This story is sooo not over!

All three seasons have been fairly consisted, premiered in September or October of 2024, 2025, and 2026. If the show gets renewed, we can probably expect it in September or October of 2027.

#6902. This Fan-Slash-White Noise Machine Solves Two Big Sleep Problems at Once

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

For as long as I can remember, I’ve had trouble sleeping through the night. I’ve tried taking supplements, cutting down on screen time before bed, and changing out my sheets to the softest and most lightweight ones I could find. I’ve even been to a sleep specialist. And while some of these things have helped me out a bit, it’s still pretty rare for me to fall asleep within an hour of getting into bed, and I almost never sleep through the whole night without waking up a few times.

I’ve tried white noise machines in the past, but I never found one that I truly stuck with. That changed, however, when Snooz sent me its new Breez 2 bedroom fan and white noise machine. I was immediately intrigued and excited to try it out, since my boyfriend swears by his Snooz original white noise machine—he says it’s the only thing that helps him sleep in his first-floor, street-facing apartment in midtown New York City. Plus, I’d just put my AC unit in storage for the fall and winter and desperately needed some airflow in my bedroom, so I thought, why not give it a shot?

Long story short: I’m really glad I decided to try it out, and I’ve been using it every night for the past few weeks. What makes this different from your typical white noise machine is that you can separately control Breez 2’s fan and sound, so you can use both at the same time or just one or the other depending on your needs. For example, I don’t need the white noise on during the day, but I keep the fan on to have a nice breeze going. At night, I’ve been using both, but once it starts to get a bit colder, I may just need the white noise without the fan.

And speaking of the fan, I’ve been quite pleasantly surprised with its power, considering it takes up less than a square foot. This thing actually blasts a breeze of cold air across my whole room (granted, I have a shoebox bedroom in a flexed Manhattan apartment), not just a little puff of air one foot in front of the fan. And without the white noise on, the fan makes a smooth whirring sound that’s not too loud, so if you do want a pretty quiet space, you can certainly achieve that.

When it comes to the controls, Breez 2 really sets itself apart from your typical fan or sound machine, thanks to the Bluetooth-connected app on my phone that makes it possible for me to adjust it from wherever I am (within Bluetooth connectivity, of course)—which means I can control it while I’m in bed and don’t have to get up in the middle of the night just to change the settings. Too hot? I can turn the fan up while I’m under the covers. Too loud? I can turn the volume down without even sitting up.

And if I really don’t want to do any work and let Breez 2 handle everything, there’s even an auto mode in which it detects the temperature of your room and adjusts the fan accordingly so it never gets too hot or cold. All you have to do is set a target temperature, and the fan will turn higher or lower to achieve that temperature.

I’ve also been making use of the timer feature and setting Breez 2 to turn off after two hours, so I don’t waste electricity while I’m asleep. The only issue here is that you can’t set a timer for just the fan or the white noise; it turns off the device altogether, so if I do want the fan running all night, I just have to keep the sound on, too. However, you can use the schedule feature to set a schedule for the fan and white noise ahead of time, so if you know you go to bed and wake up at the same time on certain days, you can go ahead and set that up. My sleep schedule is a little too wonky for that (but perhaps fixing that would be a good idea . . .).

Finally, from an aesthetic standpoint, it has a nice, modern, minimalist design with a light wood stand and a white body—there’s a very Scandinavian style to it. The controls on the face of the fan react with a light touch and light up, so they can even be used as a calming night-light if you want (the brightness of which can be adjusted in the app). So yeah, Snooz has really thought of everything here.

#6897. Inside the Lavish Estates Where The Gilded Age Was Filmed

These locations are straight out of 1880s high society.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why should the Brits have all the fun? The writer of Downton Abbey, Julian Fellowes, went stateside with the HBO series The Gilded Age, which tells the story of New York City high society in the 1880s amid the halcyon days of the industrial boom. The series explores the tension between old-money elites and their escalating feud with the nouveau riche neighbors who threaten the established social order. Meanwhile in real life, powerhouse families like the Vanderbilts and the Carnegies left their opulent footprints on Newport, Rhode Island, in the form of their famously ostentatious and absolutely gargantuan “summer cottages.” Circumstances which lend themselves to set-design magic.

In honor of season four of The Gilded Age, which premiered November 1, we’re revisiting the real-life settings that have helped bring the series to life. The social alliances may shift, but the architecture remains a dependable scene-stealer. Below, tour 21 properties that rank among the East Coast’s architectural crown jewels, from historic house museums to lavish mansions.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s ballroom and George Russell’s bedroom, as well as the billiard room.

These so-called dollar princesses and their families lived in Manhattan and spent their summers in Newport, Rhode Island. Of course, these industry magnates didn’t consider their infrequent visits (some only came a few weeks in a year) as a reason to exercise restraint. The mammoth Breakers mansion is one of four so-called summer “cottages” (read: castles) that were completed just blocks from each other in a building boom between 1892 and 1902. The mansion was designed by Richard Morris Hunt in the Renaissance Revival style, as a less-than-subtle symbol of the Vanderbilt family’s social and financial preeminence in the Gilded Age. It was architectural one-upmanship that is considered the grandest of all the manses, featuring ornate ballrooms, extensive art collections, manicured gardens, and the latest (then) in modern technology, including electric lights. It has 70 rooms, an interior using marble imported from Italy and Africa, and a library mantel purchased from a château in France.

“When you walk into the dining room of The Breakers, with its painting of the goddess Aurora on the ceiling 50 feet above you or see 500,000 cubic feet of marble in a variety of different hues, you can’t help but be awestruck by the incredible wealth of these Gilded Age tycoons,” says Trudy Coxe, the chief executive officer and executive director of The Preservation Society of Newport County, which has under its stewardship six of the grand mansions featured in the series.

The preservation society worked closely with the filming crew to ensure the safety of the objects and spaces. “We had long discussions about the billiard table at The Breakers,” she explains. “It could not be removed, but it was important to the scene being filmed. Rather than moving it, we worked with their props department to put an alternative covering on the table to simulate an actual game of pool.”

In the series, the Breakers’ neo–Italian Renaissance great hall and music room are featured as Bertha Russell’s (played by Carrie Coon) ballroom, which we see in the final ball scene. George Russell (Morgan Spector) also discusses business while playing billiards in the mansion’s massive billiard room.

Features as: Bertha Russell’s bedroom in the Russell residence

Marble House was built from 1888 to 1892 as a summer cottage for Alva and William Kissam Vanderbilt. It was also designed by Richard Morris Hunt, but in the Beaux-Arts style. For an American house, it was unparalleled in opulence, with a temple-front portico resembling that of the White House, which had been completed 92 years earlier in 1800 (lofty, no?).

Mrs. Russell’s bedroom, built on a soundstage in Bethpage, New York, was inspired by real-life socialite Alva Vanderbilt’s bedroom in the Marble House. The French Louis XV bed was painted and reupholstered in salmon-pink velvet, and the custom bedding used 300 yards of fabric that have been draped, cinched, and swagged.

And that’s just the textiles. Most of the room’s furniture is refurbished antique and everything in the room has been painted to match. Sometimes, Shaw notes, it was difficult to paint a lovely wooden antique piece. “The scenic artists would look at us and say, ‘You really want me to paint over the chestnut? Are you sure?’” says Shaw. “Yes.”

Consuelo Vanderbilt’s (Alva and William’s child) monochrome red bedroom in the mansion also serves as George Russell’s bedroom in the show.

Features as: The exterior of Sylvia Chamberlain’s house

Rosecliff was built for Nevada silver heiress Theresa Fair Oelrichs, whose father, James Graham Fair, was one of the four partners in the Comstock Lode. The famed architectural firm McKim, Mead & White was commissioned to design the mansion, which was modeled after the Grand Trianon, the garden retreat of French kings at Versailles. The French Baroque Revival gem was designed for entertaining at a grand scale—and it certainly fulfilled its purpose. Fair Oelrichs would become known as one of the three great hostesses of Newport.

The white front facade of this mansion, which recently got a $7.4 million renovation, is a stand-in for Sylvia Chamberlain’s (played by Jeanne Tripplehorn) house. You’ll see it in Episode 7, when Tom Raikes (Thomas Cocquerel) pays a visit.

Features as: The servant kitchen and the real-life Sarah Berwind’s bedroom as parts of the Russell residence

The Elms is a Classical Revival dwelling that was completed in 1901. Architect Horace Trumbauer designed it for the coal baron Edward Julius Berwind, taking inspiration from the 18th-century d’Asnières French château. It also houses an impressive collection of paintings and tapestries, as well as the latest technology of its age.

In The Gilded Age Seasons 1 and 2, The Elms stands in as part of the Russells’ lavish residence, including scenes in the kitchen and Bertha Russell’s bedroom. But it truly steps into the spotlight in Season 3, when it hosts a dazzling ball. And as a long-awaited confession of love looms (we won’t spoil from whom), the opulent Newport mansion proves to be the perfect setting for high drama and high society.

Each guest is granted a cinematic entrance, illuminated by a golden glow that bathes the grand façade—a new addition this season, as electricity is now becoming available. “The house’s interiors would not have been electrified yet, but people were beginning to use it for parties,” Shaw says. “That would mean they would have to have their own generator installed somewhere on the property to light up those lights.” To accomplish this, the crew went to specialty shops that make reproduction bulbs. “The original bulbs were much bigger than what we have in a household today,” Shaw adds. “Some of the lightbulbs we used were 10 inches in diameter, which is how big they were then.”

Features as: The exterior is used for Caroline Astor’s Beechwood house; the boardinghouse is used for a scene with Oscar van Rhijn; and another room serves as Agnes van Rhijn’s bedroom

Completed in 1852, Château-sur-Mer is one of the first grand Bellevue Avenue mansions of the Gilded Age in Newport, built by architect Seth C. Bradford for William Shepard Wetmore. Its interiors were designed by Ogden Codman Jr., who co-authored Edith Wharton’s Decoration of Houses. The mansion was host to many major parties, including the Fête Champêtre, an elaborate country picnic for more than 2,000 guests in 1857, as well as a debutante ball for Edith Wetmore in 1889. Wetmore’s son, George Peabody Wetmore, went on to have a distinguished political career as the governor of Rhode Island and, later, as a U.S. senator.

In The Gilded Age, the home’s exterior was used for Caroline Astor’s (Donna Murphy) Beechwood House. The hallway, dining room, and ballroom were used during Mamie Fish’s (Ashlie Atkinson) doll tea party. Several bedrooms serve as Agnes van Rhijn’s (Christine Baranski) bedroom, Oscar van Rhijn’s (Blake Ritson) bedroom, Anne Morris’s (Katie Finneran) bedroom, and Patrick Morris’s (Michel Gill) office. There’s also a scene filmed at the boardinghouse with Oscar van Rhijn, according to Patch.

Features as: The office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes

Hunter House was constructed in 1748 at the harborfront near the north end of the Newport Historic District at a time when the city was a bustling seaport in the British empire. The house was owned by wealthy merchants and overlooked the wharves that serviced their trading ships. It’s one of the finest examples of Colonial architecture in Newport, according to the Preservation Society. Inside, its resident families furnished the house with decorative arts produced by expert craftsmen, much of which has been preserved today. Such a luxurious lifestyle, however, depended upon the labor of enslaved people of African descent; recent research by the Preservation Society has identified at least 13 enslaved individuals who lived and worked at Hunter House. Today’s Hunter House honors their perseverance and incalculable contributions to Newport’s past.

One of the sitting rooms in Hunter House functions as the office of Brook family lawyer Tom Raikes, and is set fictitiously in Doylestown, Pennsylvania.

Features as: The Astor family summer home

Belcourt was completed in 1894 for Oliver Hazard Perry Belmont, a prominent American socialite and coachman who served in the U.S. House of Representatives. Like many of the Newport mansions, the Château-esque structure was completed by architect Richard Morris Hunt; it was designed to mimic the hunting lodge of Versailles, built for Louis XIII of France, but with a special ask to accommodate Oliver’s collection of horses and automobiles. In 1896, Oliver gave the house to his second wife, the former Alva Vanderbilt, who designed the neighboring Newport mansion Marble House. Alva used Belcourt as a gathering place for the elite of society, hosting lavish parties and balls and attracting notable figures in politics, business, and the arts.

In The Gilded Age Season 1, Belcourt shows up in Episode 8 as Mrs. Astor’s lavish new Newport home, which Mrs. Russell sneaks inside and is forced to flee to avoid a less-than-pleasant meetup.

Features as: The social gathering scene where Caroline Astor and George Russell meet

Built in 1867 by prolific Boston tea and china merchant Robert M. Cushing, the Ledges was originally the summer home of the Cushing family. This majestic oceanfront pile is perched on one of Newport’s most desirable peninsulas, overlooking Bailey’s Beach. It was built by architect John H. Sturgis, who designed a large, Stick-style cottage that was equal parts comfort and elegance. Today it features original furnishings, papered walls, and unique artwork, including a studio that was added to the side of the house for Robert’s artistic children.

Today, the estate remains in the hands of the Cushing descendants, who occasionally open the house up to film crews. It was featured a few years ago in the film Evening, starring Glenn Close and Meryl Streep. “Working at the Ledges was pretty extraordinary,” Gilded Age location manager Lauri Pitkus told The Providence Journal, “because I don't think I’ve ever been in a home with spectacular views out of every window.”

This historic house was built in 1864 for John Noble Alsop Griswold, an Old China Trade merchant and diplomat. The house is the work of Richard Morris Hunt; it was one of the earliest buildings in the American Stick style of architecture. The house has a complex roofline with a main mansarded section that is pierced by numerous gable and dormer sections. It boasts a number of balconies sheltered by deep eaves, with gable ends decorated with woodwork. Today, the house is a gallery location for the Newport Art Museum. In the series, the house features in Season 1 Episode 1, and again in Episode 5.

Features as: Mingling with the other elite scene

The International Tennis Hall of Fame and its perfectly preserved grass courts are in the Newport Casino, a National Historic Landmark property. The historic seven-acre estate was commissioned by New York Herald publisher James Gordon Bennett as a social club for the elite summer residents of Newport. The Newport News boasted three days after it opened in July 1880: “It is doubtful if a livelier place can be found.” Today, the space is recognized as the official hall of fame for the sport of tennis globally. Since 1955, 262 distinguished inductees representing 27 countries have been enshrined in Newport.

In the eighth episode of The Gilded Age Season 1, the characters are seen promenading around the piazza that overlooks what is known today as Horseshoe Court. A few characters are even playing tennis. “It was fitting to have that history depicted here, given The Gilded Age’s look into that time period,” the hall of fame’s Megan Erbes tells ELLE DECOR. “Several of our staff members got to work closely with the production team and actors, teaching everything from the history of our property to how to hit a proper forehand.” RI Monthly reported that crews returned to the courts last summer to film Season 2 of the show, so it’s possible that Newport Casino will make another appearance.

Features as: The parlor, library, entryway, and staircase of the Morris house, located on Fifth Avenue in the show; Mr. and Mrs. Morris’s bedrooms were filmed in the Newport mansions

William Smith Slater hired General William Walker to build a home as a wedding gift for his daughter. The result was this historic home, which was completed in 1877. The Victorian house is filled with treasures—the fireplace surrounds, for example, were carved by Charles Dowler, who came to the United States to produce arms for the Civil War. Today, the house is open to visitors and is known as the Clouds Hill Museum.

The Clouds Hill Museum is the only Rhode Island site outside of Newport that was used in the series. The crew scheduled filming for Season 1 in late February of 2021, when a few hundred people were on site during two days of filming, museum director Wayne Cabral told Warwick Online, noting that the taping finished around midnight on the second day of filming (after five days of work to prep the set). Almost all the parlor’s furniture was removed, and the head set designer brought in items sourced from local antiques stores in East Greenwich and North Kingstown, museum president Anne Holst added. The producers were adamant about removing the existing curtains, which were disintegrating, Holst added, so she negotiated to leave the replacements (which cost $10,000) with the museum after filming.

Features as: Visit to the home of Aurora Fane scene

The Gothic Revival–style Lyndhurst Mansion was designed in 1838 by Alexander Jackson Davis. Lyndhurst’s third owner, Jay Gould, is the real-life inspiration for the “new money” character George Russell. “He was fantastically ruthless in business, to an extent that Mrs. Astor would never let him in, he was never allowed into society, and she didn’t let his children in until he was dead,” creator, writer, and producer Julian Fellowes said in a podcast. “But he was an incredibly affectionate father and a very caring and faithful husband…and his children all had these nostalgic memories of Lyndhurst, their house on the Hudson.”

In Season 1 of The Gilded Age, Lyndhurst’s interiors are the home of characters Charles and Aurora Fane (Ward Horton and Kelli O’Hara), who are related to the van Rhijns and part of the “old money” side of society. The Lyndhurst lawns and grounds also doubled as Central Park and the Sheep’s Meadow in the opening sequence. The home boasts a bowling alley that dates back to 1894, which was re-created to be the ferry terminal that Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) and Peggy Scott (Denée Benton) take to Manhattan for the first time in Episode 1. And finally, the house’s carriage house was transformed into The New York Globe newspaper offices.

The Belvedere Estate, located between Lyndhurst Mansion and writer Washington Irving’s Sunnyside home, is a century-old mansion on 25 acres in historic Tarrytown. The estate was originally part of Philipsburg Manor, a 90,000-acre property built for Florence and Casper Whitney. “While the mansion dates from the 1920s, it has a much older European feel that could represent something from decades earlier,” says Belvedere Estate sales director Linda Fiorentino, who recalls the massive legwork required to film at the mansion.

“I will never forget the background actors descending from a yellow school bus, fully dressed in period costumes and felt hats,” she says. The Season 1 scene was filmed at the pond at the back of Belvedere Estate, acting as a stand-in for Central Park in the scene when Marian meets Mr. Raikes at Bethesda Fountain in Episode 1. The pond is a man-made feature, and the water level sometimes sinks to a mere foot or two, Fiorentino explains. “Before filming, they filled the pond with roughly 600,000 gallons of water to fill to capacity, giving the illusion of a much larger body of water and to allow them to have pleasure boats filled with passengers on the water,” she says. They also re-created Central Park’s Shakespeare Bridge from rough-hewn logs and brought in trees, shrubs, and foliage to cover the existing link fence because it was too modern.

Features as: The drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain

Toward the end of the 19th century, Colonel Elliott Fitch Shepard, a lawyer who founded the New York State Bar Association in 1876, purchased more than 500 acres on a hill high above the Hudson River north of Tarrytown. He engaged the renowned architecture firm McKim, Mead & White to design his family’s manor house here. The result was a 75-room Victorian structure that mimicked 18th-century Italian villa architecture. Think: hand-carved mahogany paneling, a grand stairway near the front entrance, high patterned ceilings, and elaborate cornice work. Unfortunately, Shepard died before it was completed in 1893, and it passed into the hands of William Rockefeller and Frank Vanderlip. These men were part of a group (including John Jacob Astor, who died on the Titanic in 1912) who went on to convert the building to the Sleepy Hollow Country Club, which remains a private members-only club today.

Scenes in the drawing room of wealthy widow Sylvia Chamberlain were shot on location here. “The entire room was redressed by our team in the Rococo style,” set decorator Regina Graves told 1st Dibs.

Features as: Part of Mrs. Astor’s residence

Glenview was built in 1876 by John Bond Trevor, a highly successful stockbroker, who engaged New York City architect Charles W. Clinton to build his estate. The mansion was a paragon of luxury and modern comfort, featuring indoor plumbing, gas illumination, and a coal-burning furnace to circulate heat throughout its 37 rooms. The exterior had elements of Victorian Gothic and French Second Empire design, complete with a mansard roof, a gray stone facade constructed of blocks hewn from a Hastings-on-Hudson quarry, and decorative sandstone elements brought from Indiana.

The mansion makes an appearance as Caroline Astor’s residence in Season 1. “They were all so complimentary about Glenview, and of course we did a lot of polishing and waxing,” Laura Vookles, chair of the museum’s curatorial department, told The Rivertown Enterprise. “They focused on our exquisite interiors and filmed in six rooms, including the dining, library, and sitting rooms, and used all but one painting that is normally on view.”

The John Paine Mansion, also known as the Castle, was built in 1896 and was deemed the grandest house in Troy, New York. It has since served as a private residence, a college administration building, a movie set (see: Martin Scorsese’s 1993 drama The Age of Innocence), and, currently, a fraternity house. In the show, one of the bedrooms in the Castle features as Tom Raikes’s office.

This residence, the home of Troy locals Bill and Sue Comiskey, stands in for the Brooklyn residence of the well-off Scott family. At one point early in the series, Arthur Scott (John Douglas Thompson) and his daughter, Peggy (Denée Benton), sit on carved-oak chairs around a damask-covered dining table. The home’s elaborate details really shine in this historic home, which dates back to the turn of the 19th century. Stained-glass windows and an elegant china cabinet are memorable architectural features that are original to the location and transport the viewers back in time.

While filming what would be the Scott family residence, Bill, the homeowner, told News10 that a few prized family possessions actually made the cut. “My desk was in it, front and center…you [also] saw the whole front and back parlor into the dining room where they were having a meal,” he explained. “[There were] great shots of this room catching the stained glass, the fireplace, [and] this bookcase my grandfather made.”

Features as: The interior is used as George Russell’s workspace

Hempstead House was built for Howard Gould by architects Hunt & Hunt in 1912 on the north shore of Long Island. The mansion was eventually sold to art mogul Daniel Guggenheim (yes, that Guggenheim). Hempstead House is one of four mansions on the grounds of the Guggenheim Estate—which includes Castle Gould, Falaise, and Mille Fleur. The Tudor-style castle was the embodiment of Gold Coast opulence, a place where the esteemed family hosted grand parties, performances, and exhibitions by gatherings of the elite of the time. To say it is massive would be an understatement: 50,000 square feet, with 40 rooms and a 60-foot-tall entry foyer. In The Gilded Age, the mansion’s interior was used as George Russell’s workspace.

Features as: The late General Henry Brook’s house

Rock Hall Museum was initially an 18th-century plantation. The two-and-a-half-story Georgian-style home was built in approximately 1767 and later restored to its original appearance in the 1950s. In Season 1 Episode 1, the house’s white facade is featured within the first 15 minutes as a backdrop for the late General Henry Brook’s house. Marian, his daughter, is seen walking out of the home on her way to the train station.

The Hart Cluett Museum is a work of Federal architecture designed by Martin E. Thompson in 1827. He was hired by William Howard. Thompson brought the design of the Hart Cluett House back to New York City, where many examples of the 1827 Federal-style townhouse were copied throughout Manhattan. This house, a museum open to the public today, was featured in The Gilded Age in Episode 2 as Anne Morris’s home.

Rachel Silva is a freelance writer and editor covering design, architecture, and culture. She previously served as an editor at ELLE Decor, and her work has appeared in Harper’s Bazaar, TIME, and Veranda, among others. A graduate of Columbia Journalism School, she is based in New York City.

#6896. We Found the Ultimate Cold-Weather Cologne

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Fulton & Roark's new scent, inspired by a church in Gary, Indiana, is phenomenal.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

Halloween is in the rearview, Daylight Saving Time is over, and the sun has decided it has better places to be (although I am grateful for that extra hour of sleep we got). You know what all that means: It’s officially time to bring out the poofy coats, the cable-knit sweaters, and the winter scents. We’re talking spicy, smoky, sophisticated, and a little dark. Think of it as the olfactory equivalent of a cashmere scarf or quilted jacket. But before you reach for the same old colognes of winters past, consider making room for something new in your fragrance rotation.

Allow me to suggest my new cold-weather go-to, released just last month by one of my all-time favorite niche fragrance houses, Fulton & Roark. This extrait has a pretty sick name: Cathedral of Ruins—and it smells every bit as atmospheric as you’d hope for with a name like that. It’s moody without being gloomy, warm without being cloying, and oh-so intriguing. Put a spritz of this stuff on and you’re guaranteed to start hearing the words, What are you wearing?

Fulton & Roark does what it calls “American fine fragrance,” meaning it doesn’t follow all the rules of your classic European perfumery. The perfumer looks to new ingredients, formulation methods, and scent combinations for all of its fragrances. Oh, and every fragrance is unisex, truly unisex. My boyfriend loves the scents as much as I do, and we often share them.

And since this is American fine fragrance we’re talking about, it only makes sense that each of the brand’s fragrances are named after and inspired by a real American place with a story behind it. That rule even holds for the brand’s name: Fulton is for Fulton County, Georgia; and Roark is for Roark’s Cove in Sewanee, Tennessee. So where is this Cathedral of Ruins? Gary, Indiana. Birthplace of the Jackson 5, and our clearest example of Rust Belt deindustrialization. The once-booming steel city is now also home to thousands of abandoned and decaying buildings, including the City Methodist Church—once one of the grandest churches in the Midwest.

Fulton and Roark co-founder Kevin Keller showed me photos of what City Methodist Church looks like now, which he took on his own trip to Gary, and the immediate sense I got from these photos was a devastating sort of beauty (or a beautiful sort of devastation). The church’s roof has fallen in; the only living congregation is the vegetation that reclaims more and more of the space every year. But, its Neo-Gothic stone walls still stand. It’s a dead relic, but it is not a dead space. It pulls up mixed feelings. This Cathedral of Ruins extrait de parfum is Fulton & Roark’s homage to it, and it nails everything you get from seeing a picture of the place.

Like I said, Cathedral of Ruins is the perfect cold-weather scent. But instead of the typical rich, gourmand notes you get in fall and winter fragrances like vanilla, caramel, and amber, Cathedral of Ruins relies on spices, smoke, and woods to create a warm atmosphere. This is not a syrupy sweet winter scent—it’s smoldering, Gothic-inspired, sophisticated, and unlike any scent you’ve ever smelled before.

At the top, you get resinous cypress, spicy black peppercorn, and, unexpectedly, floral rose. The lush, velvety notes of rose lead into the more delicate floral notes of saffron, symbolizing the grace of the cathedral (and possibly the new natural growth of vegetation over the ruins). Rich Peru balsam finishes out the heart with more warm, woody vibes.

But the base is where this fragrance really stands out. The star of the show here is burned frankincense, the classic scent of sacred ritual—imagine smoke rising above the walls, past the ceiling that’s no longer there. Frankincense is, obviously, smoky, but not in the same way as something like tobacco; the frankincense here is dry, resinous, and subtly fresh like pine. To me, frankincense even has a waxy-green quality similar to olives. The warm depth of leathered woods and musks finish out the fragrance, leaving the intrigue of smoky woods to linger on your skin.

#6893. 13 Things You Should Toss ASAP If You’ve Lived in Your Home for More Than 5 Years

You'll feel a wave of relief once you do it.

It may seem like you just moved into your new home or apartment yesterday. But in a flash, five years have passed and you’re out of storage and feeling overwhelmed. Most of us know it doesn’t take long for our belongings to pile up.

“By year five, the ‘endless storage’ honeymoon is over,” says Ann Lightfoot, cofounder, DDH Home Organizing & Move Management. “Your stuff has multiplied, your square footage hasn’t, and you now know how you actually live in your space.”

But now is the ideal time for a thoughtful edit. “Taking stock after five years gives us a chance to see what's actually taking up space, what we're still using, and what we've simply gotten used to storing,” says Laura Kinsella, founder of Urban OrgaNYze. “Our careers, families, routines, and interests can change dramatically, but the things we've accumulated don't automatically change with us.”

Decluttering also enhances good design. “To me, a beautifully designed home isn't about how much you can fit into it. It's about being intentional with what you choose to keep,” says Kerrie Kelly, NKBA, FASID, CEO and creative director of Kerrie Kelly Studio, and NKBA 2026 National Board Chair. “Editing isn't about deprivation; it's about making room for what you actually love and use.”

If you don’t know where to begin, start small. “Edit by category rather than trying to tackle the entire house in a weekend,” says Kelly. “Start with one drawer, one cabinet, or one closet. Take everything out, clean the space, and put back only what deserves to be there. It's amazing how quickly those small decisions transform the way a home feels. Empty space is a luxury, too!”

Ahead, here’s what design and organizational experts say you should get rid of if you’ve been in your home five years or more.

“Five years of conferences, events, fundraisers, and corporate gifting can leave you with an impressive collection of tote bags, reusable water bottles, and branded merchandise you never actually set out to own,” says Kinsella. Keep what you genuinely like and use, and pass along the extras.

Most manuals live online now, and that tangle of chargers likely powers nothing in your house. Recycle them responsibly, says Lightfoot.

“Threadbare towels, mismatched sheets, and extra bedding you never reach for take up valuable storage space,” says Kelly. “Keep a thoughtfully curated collection of beautiful, functional linens and recycle the worn-out ones where possible.”

“If your spin shoes haven't been touched since lockdown and the exercise bike has become ‘the chair’ in your bedroom, you may be dedicating valuable space to a workout routine that's no longer part of your life,” says Kinsella. Sell or donate equipment you no longer use so you have room for the activities you truly enjoy now.

Craft kits, furniture you planned to refinish, the bread maker and so on: “If someday hasn’t arrived in five years, give yourself permission to let it go, guilt-free,” says Lightfoot.

“Those little objects that have been sitting on a shelf for years can become visual clutter,” says Kelly. “I love a meaningful collection, but every piece should earn its place. Sometimes removing three things makes the one beautiful object remaining look even better.”

“That rug or wall art you swore would work ‘somewhere’ has spent five years leaning against a wall. It’s time to release it to a home where it fits,” says Lightfoot.

Hang on to a small labeled paint sample for touch-ups—and maybe one tile. But the rest, including the dried-up products or the five half-empty cans in the garage or basement, can go to your town’s hazardous waste drop-off, says Lightfoot.

This is a no-judgment zone: “If the avocado slicer, specialty pan, or oversized appliance hasn't made an appearance in years, it probably doesn't deserve prime real estate in your kitchen,” says Kelly. Ditto for duplicate kitchen tools.

“Whether it's a juicer, red light mask, or massage tool, we've all had that new wellness routine we're completely committed to for a few weeks before life gets busy and the habit quietly falls away,” says Kinsella. “The problem is that the gadget doesn't disappear when the enthusiasm does, and five years later, you may have an entire cabinet devoted to routines that lasted a month.” It’s fine to try something new to learn it wasn't for you, but you don't have to keep storing the equipment forever.

Maybe it's an extra accent chair that collects clothes or a side table that's always in the way. Just because something fits physically doesn't mean it belongs. Give your rooms space to breathe and your furniture room to shine, says Kelly.

Purge the old sunscreen, crusty nail polish, expired makeup, shampoo you don’t like, and half-used cleaners that have piled up in your bathroom. Toss them safely and enjoy the instant breathing room, says Lightfoot.

“If you've lived in your home for five years and still have boxes you never unpacked, it's a pretty good indication that you haven't needed most of what's inside on a regular basis,” says Kinsella. “Open the box, decide what's worth keeping, and find those belongings a proper home so you're not continuing to live around a move that’s already happened.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6888. The Democratic Party Has Rediscovered the Power of Christianity. The GOP Should Be Worried.

James Talarico of Texas and Adam Hamilton of Kansas have proven that faith isn’t just a Republican value.

Listen to James Talarico, celebrating his massive victory over Ken Paxton, who must have a really ugly portrait of himself up in the attic.

On Tuesday night, the voters elected two Democratic preachers to Senate seats that once seemed vastly out of reach: Talarico and Adam Hamilton in Kansas. They will be joining Senator Raphael Warnock, the pastor at Martin Luther King Jr.’s church in Atlanta, in the upper chamber of the national legislature. It is possible that, after decades in the wilderness, the Democratic party may be rediscovering the true political power of the actual gospel, not the gospel preached by the megachurch charlatans making culture-war hay out of things Jesus never actually said or believed. When Talarico said that God was “nonbinary” and the performance Christians of the Right jumped all over him, he was right and they were wrong. For those who believe in him, God is pure spirit, beyond all gender. So, technically, God is nonbinary. But, anyway, Jesus never said fck all about transgender people.

For his part, Adam Hamilton is the founder of a huge Methodist church in the Kansas City area. He has given sermons in which he asked his congregation to repent of eating beef and driving cars as part of a sermon on climate change. The Republicans attempted to freakify him with these ideas, but Hamilton’s fundamental decency, and his sincere belief in “stewardship” of the planet, which used to be the Republican dodge on environmental issues, were able to overcome the usual campaign slanging. His acceptance speech called for a gentler, more charitable politics. Again, a more Christian Christianity than the one purportedly practiced by the ratfcking Pharisees of the American Right.

This is a very interesting development if it survives this election cycle and if it isn’t dependent on uniquely charismatic candidates like Talarico. If the Democrats can reckon with a lost source of political power, then that cannot be a bad development.
