#6883. I Went to the World's Biggest Furniture Trade Show—These 6 Trends Are About to Be Everywhere

The furniture profiles, patterns, and palettes you’re about to see everywhere.

Twice a year, High Point, North Carolina, becomes the home industry’s giant showroom, where furniture and design brands unveil new collections for retailers, interior designers, and buyers to see and source. At House Beautiful, we’re your boots on the ground, looking for the ideas that may travel from showroom to store—and into your home next.

This fall, Market made a case for decorating with more character and muddier hues: green moved from the forest floor to the showroom floor, florals claimed entire sofas, and upholstery played with everything from a flash of leg to a full fabric wrap. Add wood-backed furniture as an accent, garden-inspired lighting, and a generous swish of fringe, and the message was clear: The details are ready to take center stage.

This year, green was the unofficial color of Market. Benjamin Moore and Behr had already put the hue on the radar with their 2027 Colors of the Year: Webster Green, a confident mid-tone balancing blue’s coolness with yellow’s warmth, and Grounded, a mossy olive softened by earthy brown undertones. Then came the showroom echoes. Versions of green, from earthy vegetable shades to deeper, moodier tones, appeared nearly everywhere. Kathy Kuo even created a “Music Room” (shown in our opening image) for Rowe Furniture inspired by the verdant shade. The appeal is easy to understand: Green brings the natural world indoors, lending a sense of calm and grounding a space without draining it of personality.

The design industry’s obsession with prints is now a love letter to botanicals. Florals erupted across Market in a myriad of scales, from small, almost sun-bleached botanicals to overscaled, tapestry-adjacent blooms. Even the tiniest prints made a surprisingly bold statement when carried across an entire sofa; the larger blooms brought the feel of a faded heirloom into full view. Both have that grandmotherly, another-era quality, but the impact is anything but timid. Yes, it’s a major design commitment, but why play it safe with a solid, barely-there pattern? It may not be for the faint of design, but we are all in a sofa fully dressed in flowers.

Here’s a furniture detail that changes the whole silhouette: Brands are letting wood take over the outside back—or sides—of upholstered seating. Baker’s sofa makes the move hard to miss, with a broad wood shell framing the piece. It’s a little like “inside-out” upholstery, where the outside gets its own treatment, but here the wood gives the piece architectural shape and presence. As floor plans open up and sofas float in family and living rooms, the back is no longer hidden against a wall. It’s part of the view, and part of how the seating area takes shape, making it all the more important that it feels worth looking at.

Upholstery continues to take its cues from fashion, with a focus on mixing silhouettes: Mini skirts that let those gams show; full, ballooning skirts with all the best parts of an ’80s prom dress; and what we lovingly call body-con upholstery, where the entire chair is wrapped head to toe in one fabric.

The appeal goes beyond a good fashion metaphor, though. Each treatment changes the chair’s proportions: a flash of leg keeps it light, a generous skirt adds volume, and an all-over wrap turns both seat and base into one continuous form, with no leg finish to choose or worry about. Each has its own distinct style POV, making your shopping choices that much more complicated, but in the best way.

If garden motifs aren’t your thing, don’t look up. The floral revival isn’t limited to upholstery and wallpaper; it’s reaching the ceiling, too. Visual Comfort & Co.’s Fairoaks collection, designed by Fisher Weisman, includes a chandelier, sconce, and mini flush mount. Sculptural branches and twigs cleverly disguise the chandelier’s ceiling cord, preserving the romance of the design. At Hudson Valley Lighting, a tulip-inspired chandelier brings a more literal bloom overhead. Both offer a way to bring the garden indoors through shape and silhouette, without committing an entire room to pattern.

Sometimes, all it takes is a little bullion fringe. The thick, tightly twisted cords fall in a dense, weighty row, adding plush texture and a satisfying swish to a skirted chair or sofa. It nods to the Victorian parlor, but stops short of period-drama excess; against a cleaner silhouette or in a spare room, the old-world trim feels playful and unexpectedly fresh. Consider it the design equivalent of a great pair of earrings: a little extra, in exactly the right place.

#6879. The Chicest Fall and Winter Coat Trends to Inspire Your Cold-Weather Wardrobe

Fall is the official start of coat season, and honestly, it might just be the best time of year to have fun with your wardrobe. The right coat can transform even the simplest outfit into a seriously stylish moment, making it the ultimate investment piece for your fall rotation. Whether you're dressing for a coffee run, a weekend brunch, or a night out, there's a perfect outerwear look waiting to inspire you.

The best part about a good coat is that you don't need an entirely new wardrobe to still bring in the compliments. Just find a coat you love, build your outfit around it, and let your outerwear do all the talking. Consider this your sign to make getting dressed the most exciting part of your day. If you need some inspiration, I’ve got you covered with some different styles you can tap into.

Cropped trench coats are the ultimate fall layering hack. Throw one over a fitted knit and wide-leg jeans for an effortlessly chic, waist-defining look. With all the timeless polish of a classic trench but a fresh, fashion-forward twist, this playful silhouette is the perfect way to give your fall wardrobe a little main-character energy.

A patterned coat is the easiest way to make a statement this fall. Style it over a simple monochromatic outfit to let the print do all the talking. From bold checks to playful plaids, this cozy layer brings personality, texture, and a little unexpected fun to even the most everyday look.

Make a bold entrance this fall in a colorful faux fur coat, pairing it with sleek denim, a simple sweater, and boots to let the statement-making texture shine. It's cozy, a little bit glam, and guaranteed to brighten up even the gloomiest fall day. Especially something like this burgundy coat, a popular color that’s been trending for a while.

Cinched-waist chic is having a moment, and a belted coat is the perfect way to define your silhouette while keeping your fall layers looking polished. It’s also my preferred everyday coat for the cooler days, keeping you fully wrapped and warm.

A good puffer jacket will take you far. Pair it with a miniskirt and chunky boots, or dress it down with a baseball cap and some jeans. Doesn’t matter the styling, it’s a great everyday coat to have on deck.

#6878. 6 Antique Trends Designers Are Watching for 2027

Above: The kitchen in designer Phillip Thomas’s Bellport retreat.

Do the words “antique” and “trend” really belong in the same sentence? In many ways, a great antique shop feels like the antidote to trend fatigue: a space where the true must-haves are hidden in dusty corners, with part of the fun in the thrill of the hunt. Yet, as we round out 2026, there’s no denying the current design cycle is casting off perfection for patina. Antiques are en vogue, and top designers are keeping a close eye on the periods, pieces, and yes, trends, that are poised to find new life in 2027.

“The return of interest in antiques reflects a trend towards more personal, unique interiors, and away from strict modern rooms which can often be cool and generic,” says ELLE Decor A-List designer Jamie Drake. This shift paves the way for more playful interactions between pieces, unexpected layering, and unabashed personality.

Other experts second the motion for more character. “I’m seeing clients really drawn to antiques with more to them than meets the eye: pieces that have a bit of humor or whimsy, or pieces with great provenance,” says ELLE Decor Rising Star William Cullum. (He lists a “ridiculously giant butterfly light fixture” from New Orleans, rattan chairs from Bing Crosby’s estate, and a disco ball from Cyndi Lauper’s Bonham’s sale in his list of recent acquisitions—professional and personal.)

Luckily for the amateur antiquer, not all trending vintage styles require a substantial space commitment or glamorous provenance—although an interesting backstory helps. “Antiques are always about a story: where you found them, who owned them, and how that story will continue on after us,” Cullum says.

Ahead, we asked top designers and experts to share the most inspiring antique trends of the moment. From neoclassic mirrors to hand-painted chests paired with modern art, their takes are sure to inform your next estate sale rummage, or inspire you to restyle an old heirloom.

We called mirrored surfaces a major trend to watch for fall 2026, and antique trends have followed suit. Unexpected (or better yet, artfully manipulated) reflections can add rich dimension and movement to any space, but in this category, antiques create a window to the past that’s tough to beat.

“One of the easiest ways to add a sense of history to a room is to include an antique mirror,” says Drake. “Whether you choose a sober and strict neo-classic piece or a more flamboyant baroque one, reflections of the past add depth and sparkle.”

“One trend I’m observing is an uptick in antique dowry chests,” antiques dealer Ali Mahon says. From Pennsylvania Dutch to Swedish Folk and Spanish carved, she’s noticed clients gravitating toward more delicately adorned storage over spare. With thoughtfully mixed furniture (from style to period) becoming more desirable than matching sets, Mahon sees more hand-painted antique surfaces in our future: “With their decorative tulips, leaves, birds, and hearts, dowry chests create a colorful splash in any room.”

ELLE Decor Rising Star Reagan Gail Struble agrees—although delicate designs are not a requirement of the trend, in her book: “Something about a playful wash of color on an awesome old piece of furniture is very alluring, especially when you can see the age and texture of the wood peeking through,” she says. “Hand-painted case pieces add a little punch of charm to any room.”

Luckily, these antiques can be just as functional as they are fashionable. Mahon recommends using a painted chest as a coffee table, at the foot of the bed, or in a space that could benefit from a bit more romance (and a few extra drawers). When styling your find in a modern space, Struble says that contrast is key: try a “chippy Swedish chest of drawers” with an unexpected photograph or painting above it.

According to ELLE Decor A-List designer Nick Olsen, Art Deco pieces are setting the tone for 2027 antique trends—although, if you ask him, they were never out. “I'm particularly inspired by the [art deco influence in the] Gujarat palace in India,” he says. “I'm fascinated by how every element seems to have a wide border: marble floors, carpets, walls of veneer and mohair velvet upholstery.”

According to Olsen, using Art Deco design compositions can do as much for a room as actual pieces from the period. “I like the idea of contrasting borders: solid textures punctuated by smaller graphic prints on furniture and more delicate lighting,” he explains. And while borders are easy to create with paint, paneling, and carpet seaming, Olsen challenges clients to push the Art Deco agenda further by bordering with luxe materials.

Whether you’re new to antiquing or simply trying to add more character to a space, lighting is an excellent place to start. “Lighting is the jewelry of the home, so it’s one of my favorite places to bring in something vintage,” says A-Lister Corey Damen Jenkins. “An Art Deco sconce or an antique brass lantern has a warmth to it that’s hard to find in something brand new.”

Adding to an existing space? Jenkins advises starting small: “Swap one overhead fixture or put a pair of vintage lamps on a console, and you’ll be surprised how much the whole room shifts,” he says. “It’s an easy way to add history without starting over.”

“In light of America’s 250th birthday, Americana in general is in vogue,” says Mahon. Among her clients, the trend has manifested in quilts and textiles, portraits of sea captains, trade signs, and ironstone—culminating in an aesthetic she describes as “elevated country—not corny.”

If you’ve noticed that no particular design period seems to reign supreme among designers’ top antique trend picks, it’s not an accident. Heading into 2027, there’s a strong argument to be made that mixed-period design is more influential than any singular reference.

“I love a room that doesn’t belong to any one decade,” Jenkins says. “A nineteenth-century chest with a 1970s lamp on top, or a Lucite chair pulled up to an antique desk. Those are the pairings that make people stop and look twice.” Drake adds that seemingly out-of-place seating is an easy way to add intrigue. “I love including antique chairs as sculptural notes, contrasted against modern upholstery and tables,” he says.

But when it comes to antiquing, there’s really only so much one can plan for. Letting a great find lead the way often turns out to be a better strategy than arriving to an estate sale with a rigid checklist. The best way to keep up with top designers when it comes to antiquing this year? Find a piece that has something to say, place it where it seems to belong, and then try it where it doesn’t.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

#6876. Hublot Just Relaunched Its First Watch. Founder Carlo Crocco Explains How It Changed Everything.

In Milan, celebrating the release of the Classic Fusion Tributo, I got to catch up with the guy who altered the aesthetics of modern watchmaking.

If you spend a lot of time following the world of watches, Hublot’s origin story might feel as familiar as one of the fairytales you heard at bedtime as a child. If you don’t, though, it might feel just as fantastical as some yarn about a princess who feels a pea under a stack of mattresses or a prince who slays a fire-breathing dragon.

You start with an unlikely hero. This time around, it’s an Italian entrepreneur with a big, bold, brazen idea. He wants to make a watch, something unlike anyone has ever seen. It’ll be gold, with an angular case shaped like the porthole on a ship. Not so crazy yet. But wait. Because, breaking entirely with the conventions of the day, the strap won’t be made of exotic leather or precious metal but rubber. Humble, industrial rubber.

This is exactly what Hublot founder Carlo Crocco envisioned nearly half a century ago, and what he brought to the Basel Watch Fair—pretty much the place to be if you wanted to be a part of the business of timekeeping—in 1980. He spent three years developing the idea, all without doing a bit of market research. He just believed in his vision. And then the fair began.

“I was very anxious, because for the first two days, no one came into our booth,” he says. But the anxiety didn’t have long to fester. Word was spreading. And then? People galore. “On the third day, we realized that everyone was speaking about us because it was really something different from all the other watches.”

That first watch—36mm in diameter, with a quartz movement and a black rubber strap—quickly became a hit. Industry buzz gave way to the kind of clientele most brands can only imagine. The King of Greece bought one in Gstaad for himself and another for his brother-in-law, the King of Spain. More royals followed. (“By the way, they’re all related,” Crocco quips.)

But the biggest thing wasn’t getting insiders to a booth at a trade fair or convincing kings to strap Hublot watches—named, if you’re curious, for the French word for porthole—on their wrists. It was changing how the industry thought about the way watches are made.

“At that time, the mentality of watchmaking was very classical,” Crocco says. “They couldn’t think, normal people, of putting together rubber and gold. I personally thought it was a really good idea because it was against the current. It was very risky, but if you don’t risk, you have no result.”

The result was a watch industry that completely recalibrated itself. Crocco says that within four or five years of the original Hublot’s release, the brand was already dealing with imitators. Now, the idea of combining disparate materials is firmly embedded in the minds of both consumers and designers. You can see it on watches from brands that sell at the local department store and brands that only sell to folks who haven’t seen the inside of one of those since the first time their private jet took off. Crocco’s big idea changed the whole game.

Which means it’s a pretty good time to go back to the beginning of this thing. Last month in Milan, Hublot debuted a new take on that first, groundbreaking watch. Dubbed the Hublot Classic Fusion Tributo, it’s an homage but not a nuts-and-bolts recreation. Instead of the original 36mm case, it’s been upsized to a more 2026-appropriate 38mm. And instead of a quartz movement—Crocco thought it was “more practical” at the time but has come around to the enduring appeal of mechanical construction—a self-winding HUB1711 movement with a 50-hour power reserve keeps things ticking along. Oh, and now you can get it in white gold, too.

Then there’s that rubber strap. When Crocco designed the original Hublot, he was insistent that the strap was entirely straight; he felt it was crucial to the aesthetic proposition. On the Tributo, it’s ever-so-slightly, almost imperceptibly tapered, a necessary concession to keep the proportions intact after increasing the case size (which, handily, lets that automatic movement sit comfortably inside).

But, as with the first Hublot, it’s not all about tech specs and watch-nerd particulars. It’s about the spirit of the thing. And the spirit of this watch stays true to that pioneering design. There are twelve screws around the bezel, so you can keep an eye on the hour despite the dial’s lack of indices—something Crocco says was a non-negotiable when he started consulting on the new release. The yellow gold version keeps the abyss-like black lacquer dial and the white gold throws us a tiny (and welcome) curveball by using onyx. The combo of precious metal and rubber still hits, 46 years later.

If you prod him how he feels about the way that he shifted the watch industry, Crocco won’t brag. He left Hublot nearly 20 years ago, when it sold to LVMH. He knows his idea changed things; he’s happy about it. But this isn’t his day job anymore. Still, when he’s asked if he could be considered the godfather of fusing high and low in watchmaking—and you give him a minute to ponder it—he’ll tell you the truth.

#6870. The Remarkable Election-Night Story We Should All Be Talking About

Don’t let the sound of the blue tsunami drown out what happened in Wisconsin.

Drowned out by the sound of the blue tsunami making landfall everywhere, a remarkable story took place in Wisconsin. The state’s Democratic party, almost gerrymandered out of existence a few years ago and helpless, succeeded in electing not only a governor but also majorities in both houses of the state legislature.

The Democrats already enjoy a majority on the state supreme court, so I feel confident in saying that the state is now completely free of the dead hand of the administration of Scott Walker, the goggle-eyed homunculus hired by Koch Industries to manage their subsidiary once known as the state of Wisconsin. They also flipped two congressional seats: Paul Ryan’s old seat, now held by one of his old aides, and that of the inexcusable Derrick Van Orden, famous corrector of lounging congressional pages.

(What is truly delicious is that Van Orden was dimed out on that rampage by his fellow Wisconsin congressman Mark Pocan, who was reelected Tuesday night, though he ran unopposed.)

(It also served as Walker’s jumping-off point.) His pathway to the governor’s office was, shall we say, eccentric. From the Milwaukee Journal Sentinel:

But his campaign for governor was something of a roller coaster.

(Hong, it should be noted, turned out to be the anti–Haley Stevens, campaigning vigorously for Crowley throughout the general election campaign. Good on her.)

It was political activist Ben Wikler who turned the shebeen on to David Crowley, and Wikler is going to go down in Wisconsin political history as the guy who rebuilt the state’s Democratic party almost from scratch. And he did so in keeping with the state’s proud progressive history and with the state’s one-word motto: Forward.

#6867. Presidents, Bullfighters, and California—Elaine de Kooning Painted Whatever She Wanted

And you can see it all at a new retrospective at Berry Campbell gallery in New York

In the history books, Elaine de Kooning is often eclipsed by her husband, the famed painter Willem. This fate befell the women in the Abstract Expressionist movement, including Lee Krasner, Grace Hartigan, Joan Mitchell, and Helen Frankenthaler, who became known as the “Ninth Street Women,” all of whom received their flowers after decades of being overshadowed by their male counterparts. But you simply cannot tell the story of mid-century art—and Abstract Expressionism—without Elaine.

De Kooning, who died in 1989 at 70, sat at the epicenter of the art world for five decades. She deftly balanced two parallel practices in portraiture and abstract expressionism, unafraid to allow these genres to intermingle. Beyond her painting, she founded the artist collective The Club in Greenwich Village and wrote art criticism for Art News, celebrating artists like Arshile Gorky and Jackson Pollock. President John F. Kennedy, New York artist Toni Ross, former Art News editor-in-chief Thomas Hess, and American painter Alex Katz are among the artist’s impressive roster of sitters. A number of these portraits currently hang at New York’s Berry Campbell gallery. Each image frames its subject with expressive brushwork and vivid, bright colors. Among these works, most featuring starry figures, is a young boy in a cowboy hat, featured in the painting Jemp #1 (1963). This little kid is De Kooning’s nephew, and the reason the show exists.

Gallerists Martha Campbell and Christine Berry connected with Chris Luyckx, the nephew, who had preserved De Kooning’s paintings for decades and felt her work deserved renewed attention. By the time they met, roughly a decade had passed since De Kooning’s last notable show, a retrospective at the National Portrait Gallery in 2015, followed by smaller shows that focused on specific periods of her life. “By doing these individual shows, you miss the incredible throughline of her work from the late 1940s until the day she died in 1989,” says Campbell. “It goes with our ethos of always believing in the artist from the very beginning until the very end.” Berry Campbell’s show, simply titled “Elaine De Kooning” and on view through December 19, celebrates the artist in her entirety, featuring around 30 works sourced directly from the family’s collection

De Kooning’s oeuvre retains its wonder by evading classification, collapsing boundaries between portraiture and abstract expressionism to forge a distinct visual sensibility. Part of her practice fit firmly in the world of abstract art. Paintings like Untitled (1960) reveled in obscuring the world around her; here, she depicts New York’s verticality. “Elaine de Kooning was an abstract expressionist, but as a woman artist, she didn’t have dealers breathing down her neck saying, ‘Paint more of what we see as Abstract Expressionism,” Campbell says, noting that this provided the artist with wiggle room.

This should not overshadow her exceptional command of pure abstraction. The 11-foot-wide California (1961), a barrage of vivid colors underscoring De Kooning’s compositional mastery, sits at the very back of the gallery. Berry affirms “it is the most important piece in the exhibition,” noting that “there is no other painting of that scale or magnitude or from that time period on the market, because very other painting that is like that is in a museum collection.” Doubling down on its consequence, Campbell recalls, “There’s a great note that we found from [legendary art dealer] Joan Washburn that says that when she was her dealer, she said, ‘This is the best painting that Elaine de Kooning has ever painted.’”

Elements of her abstract work often defined her portraiture. Energetic bursts of bright paint consumed her subjects. Tom Hess (1963), for instance, features the titular journalist among flurries of orange, yellow, and red paint, leaving most of the canvas untouched. “She was an abstract expressionist, but within her own definition of what that was, so she used these abstract expressionist brushstrokes to glorify what she loved.”

De Kooning loved her subjects, occasionally obsessing over one topic and painting it repeatedly, ranging from bullfighting and baseball to the painter Aristodimos Kaldis and President Kennedy. This new show highlights all of these hyperfocused periods, including a standout from her paintings of the former president. Her connection to him started in 1962, when Kennedy sat for De Kooning in Palm Beach to complete a commission for the Harry S. Truman Presidential Library & Museum. The fixation intensified after the president’s assassination in 1963. JFK #7 (1963), on view at the gallery, is only one example of a year’s worth of works.

“She did get fixated on JFK, because she was painting him and then he was shot,” Berry explains. “That was pretty shocking. She painted him for a full year without stopping. People only focus on JFK. But she did portraits from the beginning, in the 1940s, all the way through. She did self-portraits, as well as portraits of her neighbors and her boss, Tom Hess. The portraits are a through line of her career.”

A sophisticated command of color is evident across De Kooning’s entire body of work. Bright colors clash in her abstract works, and in her portraiture, color builds texture. Perhaps most surprising, however, is that De Kooning often avoided looking at color when creating her images. For instance, she preferred to use black-and-white newspaper clippings as references for her basketball paintings, imbuing them with color guided by her personal sensibility. “Color is key to what she does,” Berry explains, pointing to the portrait of poet Frank Lima, a friend with a drug problem whom De Kooning renders in muted greens.

One painting, Bacchus #20-G (1983), an image of Jules Dalou’s The Triumph of Silenus painted six years before her death, epitomizes what’s so striking about De Kooning’s paintings. The lush painting features the sculpture’s twisted bodies, rendered in an opulent violet, with a backdrop of verdant green. It demonstrates her capacity to seamlessly balance portraiture and Abstract Expressionism, a testament to her unwavering devotion to her distinct visual language. This vision, after years on the fringe, finally receives the flower it deserves, alongside, rather than below, her husband.

#6866. The 45 Best Movies to Watch With Your Parents Without Making It Weird

The family room has been declared an Awkward-Free Zone.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Home for the holidays? Living with your parents again? Welcome to the club... and not the fun kind of club that couldn't even handle you right now, once upon a time.

Let's say you and your roommates (aka mom and dad, maybe grandma and grandpa too) are looking for a quality movie to watch together that won't be filled with awkward scenes or jokes that only you laugh at while the dog side-eyes you from the carpet. It really puts a damper on your weekly Netflix scroll if you aren't careful. Nothing too artsy. Nothing you’ll have to explain. You want something adult, but not that kind of adult.

There's always a risk that family movie night implodes and ends with someone storming out of the room. Committing to the wrong movie is like opting to wear that cute pair of heels that always make your feet hurt even before you've left the Uber―painful and totally avoidable.

From action to comedy to classics, these movies will make your next family movie night more entertaining, less stressful, and an all-around good time. Just because a movie is the dreaded "family-friendly" doesn't mean it has to be boring or, like, for literal children. Swap your stilettos for your slippers, snuggle up on the couch, and start streaming.

You don't need to be a race-car fan (or know much about it in general) to enjoy this 2019 Best Picture nomination. An exhilarating film from both a storyline and videography standpoint, it stars your faves Christian Bale and Matt Damon. Lots of fast cars, personal demons, and some bromance.

This semi-autobiographical rock musical was written by Jonathan Larson before he found success with Rent and, subsequently, died far too young from an aortic dissection. But despite the real life tragedy, the movie is far from a bummer. Directed by Lin-Manuel Miranda and starring Andrew Garfield, it is an absolute joy and can offer your Sound of Music-loving family members a new musical to obsess over.

Don't listen to your older siblings: you're never, I mean never, too old for an animated Disney movie. Moana is something special; it’s a delightful story about embracing your identity and realizing your potential. And the music.

Isn’t it kind of crazy that this is rated PG-13? When a sequel not only lives up to, but also―dare I say―surpasses the first film, it's noteworthy. Meet the Fockers is one of the greatest sequels of all time. You also kind of NEED to watch before the next one, starring Ariana Grande, hits theaters.

Sure, you watched Ocean's Eleven back when it first came out in 2001, or caught the last 30 minutes on a hotel television once or twice. But when's the last time you re-watched it from start to finish? The legendary film about a casino heist is quick, witty, and action-packed. If the glossy crimes don't give you a rush of exhilaration and thrill, then Brad Pitt and George Clooney's dreamy faces sure will.

There's a lot to love about this movie and Ryan Gosling's abs are just six one of 'em. A quick warning, though. If you'd rather avoid a-l-l sex scenes when watching with your parentos, there are a few moments of Emma Stone and Ryan Gosling smooching in bed. And some of the power dynamics are questionable at best.

Next time you're stuck in Indecision Land, propose a fam trip to Middle-earth. Relatives of all ages will get lost in the magical world of Frodo, Gandalf, Gollum, and the whole gang. Even better, suggest a whole Lord of the Rings trilogy marathon―you’ll be set for the entire weekend.

The Family Stone often gets overlooked but it's a very touching story about acceptance and love. Especially good to watch around the holidays, it'll remind your blood relatives that every family has their own chaotic energy, not just yours. Also, not sure if you’ve heard, but there’s a sequel on the way!

Some days just call for a stress-free, aesthetically-pleasing, feel-good movie. Even better if it's about―you guessed it―food. Jon Favreau whips up a delicious film about the restaurant industry and family, but do not put this on the TV on an empty stomach. Actually, it'd be a fun one to make into a TV dinner situation.

You say "I want a heart-racing, invigorating movie." I say "Miracle." Hockey’s gotten randomly popular lately, so why not? It's about the 1980 U.S. men's Olympic hockey team and how they became a microcosm for American patriotism during the Cold War. It also has the greatest pump-up speech in cinematic history. Sometimes I listen to it when I'm feeling down and it makes me feel alive. Don't @ me, just watch.

Suspenseful, captivating, thrilling, and a true story? Argo tells the tale of one of the CIA's greatest top-secret missions that took place during the 1972 Iran Hostage Crisis. Ben Affleck stars in and directed this conversation starter of a film, which was nominated for *seven* Academy Awards and won three—including Best Picture.

An oldie but a goodie with Robin Williams and Nathan Lane. Hilarious at every moment. Once you meet Hank Azaria’s character, Agador Spartacus, you will never be the same. I laugh just thinking about it.

Is Albert Brennaman one of the greatest characters of all time? Probably not but his dance moves will go down in history. IYKYK, and if you don't, watch it.

Courtroom dramas and comedies are guaranteed crowd pleasers. When you've just about had it with your extended fam, turn on this movie and you'll be grateful Vinny isn't a relative. Unless...

If there's one thing guaranteed to please a crowd, it's a Tom-Hanks-and-Meg-Ryan romance. The classic tale of mistaken identity (and, oddly enough, the ins and outs of corporate book-selling) is charming enough that its take on the internet—chat rooms, lol—doesn't even seem annoyingly outdated, it just seems adorable. Set during the holiday season, it earns bonus points for cheer.

It's probably been way too long since anyone in your family sat down to watch the feature that put Matt Damon and Ben Affleck on the map, so why not rectify that together? Good Will Hunting even includes one of dearly departed Robin Williams's finest performances, in addition to wit and heart in equal measure.

The Oscar-nominated documentary might sound too cerebral for holiday consumption—it's about the tumultuous relationship between an artist and his wife—but the final product is touching, funny, and relatable.

An excellent movie series that the whole fam will get into. I mean, you can't go too long without re-watching the legend that is Katniss Everdeen. With two new installments, The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes and Sunrise on the Reaping, this franchise is expanding in ways that demand more and more marathons.

This award-winning Sundance documentary will effectively cure any desire for your family to overspend on gifts this holiday (it's about one of America's richest families and their major money troubles), while also providing a screamingly funny inside look at a wacky, decadent lifestyle typically seen only on reality television.

Everyone loves Jennifer Lawrence. And Bradley Cooper. And Robert De Niro. And dancing. And sports. And an exciting competition.

James Bond is always a crowd-pleaser, and Daniel Craig's third entry into the ever-expanding 007 canon offers up both action and drama to spare. Plus: Adele.

This 2012 Oscar winner for Best Documentary is a full cinematic experience, and one that just so happens to sound damn good as it goes down. The feature chronicles some of music's most under-appreciated talents (back-up singers), and weaves in inspiring stories with some incredible music. Your entire family will be dancing in no time.

The amazingly talented Lake Bell wrote, directed, and starred in this hilarious 2013 comedy about the weird world of movie voiceover artists. Despite its highly specific subject matter, the film still works as an uproarious story about a gal just trying to make it. Even better? Her dad just happens to be the world's best voiceover guy, so it's got extra family resonance.

Do your parents not quite understand your current lifestyle choices? Introduce them to the incredibly charming (and slightly dunderheaded) Frances Ha. Odds are, they'll feel much better about your direction in life, even as they fall totally in love with the perfect creature that is Greta Gerwig.

There's no better way to follow up You've Got Mail than with yet another Hanks/Ryan pairing. Get out the hankies.

Sure, the Hayley Mills original is a classic, but this modern take on the old "separated at birth" story is funnier and flashier. It also features Lindsay Lohan in her most adorable role ever. Just prepare your most diplomatic answer for when your grandmother inevitably asks you, "What's that cute girl up to these days?"

It's an inspirational sports tale that feels appropriate for the season without actually compelling anyone to take up a new sport during this winter.

If you haven't seen the re-make Annie movie, now is the perfect time to catch up on hard-knock lives, fluffy puppies, and enough good cheer to make the entire family stand up and sing.

Use this time together to catch up on classic features that might have otherwise eluded you, including the kind of stuff that will send your grandparents swooning into their own memories of Audrey Hepburn, Gregory Peck, and the Italy of yesteryear.

It's probably been a minute since you saw the ultimate family bonding feature, and, yes, it's as good as you remember.

This fictionalized making-of-Mary Poppins movie is charming, heartbreaking, and will fill you and your parents' hearts with nostalgia while also teaching you something new about the woman who invented the practically perfect nanny.

Speaking of getting nostalgia shot straight to the heart, Marielle Heller's film about Fred Rogers and a cynical reporter who profiled him is kind of a masterpiece. They’re played by Tom Hanks and Matthew Rhys, respectively. The movie also chips away at toxic masculinity in a subtle way, if you want to come to family movie night with a low-key feminist agenda.

Greta Gerwig's adaptation highlights two of literature's best parents — insert Bob Odenkirk's "my little women" gif here — and revitalizes the classic in a way that will make it fresh for you and your parents.

Nothing says "dad movie" more than watching someone who's good at their job solve a problem. Ridley Scott's The Martian is two hours and twenty-four minutes of just that. Need I say more? It’s also technically a comedy.

Share a little history lesson with your folks. Pride is the story of a group of gay rights activists in 1980s London who go to a mining community to support a worker's strike because they have a common enemy in the cops that target them for harassment. The happy, hopeful ending has "feel-good" written all over it.

Sick of your parents talking about Hamilton to the point where you're pretty sure they don't actually get it? Why not steer them towards another musical that was filmed for a streaming network? Just make sure to have plenty of tissues on hand. You'll need them.

This is a nice and tremendously well-acted indie movie about the employees at a group home and the kids they look after. It also has a cast stacked with some of today's biggest stars. So if you actually like being IMDb for your parents and answering "where do I know them from?" every five minutes to show off your Hollywood trivia skills, this is the perfect movie for your fam.

Alternatively, take a peek into what your parents were like at your age and watch this Boomer (compliment, in this case) classic. Indulge them. It does have a great soundtrack. And since it’s streaming on Tubi, you can introduce them to their next favorite app. Parents love anything that’s free.

It's science fiction, but also a comedy, and accessible for the whole family. You don’t need to know anything about Star Trek or any other science fiction franchise. Galaxy Quest makes fun of Star Trek fans, but in a loving way.

Dads love westerns, and this is a neo-western. There is "brief" "sexuality" in Hell or High Water, so fair warning there... but I think you and your folks can handle it.

This coming-of-age comedy is about a girl who loves Dolly Parton and enters a local beauty pageant in order to get closer to her mother. It's super cute, romantic, a gret way to honor Dolly’s legacy, and will get your toe tapping to boot!

If you must watch a war movie with your parents, you might as well make it a war movie full of cameos from all your crushes to hold your attention. George MacKay! Andrew Scott! Richard Madden! Colin Firth! (It's also a very good movie with engaging action and an emotional plot, you know, if you care.)

Amma Asante’s period drama about a biracial women set to inherit a fortune in 18th century England, is based on a true story. It’ll satisfy your Bridgerton craving without making things awkward. Belle also, hint-hint and nudge-nudge, features a young James Norton and Sam Reid.

Do people still watch this coming-of-age story about four boys in rural West Virginia who learn how to build rockets in the 1960s? They should! Jake Gyllenhaal plays the leader. Laura Dern plays the teacher who believes in him. It’s such a charmer.

This movie offers romance for the women in your family and the opportunity to start listing facts about Superman actor Christopher Reeve for the men. Or maybe your family defies such traditional gender roles, and it’s the opposite or some combination of the two. What you’ll all get is a great movie experience.

#6865. This Brand-New Illinois Farmhouse Was Designed to Look Hundreds of Years Old

The team at Buccellato Design and Architecture focused on incorporating heritage details and timeworn furnishings.

For Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, the best compliment they could receive about a project isn’t, “Wow, it looks so different in here.” It’s that a home looks like they haven’t changed a thing.

On a country road in Momence, Illinois, a beloved farmhouse stands as a timeworn landmark for the surrounding agricultural community. For the homeowners—a retired couple returning to their Midwestern roots—the property was as much a repository of memories as it was a place to create new ones. “Having grown up nearby, the property evoked a strong sense of memory and place,” says Aimee, who founded Buccellato Design and Architecture alongside her husband, Kevin. “At the outset of the project, and throughout, our clients wanted the new house to feel like it had always been there, so much so that perhaps the neighbors could be led to believe that the original house remained intact, yet amended by skillful renovation and discreet additions.”

It was an architectural sleight of hand rooted in a distinctly Midwestern philosophy: “Be good to the neighbors.” Alongside the Buccellatos, the couple approached the 6,000-square-foot rebuild with the restraint of preservationists and the imagination of storytellers. “Great care was taken to instill the home and outbuildings with a sense of history and rootedness, creating a home that feels as though it’s been part of this bucolic setting for centuries,” says Kevin of the project, which also included the restoration of the property’s original 19th-century barn and corn crib. “We drew inspiration from traditional farm complexes, where homesteads were added slowly over time, reconnecting the site with its Folk-Victorian-Farm-House and agricultural building roots.”

Inside, the narrative continues through antique white oak floors, salvaged beams, heritage florals, and rooms that borrow their palettes from the landscape. In the kitchen, a wall of windows replaces upper cabinetry, framing sunrise over the barn and walnut trees. The glass porch offers sweeping views of the Kankakee River, while a tucked-away speakeasy recalls the original farmhouse’s stone foundation.

For Kevin and Aimee, the guiding principle was a new construction home that felt “sensitive, not wasteful, polite, not ostentatious,” an exercise in knowing when architecture and interiors should defer to their surroundings. The result is a house with an invented past and a very real sense of belonging that does the Midwest—and the neighbors—proud.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Buccellato Design and ArchitectureStylist: Cate RaganLocation: Momence, IllinoisThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-and-a-half-bathroom home across 6,000 square feet

The entryway offers a fitting introduction to the home'’s carefully constructed sense of history, replete with nickel gap paneling, fumed and waxed antique white oak floors, and a light-filled staircase. Even the furnishings—including a vintage Persian rug and antique Jacobean gateleg table—suggest a collection acquired over generations.

Benjamin Moore’s Nelson Blue sets a serene backdrop for the sitting room, where traditional silhouettes and understated patterns make an easy pairing. “This room was designed to honor the history of this property and embody the love that our clients hold for this special place,” adds Aimee.

A tufted mohair sofa, realized in a rich umber, lends a sense of history, while an iconic feather lamp by Bunny Williams offers a reminder that even the most classic rooms benefit from a little irreverence.

In the kitchen, the Buccellatos traded upper cabinets for an expansive wall of windows overlooking the farmland, relocating the practical necessities to an adjacent dish room and larder. Salvaged white oak beams and barnboards overhead reinforce the home’s agricultural roots, while furniture-like cabinetry and leathered black quartzite countertops strike a timeless note.

With natural light pouring in from three sides, the dining room is what the Buccellatos describe as “peaceful, soft, and ethereal,” equally suited to both morning coffee and candlelit dinners. A dreamy mural wallpaper by Susan Harter references the property’s location along the Kankakee River, while formal seating lends a sense of occasion without sacrificing the home’s easygoing country charm.

Nestled among mature trees, the primary bedroom feels almost suspended in the surrounding landscape. The Buccellatos leaned into that sense of serenity with the palette. “It’s a dreamlike mix of earthy neutrals and pale blues that paints a serene backdrop for elegant details, vintage furnishings, and a delicate mix of subtle pattern,” adds Kevin.

A French country canopy bed, dressed in tailored Schumacher linen, takes center stage, while vintage-inspired furnishings and subtle pattern keep the room from feeling overly precious. An adjacent sitting room offers a place to read the morning paper or unwind with a cup of tea in the evenings.

In the vestibule, a little pattern goes a long way. A climbing floral from Schumacher in inky charcoal makes a charming counterpoint to the bedroom’s hushed palette, while a checked window bench is the ideal spot to read, giving the passageway purpose.

Pale blue walls and creamy millwork lend the primary bathroom an old-fashioned prettiness. A custom vanity introduces a tailored architectural element, while a Cowtan & Tout floral Roman shade softens the room’s more utilitarian details.

Designed as a playful respite for guests and future grandchildren, this bedroom plays off pattern and primary hues. “The red painted woodwork feels unexpected and cheerful against the blue-and-white wallpaper, striped rug, and patterned bedding,” says Aimee.

“The brick-clad main block echoes the footprint of the original farmhouse on this site, yet the story we wrote for the house begins with the folk Victorian wing, inspired by L-shaped farmhouses of that period,” says Kevin. “In our modern narrative, the clapboard-sided wing came first, followed by the brick-clad ’addition,’ which takes inspiration from the later Colonial Revival period in its scale and sophistication of detailing.”

Founded by Aimee and Kevin Buccellato, Buccellato Design & Architecture is a practice marked by twenty-plus years of close collaboration, in architectural practice and in life. With projects on both coasts and throughout the country, the firm’s award-winning work reveals a dedication to craft, artistry, and detail. Achieved in partnership with clients, their projects reflect a purposeful sense of rootedness in the landscape and abiding devotion to beauty and the comfort of home.

#6863. After I Kicked Drugs and Alcohol, I Found a New Addiction: Cheating on My Fiancée

Thirteen years of sobriety taught me that the most dangerous thing for an addict isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And I keep getting away with it.

Ethical nonmonogamy relies on a foundation of total transparency. Partners openly agree to terms and continually negotiate them together. Betrayals such as infidelity can take many shapes and break the agreement a couple has made, monogamous or not. For people in recovery, however, the adrenaline of secret keeping can feel familiar. Clinicians call this behavioral, or process, addiction—in which compulsion lies in the thrill of the chase rather than the payoff. It doesn’t excuse betrayal, but it can help explain how a person committed to honesty ends up living a lie.

For this installment of the Secret Lives of Men, we spoke to Owen,* a 36-year-old brand strategist with 13 years of sobriety who spent most of the first two years of his relationship cheating on the partner he plans to marry. His story begs the question: What does it mean to carry out “living amends,” an Alcoholics Anonymous term for permanently changing your behavior for the better, when you aren’t ready to confess?

*Names and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

Once a week, my fiancée has a therapy session over a video call that she takes from our bedroom. We both work from home, so while that happens, I’m usually barricaded in my office with noise-canceling headphones on, keeping up with work. Recently, I came out to the kitchen to pour a coffee after her hour was up, and she wasn’t home.

She takes walks, so there was no reason to panic. But my mind went where it has gone a lot lately: She found out. By whatever cruel trick of fate, she knew, and she was out there walking it off, letting the anger curdle. When she came back, she would bring hell with her.

I called once. She didn’t pick up. I didn’t try again. For half an hour, I paced the apartment unable to get a single thing done. I checked my phone every few seconds. I rehearsed what I’d say when she walked through the door: which parts I’d confess, which parts I’d still try to save. I worried myself sick. When she finally walked back in, it turned out she’d just been on a long phone call with her sister. She took off her headset, kissed me hello, and asked what I had in mind for dinner. My heart kept pounding long after she’d walked into the kitchen.

Until two years ago, I could say with real pride that I had never cheated on anyone. Not once. Not even when I was drinking and doing all those drugs, back when I lied about nearly everything else. I’d had opportunities, but infidelity was the one line I never crossed.

Advanced 12-step work taught me a strange paradox: Sometimes you have to sit with the agonizing truth that you are doing something wrong. That you aren’t ready or able to stop. I’ve built 13 years of sobriety on the idea that we are only as sick as our secrets. But I should admit something up front: I’m good at telling stories, especially about myself. Even now, I catch myself deciding which parts to share. So here it is: For the better part of two years, I cheated on the woman I’m going to marry. She still doesn’t know.

In recovery, you learn that the most dangerous thing that can happen to a person with an addiction isn’t getting caught—it’s getting away with it. And every time you skate by, the lie gets a little easier to tell. The disease gets a little more room to grow. I first learned that at 23. I had to learn it again in my 30s.

I come from a long line of talented, tortured people. My father sold commercial real estate and spent years in and out of a quiet, high-functioning addiction to painkillers. He went away for treatment twice. My mother was raised by an alcoholic and carried a lot of anger. My parents divorced when I was young, both remarried badly, and neither of their houses was a safe place emotionally.

At 19, while in college, I fell into a relationship I can only describe as wickedly codependent. We went through two miscarriages together and became trauma bonded. I didn’t know how to grieve, so I was drinking heavily and taking almost anything I could find: stimulants, psychedelics, dissociatives. Just before I turned 21, I caught my girlfriend in a lie so profound it ripped the wool off my eyes: She had been sleeping with my best friend for nearly the entire time we’d been together. One of the miscarried babies was actually his. I ended things the day I found out and took the situation as the perfect excuse to keep killing myself slowly.

I didn’t make it a year out of college before the consequences caught up with me. The fall after graduation, I was driving home from a concert when I got pulled over for a dead taillight. I was relatively sober and polite and was sure I’d talked my way out of it—right up until the officer asked me to empty my pockets. Inside were a bag of powder that I’d been promised was Molly and a few pills I didn’t have a prescription for. They booked me and sent everything off to the state lab. For three months, nothing happened. Meanwhile, I painted myself further into the corner before the floor ultimately fell out. When the charge finally came, I began a year and a half of court dates and probation.

As a first offender, I pled into a court-mandated, recovery-focused diversion program with regular drug tests. I followed the program to a T, at least for a while.

One weekend, when house-sitting alone for a neighbor, I took a drink out of boredom and pure spite. No test caught it. Then I lied about it to my counselors, the court, and my family. A month later, guilt finally won out, and I admitted it to my sponsor. A few days later, he brought it back up and told me I had to come clean to everyone.

“The fact that you were willing to conceal that from everyone for this long,” he said, “is the only evidence you need of how sick you are.”

The problem wasn’t the drink. It was how easily I hid it. I surrendered to the program and handed my life over to a higher power. I haven’t had a drink since.

I went celibate by choice for three years. I did a lot of therapy and learned that I had swung from codependent to avoidant. I had finally regained control, and I didn’t want to hand myself to anyone. Then, in my late 20s, I decided to give dating another shot.

Before long, I leaned into ethical nonmonogamy and dated as “solo poly,” which means I was seeing multiple people without a primary partner. Casual dating became my primary hobby. Some connections were purely physical; some were friends with benefits; some went emotionally deep. At my peak, I was sleeping with six people, two or three times a week. I felt I was winning at something. I was always honest with every partner about who I was, sobriety included. But I wasn’t honest with myself about why I needed so much of it.

Then, later in therapy, I came to understand that the dopamine that keeps people in an addiction loop is driven less by the thing itself than by the anticipation of getting it. Dating apps are built on that anticipation. For me, there were three hits: the match, the first nudes, the first hookup. In the program, we say the disease of addiction wears as many faces as it needs to. The chase was one of those faces.

I matched with Maya as she was getting ready to leave on a six-week work trip to Thailand. We messaged far longer than usual, trading long voice notes across the 11-hour time difference—her from hotel lobbies at night, me on morning walks. By the time we finally met, I already knew how she laughed. Our first date was electric, the best of my life. We sat at the bar until they started stacking chairs, talking about family, grief, books that had saved us. She was self-sufficient and emotionally deep. She was great at conversation. She was beautiful.

A few months into our relationship, we curled up on her couch on a Sunday afternoon and talked about ethical nonmonogamy. We’d both dabbled in it, but neither of us had done it inside a primary relationship. I told her I wanted it. She was open to it. We agreed to start monogamous and build the foundation first, but we never said for how long.

The truth is, our sex wasn’t great. Early on, I suggested something new in bed, and she went quiet and turned toward the wall. Neither of us brought it up the next morning. I internalized the idea that I couldn’t ask for what I wanted, that this was just how it would be until we opened things up. Instead of communicating, I let the resentment build.

Then, one afternoon at a coffee shop across town, I ran into Bree, a woman I used to hook up with. She had been the perfect friend with benefits: incredible chemistry but no desire for a relationship. We stayed and talked for an hour. When she mentioned she lived ten minutes away, neither of us pretended not to know what that meant. By that evening, I was at her place. It was the first time in my life I had been unfaithful in any way, and I felt awful. When I went home, I waited for the consequences. They never came.

A big part of addiction is justification. We were planning to be nonmonogamous, I told myself, so the way I’d just conducted myself was basically equivalent. It wasn’t that bad. I can see now how thin that logic was. Nonmonogamy runs on transparency and consent. I was keeping a secret.

Over the next year and a half, my infidelities came and went. A few more times with Bree. A handful of times with anonymous women in other cities. Trans women, sex workers. I traveled for work, and I took advantage of the distance. I kept my phone face down, hid dating apps, used end-to-end-encrypted texting. For each time I followed through, there were five times I stopped myself. Each close call made me feel like I had it under control.

Strangely, the woman I cheated with most also kept me from making worse mistakes. Bree became my confidante, the only person I was fully honest with about any of it. One night, I arranged to meet a sex worker. I was sitting in my car outside the hotel when clarity hit. The only thing I could think to do was call Bree. She talked me down. She and I ended up having sex that night. The program has a phrase for that kind of thinking: I want what I want when I want it.

Meanwhile, the relationship I was betraying kept getting better. We slowly began to practice nonmonogamy, and it took us far longer than either of us expected. We listened to the audiobook version of Polysecure by Jessica Fern together. We’d pause so often to talk that a half-hour chapter took two hours. It gave me a way to ask for what I wanted without it feeling like an attack on her. One night, Maya paused the book and asked me, plainly, how she could do better. For the first time, I believed she meant it, and I told her. The sex got better. Then, much better.

The final close call came in June. Bored at my desk, I connected with someone on an anonymous hookup site, and we moved to texting. A few messages in, after I’d sent a couple of nudes, my phone lit up with a long blackmail threat. Attached was a screenshot of Maya’s Instagram. This kind of thing had happened four or five times before, back when I was single. Go ahead, I would say. I never considered paying, and no one ever followed through. But this time, I closed my laptop, walked to the bathroom, sat on the edge of the tub until my hands stopped shaking, and blocked the number. This time, I had something to lose. I was forced to face how stupid I was being.

I shared the story in therapy. My therapist, who usually lets me do most of the talking, leaned back, looked at his clipboard, and said, “What the fuck are you doing? This is like the 20th time something like this has happened.”

I didn’t have an answer. Nothing had ever happened to me. That was the whole point.

This past August, on a weekend trip to the Catskills with friends, I proposed to Maya. She said yes before I could finish the question. I meant every word I said that weekend.

Maya and I have now officially opened our relationship. We’ve mostly played as a couple. When she sees someone on her own, she tells me everything the next morning. Last month, I sexted with a woman for days and chose not to tell her. Though I have permission, I can’t seem to give up the secrecy.

Growing up in the houses I did, I hid the truth to keep the peace. Recovery taught me to say it anyway, but I’ve only ever practiced with people who can’t leave, like a sponsor or a therapist. Telling Maya is different. Sometimes, I reverse the question: What if she were the one carrying a secret like this? I like to think that I’d find a way to accept it, because through everything I’ve done, I have never loved her any less.

In the program, when you can’t make amends without causing further harm, you make what’s called living amends. It starts with stopping the behavior. Then you try to love better, act cleaner, and stay present day after day, without asking for absolution. That’s where I am now. I pray regularly that this pain is my consequence. If that’s my penance, I’ll carry it.

#6861. 4 Weekend Decluttering Habits That Make Small Spaces Feel Bigger

A few smart systems can keep your counters, closets, and junk drawers in check.

The easiest clutter to manage is clutter that never has a chance to form. And in small spaces like galley kitchens and studio apartments, mess piles up naturally on any flat surface. Keeping it under control is less about “magic bullet” tips and more about systems you can actually keep up over time. So when we asked the professional organizers about how to declutter small spaces, the answer was in mindset and habits. If you can limit what comes into your space in the first place, you can better control its organization.

“I could say, ‘Grab a garbage bag and pick up all of the detritus left in the wake of a busy life and then put everything else where it belongs.’ That’s obvious,” Gayle M. Gruenberg of Let’s Get Organized says. “The hard part is actually DOING it. It requires motivation, presence of mind, initiative, and the necessary tools.”

Below, find the pro-approved habits that make a difference when you have more stuff than your space can hold. You can start these routines in just one weekend, and it’s far more attainable than tackling the massive pile in the garage.

Everything needs a home in your house. That’s the power of a well-placed “drop zone.” Even in small spaces, a catchall tray, bowl, or mini basket belongs at entryways or places of transition. A hook for your keys and bags when you come through the door, a bin for loose throw blankets, or a basket for remote controls on the mantel.

Throughout the day, it can take quite a bit of mental load to always put everything back when you use it. That’s where Cathy Orr, cofounder of The Uncluttered Life, recommends having a “collection basket.” It’s just one catchall container, where you can drop miscellaneous misplaced items that you pick up throughout the day. At the end of the night, take about 10 minutes to go through the collection basket and return everything to where it belongs. It’s just one small habit, rather than something to keep up with all day.

Impulse shopping lends itself to clutter very easily. The “One In, One Out” rule can curb this. It works like this: for every new thing you want to bring into your home, one thing has to go. Greenhalgh suggests checking your closet, fridge, and pantry before you shop—going through your back stock will save you both space and money.

“In small spaces, items accumulate very quickly,” Macie Kreutzer, founder of For the Love of Simple, says. “Before shopping for new items (home goods, clothing) clear out at least one item from this category. Don't bring in more than your space can physically contain.”

Once you have everything you’re ready to get rid of, make the donation process as seamless as possible. Fill your donation bags and boxes in the hallway or in the car. This encourages you to actually finish getting rid of the clutter, rather than letting them collect dust in the garage.

“Nothing gets to wander back inside just because it once lived there,” Carroll Cartwright, professional organizer and owner of Neatly & Co., says. “And this is why I love moving everything out of the space and into the hall or a separate area first. The things you decide not to keep are already halfway out the door. Keep them moving.”

It is so tempting to take advantage of the freebies in life. But this is how linen closets and kitchen drawers get quickly overrun. Unless there’s a specific and immediate need for them, you’re better off getting rid of them.

“They are usually cheap and break easily anyway,” Diane Greenhalgh, owner of Tiny to the Max, says. “That includes airplane and hotel freebies, special event or conference swag, and extra takeout plastic utensils and condiments. Your junk drawer will thank you.”

If you already have a drawer overflowing with freebies, it’s time to start culling and evaluating. You might part with two or 10 things, or you might choose to keep it all. “The point is that you’ve looked at every single item and made a decision about it, instead of continuing to store things simply because they were already there,” Cartwright says. “More likely than not, you’ll find something that makes you say, ‘Why on earth do I still have this?’”

The advice your grandma gave you was spot-on: It’s easier if you wash the dishes as you cook. That same principle applies to keeping your small space tidy. Mess feels larger with less square footage to work with, so once you’re finished with something, put it away.

“Put away your makeup after you've applied it in the morning, stack dishes in the dishwasher and wipe down surfaces after each meal, and tuck away work or homework into a drawer once you're done for the evening,” Greenhalgh says.

For the days you can’t clean as you go, Cartwright suggests spending 10 to 15 minutes a day “closing your circles.” It’s what helps your home stay clean over time, instead of just for a few weeks. This period is when you put the scissors back in the drawer or hang up your jacket.

“Most clutter is just a collection of unfinished tasks,” Cartwright says. “This small commitment allows you to wake up to order instead of yesterday’s chaos, and that is a much better way to start the day!”

#6857. Irish Designers Share Their 7 Coziest Style Secrets That We Should All Be Stealing

Here’s how to embrace that “Green Wave” with your interiors.

Over the past few years, Americans have become notably charmed by Irish culture—even the L.A. Times coined it a “Green Wave.” From splitting the “G” when drinking a pint of Guinness and bragging about it on social media to spotlighting Irish celebrities like author Sally Rooney, musician Hozier, and actress Saoirse Ronan, we as a society—myself very much included—have become a bit obsessed with the Emerald Isle, and I think it’s high time we let this Green Wave roll into the interior design scene.

If you’ve ever had the privilege to visit a home in the Emerald Isle, you’ll know that Irish spaces are warm, welcoming, and lived-in, with many of the design elements specifically used to prioritize comfort and community, something American homeowners have begun adopting. “Irish homes have traditionally needed to be comfortable, functional, and adaptable to a climate that can be cold, damp, and unpredictable,” Galway-based designer Niamh McKenna tells House Beautiful. “Traditionally, our homes are also much smaller, so beautiful rooms must also work for everyday life.”

However, it’s not just the environment which influences their interiors. Keep reading to find out the quintessentially Irish design details that local designers love to celebrate and believe American homes should borrow.

During the Georgian era, which spanned from the early 1700s to the early 1800s, Ireland experienced a population boom. During this time, Dublin also underwent a sizable redevelopment when it comes to architecture and urban infrastructure, according to History Ireland. It’s these elegant details that designer Julianne Kelly loves so much and thinks others should adopt.

“There is so much history in these houses and the proportions are beautiful: high ceilings, ornate cornicing, original sash windows, fireplaces, and rich paneled doors,” Kelly says. “These details give a room a sense of history and permanence.”

The Merrion Square area in Dublin, she adds, is a great place to walk around to view some of these rich elements in person, specifically the iconic painted Georgian doors.

Once you leave the streets of Georgian Dublin, you’ll find the sprawling countryside with “a beautiful, varied landscape that is impossible to ignore when designing the interiors for a house,” Kelly explains. She uses many natural elements found just outside the window in her designs, like greenery, stone, timber, and natural fibers like wool and linen that feel authentic to the country and only improve with age. Colors inspired by the scenes outside help the interior feel connected to the earth as well.

The textiles specifically add much-needed warmth and coziness to a room. “I love these as they add personality, a sense of calm, and an old worldliness that doesn’t look over manufactured or brand-spanking new!”

“The fireplace has traditionally been at the heart of the Irish home, and I think that idea is still incredibly relevant,” Kelly says. Ornate or simple, stone or tiled, wood-burning or gas—the details of the fireplace don’t really matter, as long as it serves as a natural focal point and the furniture is arranged around it.

McKenna adds that centering the hearth rather than the TV promotes conversation and connection. “It’s a reminder that a room can be designed around how people gather and interact,” she explains. The energy and warmth of a fireplace, even as modern homes move away from open fires, brings comfort and beauty to any room, and that’s something worth preserving.

Step into an Irish person’s home, and you’ll likely find a lot of old mixed in with the new. “Irish people love to hold onto their items,” Kelly says. “I’m not sure if it’s due to our famine history, but on most projects I work on, people have sentimental items that I have to work with.”

The designer will always try to work with these existing pieces to come up with some sort of eclectic mix of modern and vintage. She loves how unique and personal these homes turn out to be; they tell a great story. “And the Irish love a good story,” she adds.

In Ireland’s rainy climate, a home’s interiors—especially practical, hardworking spaces—need to feel both beautiful and comfortable.

“Bathrooms and kitchens are among the hardest-working parts of our homes, and we see it important to give the most functional part of our homes the same thought and attention as our living spaces,” McKenna explains. “We love to connect the indoors to the outdoors in these spaces, by using natural materials, earthy colors, and organic textures to create an interior that is as beautiful to experience as it is practical to use.”

Much of the residential architecture in Ireland is designed to work with the chilly, wet climate. “Traditional Irish architecture features deep window reveals, thick walls, and smaller windows than what might be seen elsewhere,” McKenna explains.

These choices could easily make a home feel dark and cold, but the designer says that when oriented correctly to the outside light, these elements only add to a cozy, warm environment in the winter and a cool place for relief in the summer. “I love that these characteristics also create a depth, shadow, and texture that are difficult to replicate with decorative finishes alone,” she adds.

Similarly to the architecture, the interiors are decorated to account for the damp climate, as well. “We make comfort a priority rather than a finishing touch, by layering furnishings such as window treatments, tactile rugs and cushions, and blankets,” McKenna says. Even if someone were to decorate their home rather minimally, the layers of tactile fabrics found in Irish homes still ensure the space feels inviting and lived-in.

#6853. Michael J. Fox Says Parkinson's Taught Him This

“I don’t look at any time [in my life] and say, ‘Who was I then?’ or ‘Who will I be in a week?’ or “Who am I now?’ ” the 65-year-old Back to the Future actor says.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Michael J. Fox is a fighter. When the Back to the Future and Spin City actor was diagnosed with Parkinson’s disease in 1991, he refused to let the condition cut his life or his career short. He kept acting, earned some Emmy Awards and Golden Globes statuettes, and most recently appeared on season 3 of Apple TV’s Shrinking with Harrison Ford.

As the 65-year-old actor tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian in the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned,” his drive all stems from a quote his father told him when he left Canada to pursue acting in America: “Well, if you’re going to be a lumberjack, you might as well go to the goddamn forest.”

Though Fox’s father, a former Canadian Armed Forces soldier, was likely talking about Hollywood, Fox says that he took his advice to mean that if you’re going to do anything in life—whether it be his acting, his activism, or even his sheer optimism in the face of disease—you better fully engage yourself in it.

“My parents were born into the Depression ... so they were risk averse. Their whole life was just to have a nice life that didn’t leave anything exposed,” Fox continues. “And I wanted to do all this crazy stuff.” Namely Teen Wolf, three Back to the Future films, playing onstage with Coldplay, poking fun at his condition on Curb Your Enthusiasm (“because it’s hilarious”), and never letting Parkinson’s slow him down.

“I went through eight years of keeping it to myself and trying to forestall it with exercise and diet, learn a little bit from the doctors and experts, and then when I decided to share it it was like this great slap on the forehead,” Fox says about why he formed the Michael J. Fox Foundation. “I thought, There’s all these people that are experiencing this. Why aren’t we sharing our information about it?”

Elsewhere in the episode, Fox shares what he’s learned from Rush Limbaugh accusing him of faking his diagnosis, meeting Barack Obama before he was president, and raising roughly $3 billion for Parkinson’s research. You can watch the episode above, watch it on YouTube here, or listen to it on Spotify and Apple Podcasts.

“I don’t look at any time [in my life] and say, ‘Who was I then?’ or ‘Who will I be in a week?’ or “Who am I now?’ ” Fox says. “I just put my head down enough that I could still see where I was going and just plow ahead. And I realize now that I’m in this great position where I can have an effect on people’s lives.”
