#6852. 5 Common Mistakes That Are Making Your Vintage Decor Look All Wrong—and How Designers Get It Right

Experts say the best rooms mix eras, styles, and stories.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: This space, designed by Sarah West, strikes the right balance of vintage and contemporary decor, proving that a mix is always better than sticking to one design era.

It’s not just the Instagram algorithm playing tricks on you: Everyone really is looking to antique and vintage decor. Homeowners are growing tired of rooms that look as though everything was bought from a single brand in one fell swoop. We’re now at a point where a collected, curated, personalized feel has replaced quiet luxury.

“I think it’s important to add at least some kind of antique or vintage piece in every room,” says Sarah West, founder of Sarah West & Associates and a designer for over 20 years. “Even if you’re doing a more modern or transitional room, a sense of history adds weight to a space.”

It’s not necessarily as easy as plopping one antique into a room and calling it a day, however, and that can feel intimidating. Even once you learn to shop for antiques and vintage like a pro, what do you actually do with those finds when you’re home?

While the designers we spoke with are quick to point out there really are no strict rules here—it’s about your style and what you love—there are a few key mistakes that pop up most often when people start styling with antique and vintage decor. These designers have the scoop on how to avoid those missteps and make your treasures, and your entire room, shine.

Committing to a massive piece of furniture or tasking yourself with overhauling an entire room with antique or vintage pieces can get overwhelming fast. You may make decisions you don’t love down the line.

If you decide you’re going to decorate a whole room in a limited amount of time, and you’re set on sourcing antique or vintage items, you’ll likely end up settling for things just because you need them, says Nancy Cavaliere, a New York–based interior decorator, stylist, and vintage collector. Go slow and start small.

“I’d rather live with an empty corner while I wait for the perfect piece than fill it with something I don’t love,” she notes. “The most interesting homes evolve over time. I think a room should tell the story of the person living in it, not the story of one shopping trip.”

Ease your way into decorating with vintage and antiques by starting small. Think one piece of art, one mirror, one vase.

“Don’t overthink it,” West advises. “Take a small piece, try it out in the room for a couple of days, see how it feels.” Do you like the piece in that spot? Does it work with other elements of the room? Does it inspire other styling choices for the space? Try moving it around and interacting with it to find its perfect positioning and see where that takes you.

As you start out getting a feel for that smaller piece, remember that just because it’s unique doesn’t mean it has to be the focal point of the whole room.

“Don’t treat vintage pieces like they have to be the star of the room simply because they’re old,” says Cavaliere. “Not every antique needs a pedestal. Sometimes the most beautiful thing you can do is let an extraordinary piece sit comfortably alongside something completely ordinary.”

Fully incorporating vintage or antique pieces into a space imbues the room with that richness and intrigue.

“These pieces give feelings to a room,” West says. “If you put something in a corner, as you transition throughout the room, it gives this element of surprise.”

This includes creating high-low mixes, says Cavaliere, who points out, “a $20 flea market find can look incredible next to an expensive designer piece. It’s all about how you put things together.”

In fact, that mix is something to strive for, says Alex Bass, founder of Salon 21, Art Advisory & Interior Design Firm.

“I love a contrast, like a room with modern architecture that leans heavily into antiques or vice versa. If you want to incorporate pieces from Pottery Barn, CB2, that’s fine, but there are also so many affordable vintage finds. And something like an antique light fixture can make all of these pieces work.”

Speaking of contrasts, all of our designer sources say a very common misconception they encounter is that you can only decorate a room with pieces from the same era.

“Just because you have a Victorian chair doesn’t mean you need a Victorian living room,” Cavaliere says. “My own home is a mix of different periods, styles, colors, and patterns, and that's what gives it personality. I love the tension between pieces that seemingly have nothing in common. And it's fun!”

Bass cites the example of Art Deco being popular right now, while articles are claiming midcentury modern is “out.” She encourages people to think of pieces less rigidly, assuming they have to commit fully to one style, or toss another style because someone declared a new one the next big thing.

“These are not ‘trends,’ these are design periods that already existed,” she explains. “It’s about having the eye to pull pieces from different periods that speak to you and make sense in your room.” You can mix Art Deco and midcentury modern, for instance, by using elements characteristic of them both, like marble, warm woods, mixed metals, and bold color pops.

There can be a fear with vintage and antique pieces that changing anything “ruins” them. But unless it’s a significantly valuable piece you’re investing in or something you plan on having appraised, Bass says, embrace the ability to make special pieces work for your space.

“Don’t be afraid to reupholster,” she says—and not just if the fabric is in poor shape. “If I buy a piece and the structure is beautiful but I want to add a contemporary fabric and print, that can make for a fun contrast with the frame.”

For example, Bass fell in love with a modular midcentury sectional, but its fabric didn’t quite complement the space she was designing, so she chose a contemporary fabric that worked with its aesthetic.

This, Cavaliere points out, frees you up from obsessing over condition when shopping for pieces. If something has great bones but its fabric or finish has seen better days—and if you love it—don’t pass it up. Remember you can reupholster, strip and stain or paint, and repair.

Just factor the cost of those fixes into the price of the item to help make your decision. Especially because, as Bass advises, it’s better to consult a professional unless you’re well-versed in certain projects. Stripping and staining a wood piece is fairly easy, but when it comes to reupholstering and other repairs, you’ll likely actually save money by outsourcing to a pro rather than potentially damaging the piece yourself.

Similarly, it’s typical for people to feel they can’t actually make use of vintage and antique pieces, because they think they’re too precious. But especially considering how well-made many older pieces are, they’re begging to be put to work. Again, it’s actually incorporating them into your everyday life that gives your room and entire home that sense of personalization, curation, and storytelling.

“One hundred years ago, people were using their silver,” West says. “In my kitchen, you’re eating with hotel silver.”

This includes getting creative with how you use antique and vintage pieces—don’t feel bound by what something is “supposed to” be for.

“One of my favorite tricks is turning vintage drapes and valances into bed canopies,” Cavaliere says. “I did this in my daughter’s room and I absolutely love it.” Cavaliere also finds unexpected uses for small vessels and decorative objects: a creamer and sugar set in the bathroom to hold Q-tips and cotton balls; dishes, trays, and crystal bowls to sort accessories in her closet.

“A vintage china cabinet doesn't have to live in a dining room just because it was designed to hold dishes,” she says. “One of my favorite tricks is using china cabinets to display and store shoes and handbags in a dressing room or closet. Why spend a ton of money on custom cabinetry when you can repurpose a beautiful vintage piece that has so much more character?”

West agrees. “Purchase an armoire, flip the doors, add mirrors, and make it a bar,” West suggests. “Find an old door knocker you love? Hang a towel off of it. Use a tablecloth to make a pillow. Tell a story.”

#6848. So...What’s Going on With the Bachelor Franchise? We Finally Have a Premiere Date for ‘The Bachelor’ Season 30

Ever since ABC pulled the plug on Taylor Frankie Paul’s season of The Bachelorette just days before its premiere, fans have been wondering: What’s next for the Bachelor franchise? And after a month of rumors surrounding the fate of The Bachelor and its many spin-offs, fans finally have an actual update. According to Variety, The Bachelor season 30 is set to premiere on Sunday, March 21, 2027.

It was first revealed earlier this year by Deadline that both The Bachelor and Bachelor in Paradise would return for new seasons in 2027.

“The Bachelor is almost a perfect format and it is really powerful,” Disney’s reality chief Rob Mills told the outlet at the time. “It’s over two decades old, and it’s seen really good days and really bad days. But it’s always had a lot of resilience, especially when the people making it really, really care about it, because that fan base also really cares about it. However The Bachelor comes back next year, it will be done with a lot of thoughtfulness and care. I do think better days are ahead for the franchise.”

As a reminder, season 22 of The Bachelorette was scheduled to premiere on March 22 but was canceled amid reports that Taylor and her ex, Dakota Mortensen, were involved in a domestic dispute.

A statement from a Disney Entertainment Television spokesperson was released on March 19, saying, “In light of the newly released video just surfaced today, we have made the decision to not move forward with the new season of The Bachelorette at this time, and our focus is on supporting the family.”

Rumors about how the franchise would move forward (if at all) have been circulating ever since, as the decision to cancel Taylor’s season reportedly also impacted production on The Bachelor and Bachelor in Paradise. TMZ previously reported that the network had planned to cast one of the finalists on Taylor’s season as the next Bachelor but “it was all predicated on that contender becoming famous” from Taylor’s season…which, of course, didn’t happen.

Likewise, a separate source told TMZ that the season 22 contestants would possibly have another chance at love on the upcoming season of Bachelor in Paradise, though “it’s unclear how many might get that opportunity.”

And according to yet another source for People, Warner Bros. reportedly held a meeting with Taylor’s men on the same day the show was canceled (save for her winner, Doug Mason) and told them their “contracts will be in place for a year.”

As for Taylor’s unaired season of The Bachelorette? Rob told THR that he doesn’t know whether it will ever air on ABC or stream on Hulu and is taking everything “a day at a time”...but at this point, it feels safe to conclude that season 22 of The Bachelorette will be shelved indefinitely.

#6847. Piece Together the Perfect Pandora Holiday Gift for Her

From a little sparkle to a personal engraving, these pieces make a point of showing you care.

Great gifts prove you’ve been paying attention. They show you know what brings her joy, what she reaches for every day, and what she’d never think to buy herself. For the women who matter most in your life, that kind of thoughtfulness goes a long way. Mom, for example, deserves something that thanks her for a lifetime of being your first call when something goes wrong. Your sister craves a piece that feels unmistakably her. Your significant other needs something that says more than a dozen roses ever could. The trick is finding a gift with a little staying power, something sentimental, meaningful, and made to last. Jewelry does all three.

This is where Pandora comes in. Founded in Copenhagen in 1982, the jewelry brand has grown from a small Danish shop into a global name, known for its hand-finished designs and signature charm bracelets.

To make the search easier, we’ve picked five pieces for five important women: your mom, sister, significant other, best friend, and daughter. For each one, these sparkling options will become part of her daily rotation, a marker of a particular milestone, and a reminder that the person who gave it to her got it right. Consider this your holiday cheat sheet.

Your sister has always had a way of stealing the spotlight. Might as well give her the earrings to match. The set pairs sterling silver hoops with baguette-cut stones and detachable star charms, so she can switch up her look depending on the occasion. This means she gets two ways to wear them, because even after a lifetime of knowing her, you’re always discovering another side to her that’s worth celebrating.

Some things are better together, including the two of you. This pendant necklace brings three connected stars to life in sterling silver and 14k gold plating, giving the mixed-metal design a little extra dimension. It’s a thoughtful way to celebrate the person who makes your world brighter, shares your best adventures, and puts up with your quirks. The last part alone deserves a gift.

She may be growing up, but she’ll always be your little princess. This ring gives that title a stylish upgrade, with a golden chevron silhouette and a tapered baguette-cut stone at its center. Crafted in 14k gold plating, it’s a sweet reminder that she shouldn’t have to wait for a royal wedding to wear a tiara.

A good best-friend gift proves you know her taste almost as well as she does. The Sparkling Baguette Tennis Bracelet makes a strong case for that, with a sleek row of baguette-cut stones set in 14k gold plating. It’s polished enough to dress up a special occasion outfit, easy enough for every day, and just the sort of thing she’d pick out herself. Knowing her that well? That’s the real flex.

This tag pendant in sterling silver can be engraved with a date, a few meaningful words, or initials that form a message that needs no explanation to anyone else. She can wear it on its own or layer it with her favorite necklaces. The best part? You get to decide what it says. Mom has spent years remembering the little things about you. Now it’s your turn to give her something personal.

#6844. This Luxury Spa Is New England’s Best-Kept Secret

Powered by Valmont at The Preserve Sporting Club & Resort, the Hilltop Spa is Rhode Island’s hidden gem

Rhode Island may not be the first place you think of when booking a luxury spa and resort getaway, but it should be. While the Ocean State is well known for its miles of Atlantic coastline, an exclusive, if-you-know-you-know destination is tucked away among 3,500 acres of rolling hills: Richmond’s The Preserve Sporting Club & Resort.

The Preserve is designed for nature lovers, with hiking trails, on-site equestrian training stables, and the nation’s only North American Bentley off-road track (the resort even picked me up in a Bentley truck from the Providence train station). The grounds also offer a range of activities, like clay shooting, archery, rock climbing, ziplining, and more, depending on the season. But beyond the private sporting club and resort’s recreational charms, The Preserve is equally intentional about members’ and visitors’ self-care, one reason the resort partnered with Valmont Skincare to develop the beauty brand’s fourth spa location, the Hilltop Spa.

Valmont’s 41-year-old brand is steeped in Swiss scientific expertise, which I experienced during my weekend-long stay at The Preserve and my visit to the Hilltop Spa. Beyond steam rooms, saunas, and private whirlpools in both the women’s and men’s locker rooms, the spa features a coed hydrotherapy area with two whirlpools, a cold plunge, and a private after-dark experience complete with champagne.

After a quick change into a fluffy robe, I relaxed with a brief aromatherapy session, then received the Preserve Custom massage, which was deeply restorative. A few minutes later, after lounging in the spa’s designated relaxation room and enjoying some healthy snacks, I moved on to my customized Hydration of the Bisses facial, a plumping 60- to 90-minute treatment that targets the lymphatic system to improve fluid flow. No matter which Valmont treatment you choose at the Hilltop Spa, each includes its own Butterfly Motion, a proprietary massage technique.

“Valmont’s signature facials target main skin concerns like hydration, lifting, and firming, and combine cellular cosmetics with exclusive massage techniques,” says Viviana Garrido, Valmont’s U.S. sales director. “Guests love the immediate glow, lifting effect, and sense of deep relaxation. What makes the experience unique is the combination of performing yet relaxing protocols in an immersive sensory journey inspired by the Swiss Alps, delivering results for both the skin and the mind.”

True to The Preserve’s ethos of holistic beauty and wellness in the treatment room and beyond, the spa is often a first, though likely recurring, stop for its guests. Beyond its Valmont-approved amenities, the sporting club offers yoga, Pilates, sound bathing, and personalized fitness training, some of which I also took part in during my stay.

“Each provides an opportunity for guests to reconnect with themselves through movement, mindfulness, and relaxation,” notes resort manager Clinton Cox. To mentally disconnect in warmer months and through mid-fall, The Preserve also offers its bespoke Tree House Experience, developed by Cox himself and designed for couples, close friends, or small groups. The two-hour immersive forest wellness journey combines therapeutic body treatments, guided stretching, a 90-minute massage, relaxation, and a specially curated culinary menu.

“At The Preserve, we believe skincare should extend beyond the treatment room,” explains Cox. “It is not simply about how your skin looks when you leave the spa; it is about investing in its long-term health, vitality, and appearance while embracing an experience defined by exceptional service, refined surroundings, and genuine hospitality.”

Garrido agrees, noting that the company partnered with The Preserve as its fourth spa location in the United States because of shared philosophies of exceptional craftsmanship for guest and user experiences, a deep respect for nature, and a shared commitment to longevity. “Its unique setting and reputation for luxury hospitality provide the perfect environment for Valmont treatments and skincare expertise,” says Garrido. “The partnership allows us to offer guests a truly immersive wellness experience where luxury, performance, longevity, and well-being come together naturally.”

With so much to try, you can tailor a visit to The Preserve to your unique beauty and wellness preferences, which is part of what makes the Rhode Island sporting club and resort so singular. Seemingly off the beaten path, but more like hidden in plain sight, it’s virtually a home away from home for New England residents—the property even has a selection of tiny homes you can stay in—and a short train ride for those in the tri-state area.

For more than 150 years, Harper’s Bazaar has been the preeminent fashion and beauty resource for women at every age. We cover what’s new and what’s next in beauty by working with the world’s leading authorities in dermatology, plastic surgery, cosmetics, skincare, haircare, and fragrance. Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

#6843. What It’s Really Like Being the Third in a Threesome

“It kinda takes all of the pressure off of me.”

Although Michelai, 33 had been in threesomes before, she was typically the person in the relationship inviting an additional body to the mix. But for the first time, she was going to be the third—and she was thrilled by the idea of it.

She was approached by a married couple on a trip to Vegas. "They were seeking me out, which also made me a little bit more confident and comfortable,” she says.

When it comes to threesomes, conversations usually revolve around experimenting sexually when you’re already in a relationship: What boundaries to discuss, whether it’s the right decision for your and your partner, and what aftercare may look like. It’s rare that the third person in the scenario is addressed. But communication, attention, and care are required for everyone involved, including the person joining just for the night.

“A ‘successful threesome’ isn’t about fulfilling the couple’s fantasy—unless that is explicitly discussed, negotiated, and consented to,” sex therapist Dr. Ty David Lerman, PhD tells Cosmopolitan. “Rather, it’s about all parties showing up as equals with all needs, desires, pleasure, and boundaries being honored.”

And when you do it right, it can be really hot and fun, without all of the responsibility that may come if you’re the one in the relationship.

“It kinda takes all of the pressure off of me,” Jess, 30 says.

Jess joined her best girlfriend and her situationship at the time. At first, it was just to watch them (which she really enjoyed). But eventually it turned into a regular hookup with the both of them.

Stepping into an established couple’s world could feel awkward, but Michelai says it didn’t. Because she’d been the “primary” woman before, she knew which questions to ask. “I made sure there were no unspoken rules,” she says. “I laid everything out on the table, and they laid everything out on the table.”

She also read the room. The husband was clearly letting his wife lead, so Michelai directed her questions to the wife before she ever approached him. And the husband’s etiquette in the moment won her over: “I really respected that he would straight-up ask her something in the middle of us doing something, like whether he could do this or that. I was like, ‘You know what? Hell yeah.’”

Jess, meanwhile, felt like a bit of an outsider at first—but only because she wanted to give her friend time to adjust. Since the threesome was her idea, she didn’t want to overstep. “The first time I was with them, I just watched,” she says. Afterward, her friend and the situationship asked her: Why didn’t you touch yourself? Why didn’t you join? “I didn’t want to overstep.”

Since then Jess has learned how important communication is to being a third.

“Communication is probably just as important as the threesome itself,” Michelai says. “You need to know what everyone wants, what everyone’s comfortable with, and what everyone is expecting the experience to be.”

She’s heard the horror stories of couples that only sought out a third to save the relationship or where boundaries were drawn around the man’s pleasure in an MFF.

“There’s a term in the polyamory community called ‘unicorn hunters,’” Dr. Lerman says. It’s reserved for a couple, usually heterosexual, seeking a third, usually a woman, to fulfill a fantasy, usually the man’s. In practice, he says, this often means the couple using the third for their own purposes. “The third feeling objectified, used by one or both of the couple, disempowered to really consent to the role they are playing for the couple, and often leaving unsatisfied.”

His fix is a stoplight system: green means hell yes, yellow means needs negotiation, red means hell no. He recommends running through it before anyone gets near a bedroom, and using it again mid-action, because consent is ongoing and can be revoked at any time. Or just ask, “How is everyone doing?” Simple, and it works.

But this doesn’t mean there’s a set routine that’s going to play out in the moment. “There’s this expectation that threesomes are all three people going at it equally, but I think it can be something a little bit more defined than that,” Jess says. She and her friend had decided beforehand that they wanted to “use him like a toy,”—something he had no issue with—so the two women didn’t hook up with each other. The rules can be unconventional as long as everyone agrees to them.

And as unsexy as it may seem to have a lot of discussion, it really pays off during the sex.

“We had conversations about everything before we got in the bedroom, so when we got in the bedroom, everything felt really organic,” Michelai says.

Being a third may seem more “relaxed” than being in the couple inviting another person in, but there are still instances where it might not be a fit.

A major red flag is if it’s obvious that the couple hasn’t talked to each other. “A major tell for the third in vetting a couple is if the couple has already had these conversations before you enter the picture,” Dr. Lerman says. “If they haven’t, don’t walk, run! It’s a glaring red flag that they do not know what they want, and they don’t have the communication skills to talk about it together, which means they sure aren’t going to be able to do that with you either.”

Things like double standards, rules that only apply to you, no conversation about sexual health, or any action that may make you feel like more of an accessory than a person.

“Pay attention to how the couple reacts when you say no, or voice a need, or your own boundary,” he says.

Michelai saw the flip side of this when she was the primary. She and her boyfriend brought in one of her best friends from college. “He was hyper-focused on her for like 10 minutes, and that was the longest 10 minutes of my life,” she says. She left the room. (“Should I have left? Probably not.”)

For the person who goes home alone, aftercare can be an afterthought, which is exactly the problem. Couples often assume that once the sexual interaction is over, it’s fine for the third to just get up and leave. But how would that feel in any other sexual scenario? Not so great, right?

Michelai explains that the married couple made sure to make her feel welcomed—without any awkward obligation to feel part of the relationship. “We definitely had the Kiki, cuddle, laughs, yap session after each time we had sex, and I felt very comfortable.” The wife even asked for alone time with her, so the two ordered champagne and took a bubble bath while the husband slept. “We didn’t have sex again without him, but we were cute, cuddly, making out, and had a really nice bubble bath.”

And when it was over, it was over. “I think we all happily had a great experience and happily went our separate ways,” she says.

Jess’s first time was a different story. The guy got weird afterward. “He was like, ‘OK, I’m just going to go to bed,’ and didn’t invite us to stay the night or ask how it went or anything like that, which was very jarring.” She and her friend salvaged the night with a debrief on the car ride home: How did you feel? Did you like that? It was hot when he did this. Ideally, she says, everyone would debrief together, or at least one-on-one.

Dr. Lerman says aftercare should be negotiated upfront, like everything else. “Good aftercare looks like starting that conversation ahead of any play,” he says. Will there be a puppy pile of cuddling? A text the next day? A second date to debrief? “If we can’t consent to aftercare, we don’t play. Simple.” Being the third adds a layer: what happens when the couple goes back to being a couple and you’re outside it? “Aftercare is ultimately about making sure nobody feels disposable once the clothes go back on.”

“I think you can suss out the vibe and feel the vibe between the couple to know if it’s a safe space to join in on,” Michelai says. She’s flattered when couples approach her, too. “That actually makes me feel more confident.”

Jess’s criteria are simple: both people have to be into it. “It wasn’t just one of the partners trying to do something sexually adventurous.” And she wants to be treated like a person. “These are real people that you’re probably going to be having a sexual encounter with, and they should be treated as such.” Couples can get a little fetishistic about a third, she says, but you can weed that out quickly by seeing whether both of them want to sit and talk with you like a real human.

“The most common mistake occurs when a couple takes on the mindset of ordering up a sexual experience rather than approaching this as a negotiation with all parties,” Dr. Lerman says says. Opening with a laundry list of rules the third has no say in creates a power imbalance from the jump. He also warns against expecting equal chemistry all around, which rarely happens. Be conscious of everyone and bring them all into the play, but let attraction go where it goes.

#6842. This Colorful California Mountain Home Gives Classic Ski House Style a Fresh New Look

Using the surrounding landscape as palette inspiration, designer Alden Fletcher brought 70s retro flair to this family retreat.

When most people think of a classic ski chalet, they picture log-lined walls, dark corners, a raging hearth, plenty of plaid, and maybe even a taxidermied deer or two. But a certain kind of magic strikes when high-altitude architecture hits the right notes, and this Truckee, California, retreat is a masterclass in making the mountains sing.

Built almost 20 years ago, this family holiday home boasted all the 90s mountain classics: an abundance of knotty pine, traditional slate floors, and a minimalist beige palette. Undoubtedly stylish for the time, the retreat now felt out of step with the Bay Area family looking to make it into a personalized winter escape. The owners knew updates were imminent (necessary, even), but they feared losing the home’s classic California soul. Fighting modernism’s white-box aesthetic, they called in Alden Fletcher of the eponymous San Francisco-based design firm to meld the home’s retro architecture with a fresh, forest-inspired brief.

Noting the owner’s concerns and dedication to sustainability, the project’s goal was not to remodel for grandeur, but to restore the home’s charm. Fletcher’s task was to design around storied memories, the family’s obvious love for winter sports, and their passion for entertaining—all while combining the crisp aesthetic of the alpine wilderness with cozy textiles and natural details to create the ultimate mountain sanctuary. By pulling saturated hues from the surrounding landscape and sourcing perky patterns inspired by the local culture, the redesign breathes new life into the slope-side abode without sacrificing sentiment.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Alden Fletcher Interiors Stylist: Beth Protass Location: Truckee, CAThe Space: A five-bedroom, four-bathroom home across 3,000 square feet

The home's hallways set the tone for the entire design scheme. On the first floor, just beyond the main door, wall-mounted ski runners double as decor. Upstairs, Fletcher redefined the boundaries of the primary suite to carve out a nook for a vignette displaying the home’s most bespoke decor piece: a vintage cherry-red chairlift. “The home’s location is unique,” adds Fletcher. “It sits on a ski slope, and you can view chairlifts out the back windows!”

A true gathering space, the living room was designed around the original stone-faced fireplace, though Fletcher did replace the former pine mantel with white oak, connecting the floor details with the exposed oak beams running along the ceiling.

Swiveling burnt orange armchairs accompany an inky indigo sectional. Both gather around a button-tufted ottoman, which doubles as a coffee perch or game table. Shaded sconces flank the fireplace to give the room a glow, even when the hearth is unlit, while a pair of vintage ski shoes hang on the wall, crowning the design.

In the playroom, the deep green hue of the wallpaper paired with the natural texture of knotty pine borrows inspiration from the forest-like landscape just beyond the window. Vertical lines carried through both the wallpaper and the wainscoting create the illusion of height, while playful ski motifs bring the local sport’s culture into the play space.

“The clients are sustainably minded and didn't want to get rid of what was working,” says Fletcher. With conservation in mind, she opted to keep the original perimeter cabinets, refreshing them with paint, new hardware, and Virginia Black Satin Granite countertops, a sturdier alternative to show-stopping soapstone.

To optimize the space, she nixed the two existing islands, reimagining the room to fit a larger iteration that boasts a sink and easily seats five. The design team then added upper cabinets, transforming the space into a storage haven. Later, she placed a library ladder and golden hardware for easy access and a touch of whimsy. Then, she turned her attention to making sure the lighting was up to par. Fletcher firmly believes a beautiful space should be equally as functional. To ensure no detail fell short, she installed discreet recessed lights alongside under-cabinet lighting, and two large glass pendants to adorn the space. All dimmable for ambiance—of course. “There’s nothing worse than a home kitchen that is just flooded with bright light everywhere. A kitchen should still feel cozy and warm, and good lighting is the key to accomplishing that,” says Fletcher.

Fletcher compensated for this former powder room’s lack of windows with vibrant wallpaper to energize the space and make the most of interior lighting. Borrowing space from an adjacent closet, she converted this former two-piece bath into a full retreat with a shower, vanity, and storage, finishing the space with gold hardware for an additional touch of luxury.

Perhaps the most customized room in the home, the mudroom boasts bold color and smart storage solutions for a space as functional as it is chic. The custom cabinet is an ode to smart design, featuring bench seating, pegs, an overhead shelf, and under-seat shoe storage—but the real showstopper is its bold red hue.

“My favorite element of the built-in is the color. The brick color has just the right amount of earthiness,” says Fletcher.

The family loves to entertain, often hosting large groups of friends and family. To accommodate their social lifestyle, Fletcher designed a hospitality hub. Removing a bathroom door, she altered the room’s layout, allowing space for this guest suite to come to life.

With the new dimensions secured, she covered the walls in printed wallpaper inspired by the great local outdoors. The bold pattern set the tone for the rest of the design as she pulled colors, patterns, and themes to complement it.

For the primary bedroom, the theme was warmth: a warm bed, warm home, warm soul, all channeled through muted terracotta walls. But before it could truly transform, the team had to iron out a few oddball details.

“This room used to have a few odd details, including transom windows to the adjoining bathroom and an exterior door to a deck. We removed those elements, which allowed for a better furniture layout and a cozier feeling,” says Fletcher.

The room features a traditional four-poster bed in a nearly black finish, which carries over to the lighting details and furniture hardware, bringing out the warmth and depth of the surrounding rich red hues.

For the primary bath, the team capitalized on the luxury of the sloped-side views, framing a classic 400-pound slipper tub with a nearly floor-to-ceiling window featuring vintage pine trim. Truly the focal point of the design, Fletcher finished the window treatment with woven shades to tie in the natural wood details of the home and stand up to moisture. Natural grain runs through the room’s doors and vanity, topped with soft white Calacatta marble, channeling the classic ski chalet aesthetic. Gold fixtures extend from the vanity to the lighting, towel rack, and tub, for a touch of glamour.

Boasting the project’s biggest splurge, a custom bunk-trundle bed, the kid’s room combines playful hues with forest-inspired details and maximum sleepover potential. “We wanted to maximize sleeping spots in this home,” Fletcher explains. “The Casa Kids bunk bed in the kid's room was a splurge, but it can sleep 5!”

Alden Fletcher first fell in love with design while working as an art teacher and refurbishing furniture in her downtime. In 2020, she grew that passion into a full-service design firm focused on bringing functionality to storied designs and creating ultra-personalized interiors.

#6837. The 6 Best Hair Rollers for Supermodel Volume

Salon-level bounce, no appointment necessary.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

If you've ever been envious of a big, bouncy blowout à la a ’90s supermodel, I have amazing news: Those angelic waves are actually pretty simple to recreate—no salon visit required, promise! Even if you don't have the skills of a professional celebrity stylist (because I definitely don't), you can still achieve tons of volume with the right hair routine and the best hair rollers.

If you’re after va-va-volume, using your thermal round brush, multi-styler, or blow dryer on its own simply won’t cut it. A set of hair rollers is the key to long-lasting volume. As a long-haired beauty editor who loves nothing more than the way my hair looks after a salon blowout, I've tried pretty much every type of hair roller out there to DIY those same coveted waves at home. I also tapped expert hairstylists to share how to properly use these root-lifting tools, below.

Why we love it: These ceramic Velcro rollers are perfect for achieving that bouncy blowout look. They come in three different sizes so you can easily place them around the head, with the smallest being perfect for shorter layers and bangs. You won't have to worry about them falling out of your hair, either, since they're made with a self-grip surface and don't require any clips. Just roll ’em up, and you’re good to go.

Why we love it: Want to keep your hair effortlessly voluminous on the go? (Who doesn’t?!) Mane’s travel-friendly roller set will ensure your mane (heh) stays bouncy, no matter where you are in the world. The barrels on this six-piece set are self-gripping, too, so there’s no need to pack extra clips that are bound to get lost in the bottom of a bag.

Why we love it: If you've got a lot of hair, first, consider yourself blessed, but you might need more than the standard six-to-nine set of rollers. Luckily, this convenient kit comes with 36 (!!!) along with 18 metal clips to lock them in place. There are also long- and short-hair size options, so if you've got a bob, creating the perfect curls and waves will still be a breeze.

Beauty editor Beth Gillette loves the smallest size for her super-fine bixie lengths, “These are so gentle on my hair, even as I pull them out—I can see why the Pheloung sisters have such good hair,” she explains. “But I especially love the range of sizes and how many come in this chic case.”

Why we love it: If you want XXL volume without needing to re-wet your hair, a set of heated rollers is a one-and-done way to get the look of a defined blowout on already-dry hair. Not only do the T3 hot rollers have a dual temperature setting, but they have cool-grip edges, so you won't burn your precious fingers while setting them in place. The set even comes in a sleek case and has an auto-off setting—no need to question if you left them on while you're out at dinner (we’ve all been there).

Why we love it: While most rollers are made of plastic, these are constructed out of metal, which evenly distributes heat and really locks in the volume when you’re blowing out your hair at home. You might think that metal would slip right off your mane, but these have a self-grip exterior that latches onto your hair. (The set does come with clips if you need some extra support, though.) You shouldn’t be surprised if your hair still looks voluminous two or three days after using these.

Why we love it: Sure, you can sleep in hair rollers, but you probably won’t get a good night’s sleep. (I’m speaking from experience.) If you're looking for a comfortable method that will ensure tons of volume and gorgeous waves, look no further than Kitsch's Heatless Curling Set. It's made of soft satin, so there's no pulling or tugging, and it comes with two matching scrunchies to hold your hair perfectly in place overnight. When someone compliments your hair, you can literally say "I woke up like this."

Every hair roller is different, and each hair type will require unique steps. However, it's a good idea to keep these expert-recommended tips in mind if you're looking to achieve a bouncy blowout at home:

"The way your curlers and hair sets is the way that it is going to flow," explains Tippi Shorter, celebrity hairstylist and Mizani global artistic director. "Place the rollers at the crown of the head and roll your hair under the curler; that way, you will get more volume and body."

Joseph Maine, celebrity stylist and artistic director of Color Wow Hair, suggests using a brush and rollers that are on the larger side in order to achieve a smoother bouncy blowout. "The smaller they are, the more curls they're going to leave in your hair."

Use a diffuser attachment on a blow dryer to gently heat the rollers back up after each one has been placed. This will ensure that they are set perfectly. Then, keep the rollers in your hair for about 15 minutes or until they cool down completely.

In short: There’s no one-size-fits-all roller, and each type serves a slightly different purpose. You probably have seen quite a few throwback pics of supermodels getting glammed up with Velcro rollers, but times have changed, and rollers have evolved.

Hot and heatless rollers can be used to create the effortless and bouncy look you're going for—it just depends on the size of the roller and the process you use. And remember, the bigger the roller, the bouncier the hair. That means the smaller the roller, the tighter the curls will be.

Additionally, some rollers are made from ceramic or metal, while others are made of plastic. The ceramic and metal distribute heat better and are able to boost and lock in volume more easily. The plastic rollers are also effective, but their effects don’t last as long.

It depends on the type of roller you use and what your desired final look is. For example, if you’re hoping to create heatless curls, stick with satin or plastic rollers and sleep with them overnight for defined ringlets come morning. All rollers can boost volume, and if you want to restore bounce, they should be used on dry hair to lift your roots.

"Most people need to have extra moisture in the hair in order to get a hair roller style to stay," says Maine. To ensure that your hair is properly hydrated, apply blowout mousse, spray, or leave-in conditioner before styling.

Jasmine Hyman is the assistant beauty editor at Cosmopolitan who writes about everything from the best hair products and tools to all the biggest celebrity hair trends. She's tested dozens of hair rollers in her years as a beauty expert and narrowed it down to the best five based on ease of use, price, results, and more.

#6836. 5 Fall Trends Designers Think Will Still Look Good Next Spring

Above: A hallway view of Eric Egan’s Milan apartment.

If any season has the power to inspire a complete aesthetic overhaul, it’s fall. Nature’s changing palette inspires us to follow suit, as each year our mood boards make new space for jewel tones, cozy textures, and a bottomless candle budget. Autumnal trends are so alluring, it almost feels like a risky time to make long-term design decisions. But with the right consideration, that seasonal inspiration can translate into choices that look good year-round.

This fall in particular, designers are urging clients to trust their guts—making sure they see themselves in the spaces they create, rather than blindly following the latest fads. “For me, a trend has to be authentic or it’s just noise,” says designer Chad Dorsey, who’s eager to ditch the spare minimalism of years past for looks with more personality. ELLE Decor A-Lister Kerry Joyce seconds the motion: “By prioritizing personalized storytelling, a space becomes evergreen, evolving as smaller objects are swapped out over time.”

But an expert nudge in the right direction never hurts. This fall, top designers are gravitating toward creative color-drenching, cleverly concealed storage, tactile upholstery, and rooms built around art. Read on for their takes on the fall 2026 trends worth investing in, plus tips on building (as Dorsey puts it) “spaces designed to be lived in, not looked at.”

Fabric and upholstery are trending ultra plush and textured for fall, but when thoughtfully integrated, they feel more timeless than seasonal. “Design is shifting toward how a space feels, not just how it looks,” says Joyce, who’s seeing an increased popularity in fabrics like mohair, velvet, cashmere, and alpaca hair. “This focus on texture does more than elevate a room's aesthetic,” he explains. “It creates a sensory experience that comforts the soul.”

Start your bedroom redesign this fall and your space will strike the perfect balance between fresh and lived-in by spring. Between client requests and a personal home build, Amber Lewis has bedrooms on the brain right now. One trend takes the lead in her eyes: “I love matching the walls to the bed upholstery and bedding so the whole room feels wrapped in one warm color family. It’s calm and peaceful, and that enveloping feeling works in any season.”

Luckily, this cozy trend doesn’t require a complete room overhaul: “The easiest place to start is the bed itself. Reupholster your headboard in a pattern you love, or drape a vintage suzani across the foot of the bed,” Lewis says. “One move like that changes the whole mood of the room without starting over.” To bring in a little dimension and pop, she recommends adding a singular statement, like a lone striped pillow.

If your primary suite needs a more holistic update, Lewis recommends creating a small sitting area. Sourcing vintage chairs and small sofas for primary suites this fall has given Lewis a new perspective on where bedroom design is headed: “True sanctuaries that feel layered, personal, and built for actually living in.”

Color drenching isn’t going anywhere for fall 2026—or long after, according to Dorsey. When choosing a shade for a room, he finds that any color works harder when it’s layered and intentional. Color capping—a term coined by Benjamin Moore referring to painting a room in multiple tones from one color family—can help keep a space from feeling flat or overwhelmed beneath a statement hue.

According to Dorsey, the staying power of color drenching goes hand in hand with the shift away from spareness and toward “collected” spaces: “Neither is really a ‘look;’ they’re about living with things you love, which is why they’ll carry well into spring.”

Designer Regan Baker says that cleverly hidden space solutions are also on the rise. “It could be a TV that slides behind a commissioned art panel, a Murphy bed tucked into a wall of millwork or that serves as a desk when it's stored, or a peg wall that’s a place to hang a jacket one minute and a standing desk the next,” she says. These solutions are tailored to her clients’ spaces (rather than tied to a season or palette), which gives them staying power.

This concept is an easy way to turn the corner you’ve never known how to style into your favorite part of the room. “In an existing home, look at the awkward spots, like an alcove or the wall around a fireplace, and treat built-ins there as a design moment instead of an afterthought,” Baker advises.

If there’s one built-to-last fall trend most designers can agree on, it’s added eccentricity. “Homes are steering away from the sterile, overnight look of a showroom delivery,” Joyce says. “The defining philosophy now focuses on layering modern staples with antiques, secondhand finds, travel mementos, and new pieces that carry a sense of patina.”

Dorsey describes this style as “collected rather than prescribed,” where a high-low mix of vintage and custom elements gel together. And while it may feel loftier than a trending, sold-out piece, Baker says that most clients start with an artistic anchor, then build around it: “We’re designing more homes around a piece of art than around a color trend. That might be wallpaper by local designers, a commissioned piece, or even a palette pulled from a lichen-covered rock found on the property.”

One key takeaway? Curate like a collector. Your reward may be a space that never goes out of style.

Grace McCarty is a freelance Associate Digital Editor at ELLE Decor, where she covers design trends, shopping, and culture. She previously held a staff position at SELF Magazine, where she focused on beauty, style, and wellness. Her work has also appeared in Glamour and Parade.

#6835. Under the Surface

Canali gives a behind-the-scenes look at sartorial excellence: the time, precision and mastery that transform raw materials into a jacket made to last

Under the surface, unseen layers lie. They stay there, silent. They don’t demand the spotlight. They don’t seek glory. And yet, like a beating heart, they form the innermost foundation of any real achievement. Where passion, enthusiasm, and discipline meet, the logic of excellence takes shape. Such is the story of Under the Surface, the newest creative collaboration from Canali.

Under the Surface is a look at the unseen architecture of a sartorial jacket by Canali. Every Canali jacket is born from a complex system of about 200 steps, each executed with meticulous care. Around 15 specialists are involved in the process—one that sees more than 520 feet of thread sewn into 22,000 stitches, both machine- and hand-made. Unseen when looking at the finished garment, the process informs each piece’s quality, comfort, shape, and durability.

It’s not just a matter of talent: rather, it’s about staying faithful to one’s goals, pursuing them with dedication even when no one is watching. It’s then that quality truly makes a difference, and the end result becomes the visible manifestation of a long creation process that demands great effort, experience and willpower.

That commitment of excellence unites the sartorial world of Canali and Ethan Hawke, the brand’s new Global Ambassador. The actor, writer, and director has dedicated almost four decades to perfecting his art, with endless, ever-learning curiosity. For their new Fall/Winter 2026 campaign, Canali has decided to capture what goes on behind the scenes. In the film, Hawke pores over his scripts, rehearses lines, refines each motion: a painstaking introspective process made of research, study, and exercise, preceding each of his performances.

The behind-the-scenes of a Canali jacket starts with selecting the finest fabrics, sourced from the best Italian wool mills. Then, each textile is examined and interpreted. While technology now allows extreme precision and control, some assessments are still left to the eye and expertise of human specialists. Even tiny details such as slightly different shades of color or variations in texture can influence the final product.

The fabric is then stabilized via steaming cycles and specific treatments, to ensure its behavior remains consistent over time. Then it’s time for digital mapping, guiding every cutting room decision: here, each of the jacket’s pieces is carefully positioned to also help keep waste to a minimum. It’s the first irreversible step.

The garment’s three-dimensional structure is then shaped upon the canvas. Canali crafts theirs in-house, adapting it to each model’s fit and fabrics. Made from horsehair, plant fibers, and natural reinforcements, the canvas is modeled on custom-made concave shapes, known as Schiena d’Asino (Donkey’s Back), which replicate the chest’s anatomy. The canvas is crucial for determining how a jacket drapes over the body, avoiding stiffness. It’s here that the volume of the chest, the balance of the silhouette and ease of wearing begin to be defined.

Then it’s time to craft the front, lapel, padded shoulder, and the sleeves. From planning to execution, nothing’s left to chance. The tailor observes, corrects, iterates. It’s a patient process, requiring time, knowledge, and skill. Lastly, the various layers are united through basting, stitching, and ironing processes. As these details interact, the garment takes on its structure and sophisticated character.

Something special also takes place behind the scenes with Hawke: day after day, the actor’s preparation subtly transforms his thoughts and behavior until, almost by instinct, he takes on each role’s intimate characteristics.

Time underpins the whole process. After being assembled and pressed, the jacket is allowed to rest, to dissipate the steam and stabilize the cloth. This is how the fabric finds its balance, and the garment gains a sort of memory: the ability to naturally find its shape again, even after having been worn, folded, or packed into a suitcase.

In over 90 years of heritage, Canali has refined their techniques through repetition, experience, and experimentation. Each garment is built upon the time-tested values of care, know-how, precision. The Canali style is not one made of grand gestures, but daily practice, intimate and subtle: the constant research into a shape that lasts. One hero of the Under the Surface project is the vicuña jacket, available exclusively at selected flagship boutiques by Canali.The campaign, and the collaboration between Canali and Ethan Hawke, speak to the art of shaping oneself, guided by an ambition to do things well with patience and self-awareness. Both in tailoring and in cinema, there is so much quiet work that happens behind the scenes to make creativity possible. Discipline and rigour do not constrain creative freedom; they make it possible. For Canali, excellence takes shape through the craftsmanship with which each element is made. For Ethan Hawke, it is the preparation that allows him to approach each role with confidence.And, as the Under The Surface campaign reveals, each of these steps adds something essential to the finished product. In the case of the Canali cashmere and vicuña blazer (the hero style of the FW26 campaign), it is structure, movement, character, durability, and a lightness that can only be achieved through the combination of expertly-chosen materials and complex fine tailoring. The knowledge required to execute this is almost tangible, felt in the quality of the piece.

The finished result only has value because of the strong, unseen structure underneath. It is a story we can all resonate with. Little by little, layer by layer, day by day, we each build ourselves in pursuit of excellence. Our actions create the people we are – even, or especially, when nobody is watching – in the same way that the apparent simplicity of a piece of clothing hides the depth of work required to craft it. This is the essence of Under the Surface.

#6833. 9 Curb Appeal Trends That Will Take Over in 2027, According to Designers

These are the exterior elements that are getting a major refresh.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Curb appeal is more than having a pretty house.

“It's not just about how a home looks, but how it lives,” says Danielle Faustini, landscape designer and owner of Helix Green Designs. Homeowners and designers alike are stepping up their game when it comes to creating an appealing facade—in some cases, the shift is quite literal, as stairs are becoming an “it” feature.

The latest U.S. Houzz Outdoor Trends study is seeing a shift toward natural elements like wood cladding, beige exteriors, and native plants.

"Professionals on Houzz have shared that homeowners are looking for exteriors that feel grounded and connected to their surroundings," says Annie Thornton, Houzz outdoor trends expert. “Warm beiges and wood siding blend naturally with stone, native plantings and other organic landscape materials, and they suit a wide range of architectural styles. Our studies show that people are moving away from the stark white and cool gray palettes of recent years toward something that feels more timeless, a look that adds a sense of history without tipping into nostalgia.”

Simple upgrades, like a fresh coat of paint on the front door, are also pivoting into colorways anchored in the past. Designer Alexis Warren is seeing a shift toward darker, moodier front doors in shades of green, deep purple, and near-black, like Farrow & Ball’s Paean Black.

Here are 10 curb-appeal-boosting design trends for the year ahead to make your house the envy of the neighborhood.

Introducing a sculptural element—like an outdoor artwork or creatively placed flora—sends a message that your house has a point of view.

“It elevates the sense of arrival and separates your home from other homes in the vicinity,” says designer Lisa Erdmann. To ensure your home truly stands out, look for one-of-a-kind pieces.

For the home pictured above, Robert J. Bell, founder of landscape architecture firm Bell Design, commissioned artisans to create a copper light inspired by a feather. “More than the end of minimalism, the new focus on sculptural shapes brings back a sense of craft, and the expressive and sensory nature that a simple black metal and glass fixture cannot possess,” he says.

To contrast against the patina, Bell chose purple bougainvillea and a sunny yellow paint on the door.

It’s all about adding warmth and texture, and that’s one of the reasons that beige exterior colors have more than doubled in popularity in the last two years, jumping from 10 to 24 percent, according to Houzz.

Think of a palette that extends into the landscape—warm neutrals such as tan, soft cream, khaki, sand, and greige, says Thornton, who also notes that natural wood and wood-look cladding, sandy stucco, and stone are a welcome change.

Adding paneling can mimic the warmth of wood without worrying about upkeep, points out Francisca Alonso, cofounder and CEO of AV Architects + Builders. This trick also helps soften the front elevation. “We like to combine minimalistic modern details with warm tones to make it more inviting and less commercial-looking,” Alonso adds.

A picket fence may be just what your house needs, but that doesn’t mean you have to resort to the predictable white paint.

“Adding a low-profile picket fence is one of the best ways to gently delineate a property line without in any way compromising sightlines of the home,” says Joelle Nesen, principal designer at Maison Inc. “The picket fence has the kind of classical lines that work well with an historic home like this Craftsman in Portland. By not defaulting to the expected white and leaving the fence a natural wood, the picket fence, with its carefully spaced slats that are almost see-through, allows it to blend in with the landscaping and trees that surround the home.”

When you choose gravel over grass or pavers, it’s all about an understated but classy look.

“Gravel and other permeable aggregates like crushed shells are some of my favorite materials for creating more informal outdoor living spaces,” says Faustini. “It is a beautifully tactile and sensory material. Who doesn't love the soft crunch underfoot? And it really softens a space, helping blur the line between outdoor living areas and the surrounding landscape.”

The look works especially well with decking and patinated light fixtures.

Adding native plants around your house feels cohesive, relaxed, and rooted in place, Thornton says.

Rachael Grochowski, founder and principal of RHG Architecture + Design, adds that she expects to see an increase in native landscapes, meadow-type gardens, as well as an integration of clover into lawns.

“The trend to embrace natural landscapes is not only about supporting environment regeneration, but it also supports our wellbeing by instigating wonder, beauty, and randomness,” she says.

Curb appeal is all about first impressions, and homeowners are not taking this feature lightly. Houzz reports that searches for “front walkway” have more than quadrupled (up 357 percent) in the first three quarters of 2026, compared with the same period last year.

Step design, in particular, is one feature that is growing in popularity.

When Sarah Crawford, associate designer with Courtney B. Smith Design, renovated her home, she added steps to her 1920s bungalow to literally and figuratively elevate the experience. The home initially had just one step from the sidewalk to the front door. By adding a series of stairs to gently guide the way to the door, she added a quiet sense of elegance in keeping with the wood-shingled exterior. Key to the transformation was selecting materials that reflect the character and style of the original home, in this case reclaimed granite. Scale and balance were essential to adding a series of stairs to gently guide visitors to the door.

“Steps should never be narrower than the doorway,” Crawford says. “In fact, the wider the steps, the more inviting the approach.”

For a different approach to the front entrance, consider stone pathways, especially those with a casual look, like grass pavers.

Interior designer Darci Hether likes large-format bluestone pavers, which offer a clean, timeless look. “The generous scale of the stones creates a sense of openness, while the greenery between them softens the overall appearance and adds a beautiful, natural element,” she explains.

Erdmann encourages her clients to forgo safe neutrals and treat the front entry door as a place for personality.

“Whether it is hot pink, lipstick red, cobalt blue, or Benjamin Moore’s color of the year, Webster Green—make a statement!” she says. “Don’t match the door to the shutters and choose something fun. A front door is the easiest, lowest-risk place to experiment since it takes one afternoon and a quart of paint.”

Case in point, Elizabeth Cross-Beard, interior designer behind Palm Henri, took to a vibrant peacock blue to accentuate the lines of the above 1925 French manor house. “When paired with natural materials and muted tones, [the paint] brings just enough color to intrigue the eye without disrupting the overall sense of classic grandeur. Unlike other blue tones which can call too much attention to them, this rich color is the front-door sweet spot,” Cross-Beard explains.

Go beyond ho-hum pots that stand sentry at your door. Look for large pieces with personality to anchor the entrance.

“Aged terracotta, weathered stone, or antique urns bring patina and character, helping even a newly built home feel established,” says MK Smith Boykin of Smith Interiors. “With that kind of presence, the planting itself can be quite simple.” The details are especially important when your home has architectural symmetry, like columns.

“Using matching plants only increases the allure of perfect visual balance,” Nesen adds. “The key is that the scale of the planters needs to match the proportions of the door and porch: Selecting pots or containers that are too small will immediately drain the drama and beauty of the front door statement.”

#6832. Kim Kardashian’s Beverly Hills Marital Villa Lists for $8.5 Million—Plus 4 More Luxury Homes for Sale

This week’s standout luxury listings include the Notting Hill blue-door townhouse, Diana Jenkins’s Hidden Hills mansion, and a Connecticut estate by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé.

Even if you keep up with the Kardashians, you might not recognize this Beverly Hills house, which was one of Kim Kardashian’s first real estate purchases. The reality star bought the gated home 16 years ago for $3.4 million, and it served as the setting when basketball player Kris Humphries proposed to her in 2011. But the residence has gotten a major overhaul in the years since Kardashian sold it following the short-lived marriage, and now the Tuscan-style villa is on the market for nearly $8.5 million.

Other listings on our radar this week include another reality TV–linked estate: A former Real Housewife of Beverly Hills is parting with her 13,200-square-foot Hidden Hills house. On the East Coast, a striking Connecticut home by a Frank Lloyd Wright protégé is also on the market. And across the pond, the memorable blue-door townhouse from ‘90s rom-com Notting Hill in London is also for sale. Check them all out below.

One of Kim Kardashian’s earliest real estate purchases was this gated property in Beverly Hills, which she acquired in 2010 for $3.4 million. The Tuscan-style villa quickly became a familiar backdrop on her family’s E! reality show, Keeping Up with the Kardashians, and was even the site of Kris Humphries’s (televised) 2011 proposal to her. In the aftermath of their 72-day marriage, Kardashian sold the half-acre estate, and its new owners tapped fast-rising designer Sebastian Zuchowicki to oversee a complete overhaul. Now on the market for just under $8.5 million with Jack Harris and Michael Fahimian of Beverly Hills Estates, the five-bedroom home has a double-height entry foyer, custom kitchen cabinetry, and a primary suite with a fireplace, two walk-in closets, and a soaking tub.

Entrepreneur Diana Jenkins spent much of her time on The Real Housewives of Beverly Hills nesting in her sprawling Hidden Hills mansion with her newborn daughter, Eliyanah. Now, four years since leaving the Bravo reality series, the native Bosnian is returning to her home country, per Realtor.com—and listing the 13,200-square-foot mansion for $23 million with Sandeep Dhariwal of Sotheby’s International Realty—Beverly Hills Brokerage. Set on 4.4 acres, the seven-bedroom house was built in 2019 and comes with a plethora of perks, among them an infinity-edge pool, barbecue, fitness studio, and home cinema.

It’s perhaps the most famous house in one of London’s most charming neighborhoods, so beloved that crowds are known to gather around it like the English version of Carrie Bradshaw’s West Village apartment. The blue-doored townhouse where Hugh Grant’s fictional bookseller lived and wooed Julia Roberts’s paparazzi-plagued Hollywood actress in 1999’s Notting Hill is now on the market. And the 2,750-square-foot house can be yours for roughly $10 million.

With its organic silhouette, cantilevered roofline, and clever use of space, Leuthold House by Allan Gelbin bears the hallmarks of his mentor, legendary architect Frank Lloyd Wright. Completed in 1966 on five rolling acres in New Canaan, the five-bedroom house spans roughly 5,700 square feet and underwent a restoration by Hobbs Inc. and JMK Architects in 2014. The interiors feature long expanses of glass and mahogany, while the grounds come with a spring-fed pond, a pool, a one-bedroom guest house, and 150 specimen trees. Listed for $6.2 million with John Engel and Melissa Engel of Douglas Elliman, the two-story house is on the market for the first time in 29 years.

One of the art world’s most famous couples once lived and worked in a gated townhouse a block from Washington Square Park. Jackson Pollock and Lee Krasner didn’t own the MacDougal Alley home—a brick-faced carriage house built in the 1870s—but rather stayed there in the winter of 1950 due to the generosity of its owner, a fellow celebrated artist and their friend Alfonso Ossorio. Now, Ossorio’s 1,500-square-foot residence is on the market for the first time since 1988, per the New York Post. The asking price for the two-bedroom, two-bath home is nearly $8 million.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6828. Is Your Home Too Matchy-Matchy? These 5 Designer Tricks Break Up the Blah and Add Instant Character

If your bedroom design began with a furniture set that matches your nightstands and lamps, it may be time to shake things up. Having a space where everything coordinates perfectly may seem like the easiest route to a stylish home, but many times, it can feel more sterile than a true reflection of who you are and what you love.

“A home often looks sterile when everything comes from the same set,” explains Preeti Shah, founder and principal designer at Spotlight Style.

By stepping out of your comfort zone and switching things up a bit, you can create a cohesive look that still brings character and depth to your home. Here are five designer tips to steer you in the right direction.

Just because one of your surfaces is natural white oak doesn't mean everything in the same room needs to follow suit.

“We love mixing dark wood tones with light tones and everything in between,” says Leah Hook, founder and principal designer at Gray Oak Studio.

Repeating wood tones can make a room feel cohesive, but you don't need to use the same tone everywhere. Think outside the box and look for subtle ways to tie the space together, such as repeating a wood tone in an art frame or decorative tray.

Believe it or not, your island does not have to match your kitchen countertops in every possible way.

“Choose a more exciting stone for your island. You can even miter it two inches thick instead of keeping everything the standard 1.25-inch thickness,” explains Gaelle Dudley, founder and creative director at GLDESIGN.

Dining chairs are almost always purchased as a matching set, but sourcing different styles from flea markets or Facebook Marketplace can give your dining room a fun, unexpected look.

“Select just one element from the table that will be repeated in the chairs, and go with something from a different collection or even an entirely different furniture line,” says Pattie Kelly, certified interior decorator and owner of Inspired Home Interiors.

Consider the height of the chairs (so that they still make sense together at the table) and how much room you have to pull them out in (so you’re not running into the wall behind them).

The most interesting homes display a mix of different art styles. Instead of sticking to one color palette or aesthetic, seek out pieces that contrast. For instance, Shah is a fan of contrasting graphic black-and-white drawings with gold-veined abstract canvases in various rooms of the home.

Speaking of artwork, as the space pictured above by Nina Garbiras illustrates, don’t be afraid to mix and match your frame materials. Just keep one element consistent, like the mat size or the frame width, so that it still has a curated (not chaotic) feel.

Just because you’re choosing one type of fixture finish doesn’t mean you have to repeat it everywhere. In fact, your space will look more “expensive” if you match shiny finishes very minimally.

For example, the above space by Vestige Home showcases how well brass and matte-black hardware can play off each other, adding depth, harmony, and visual interest. Choosing to go with one finish throughout this space would’ve become too matchy-matchy very quickly.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.
