#6824. I Wear-Tested En Route’s Viral, Under-$50 Jewelry—Now I Get Why My Favorite Celebs Love It

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Shop the pieces worn by Bella Hadid, Lola Tung, and Doechii.

What do Bella Hadid, Doechii, Camila Cabello, and Lola Tung all have in common? They’re all known for killing it on the carpet, yes—but once the cameras went down, I took notice that they’ve each been wearing the same under-$100 jewelry brand.

It might surprise you that some of the biggest stars in entertainment are wearing budget-friendly baubles. But after adding a few choice accessories from the brand to my own collection, I have to admit: En Route has some of the trendiest, most compliment-garnering jewelry the internet has to offer. (And, let’s face it: it doesn’t hurt that it costs less than the price of my favorite takeout.)

Ahead, I’m giving my honest review on En Route’s most popular pieces after months of wear-testing in my everyday jewelry rotation. Keep reading for the rundown on this viral, celebrity-loved brand—including the affordable pieces I think are worth it.

As an editor who’s been covering jewelry trends for years, I’ll admit that I was a bit skeptical when I saw the price tag. In my experience, $30 jewelry either (A) turns your skin a sickly shade of green, or (B) tarnishes to an unbecoming rust color in a matter of weeks. That’s why I wanted to put En Route’s pieces to the test. I wore them to work, regularly washed my hands with them, kept them on for sweaty outdoor runs while marathon training, and even accidentally showered in them a time or two. (Ya know, the normal wear and tear that your jewelry is going to experience throughout its lifetime.)

The brand claims its jewelry is hypoallergenic, meaning that it’s lead- and nickel-free and should be safe for shoppers with sensitive skin. Its gold-plated jewelry is also three times thicker than industry standard, according to its FAQ section, which means it’s designed to last longer than competing budget brands—a claim I was excited to put to the test.

Without spoiling my full review, here’s the TL;DR: if you want to shop trendy, unique pieces that show off your personal style, you should be running to add En Route to your collection.

Before we dive in, it’s worth noting that all of En Route’s jewelry is gold-plated—not solid gold. What does this mean? The base of the jewelry is made from an inexpensive metal, like brass or stainless steel, and then a very thin layer of gold is electroplated on top. This gives the look of real gold without the price tag.

As you might expect, gold-plated jewelry isn’t nearly as long-lasting as solid gold and silver; the thin layer will eventually rub off, particularly on jewelry like rings or bracelets that receive a lot of wear and tear. In my experience, standard gold-plated jewelry will begin fading or tarnishing within one to three years.

To ensure the longevity of your jewelry, En Route recommends avoiding water and chemicals like makeup or perfume (though showering every once in a while is A-okay)—and they even have an entire section of their site for waterproof jewelry that’s safe to get wet.

I’m not exaggerating when I say that I receive at least one compliment every time I wear one of my necklaces from En Route. Over the summer, I found myself reaching for the Infinity Choker almost every day, since its colorful beads glimmer in the sunshine and look great with a simple white tank top or sundress. These days, I’m spicing up turtlenecks and basic tees with this vintage-inspired collar necklace that looks like a family heirloom but costs less than $40.

After several months of frequent wear, both of my picks have held up without so much as a smudge. Just note that neither is advertised as being waterproof, so I’d definitely avoid wearing them in the shower or during a workout to keep them looking shiny and new for as long as possible.

Upon first glance, I was immediately impressed by how unique En Route’s ring selection is—and after testing them for a few months, I’ve become even more of a fan. The selection is basically perfect if you’re looking to shop personality-driven pieces that express your unique style. Take Bella Hadid, for example. The star was spotted doubling up with the Wonder Pearl ring on multiple fingers, showing off the chunky sculptural bezel and a pretty faux pearl inside. Or, consider Lola Tung’s stack of hammered silver rings—a master class in simple yet effective ring stacking.

I, on the other hand, found myself enamored by the Mojave Buckle ring. It runs a little bit small, but I love the subtle Western flair it adds to any look. The brand recommends keeping its rings out of water as much as possible, but even with regular hand-washing, I haven’t noticed any tarnishing or fading so far.

As someone with multiple piercings, I’m here to tell you that you can never have too many earrings. The greatest perk I found while testing En Route’s earring selection is just how lightweight they are—which is essential if you want to be comfortable all day and avoid stretching out your piercings. Despite sturdy-looking shapes and ornate details, the earrings I tested were feather-light and virtually unnoticeable all day long.

Possibly my favorite jewelry piece I tested from the brand were these Star earrings. They garnered multiple compliments from friends and strangers alike, and people were shocked when I told them they cost just $22. What’s more, the stainless steel material doesn’t irritate my ears and this pair just so happens to be waterproof.

Bella Hadid wearing the Silent Drop earrings in gold.

Lola Tung wearing the Clio earrings in silver.

My only word of caution: while the earrings look expensive, their lightweight construction makes them feel a little cheap in your hands. This isn’t a dealbreaker for me since they’ve held up flawlessly so far, but don’t expect them to have the same heft and quality of your pricey solid gold studs.

I’m not a big bracelet person. There’s something about the feeling of jewelry sliding and clanking around on my wrists that really bothers me—so it’s a rare occasion when I decide to put one on. But for the sake of this review, I tested the Athena bracelet and the Harmonic Hour watch, and honestly? I kinda loved them.

While I’m still not a fan of the clinking feeling, I loved the colored gemstone details that glittered in direct light and added a cool-girl flair with virtually zero effort. The lightweight construction makes them almost unnoticeable throughout the day, so I didn’t notice them as much as hefty bangles. Bonus points for the fact that they’re pretty easy to put on and take off by yourself, since they have simple clasps and T-bar closures. There’s even body jewelry and hand chains for more ways to personalize your jewelry stack.

After several months of constant wear-testing, I’m convinced: En Route is one of the best affordable jewelry brands on the market if you’re looking for cute, trendy, and personality-driven pieces. The wide selection of options—ranging from chunky statement pieces to wearable basics in both gold and silver—makes it super easy to express your personal style without dropping hundreds of dollars.

For shoppers with sensitive skin, I love that everything is hypoallergenic, according to the brand, so you don’t have to worry as much about how your skin will react to your new affordable jewelry. And while not everything is designed to hold up in water, I love that there’s a dedicated section on the website for shopping waterproof pieces. Even the rings, earrings, and necklaces I tested held up well with occasional splashes and frequent hand-washing.

En Route’s overall quality for the price is one of the best I’ve seen. That said, if you’re looking for investment-worthy jewelry you can wear literally 24/7—as in, at the gym, in the shower, or to sleep—I’d recommend investing in a longer-lasting (albeit more expensive) premium metal option. Nearly everything onsite is gold- or rhodium-plated, so they’ll likely start tarnishing within a couple of years, unlike pricier vermeil or solid gold and silver pieces.

Samantha Jones is a senior shopping editor at Hearst Magazines, where she specializes in style, wellness, and home content. She has more than 8 years of editorial experience, with bylines across ELLE, Cosmopolitan, Esquire, Good Housekeeping, Better Homes & Gardens, PEOPLE, Real Simple, and more. At the office, Samantha can often be found reporting on emerging style trends, curating the perfect 5-minute makeup routine, or wear-testing the latest running shoe launches for a story. She’s also an avid runner, repeat marathoner, and former Division I athlete. (In fact, when she’s not at her desk, she’s probably out for a run.)

#6822. Inside a Modern Hamptons Home With Views in Nearly Every Direction

Leroy Street Studio designed this Water Mill retreat around a large blended family—and a landscape that changes with every season.

It’s hard to pick a favorite time of year at Peninsula House. Nicknamed for its location on a point overlooking Mecox Bay in Water Mill, New York, the residence was designed by the architecture firm Leroy Street Studio to capitalize on the site’s 270-degree views. An array of indoor and outdoor vantage points capture the ever-changing cinematic interplay of light and water as well as nature’s shifts from season to season.

The owners, a couple with a large blended family, wanted a multigenerational gathering spot that they and their young daughter could share with the husband’s adult children and their spouses as well as friends. From the outset, the clients made it clear they were looking for out-of-the-ordinary architecture that is thoughtfully, creatively integrated into its unique setting. “They really liked the idea of doing something that was countercurrent for the Hamptons,” says Morgan Hare, the Leroy Street founding partner who headed the project.

Adopting a pinwheel-type plan, the striking 14,000-square-foot house features multiple wings radiating from a central core. These spaces vary in scale and height, and some feature deep overhangs, providing shade and amplifying the design’s dynamic horizontality. “The oversailing roofs create a solar brim that helps keep the rooms cool in the summer,” says Alex Witko, a senior associate at Leroy Street who served as the project lead. “They also amplify the views, including from the primary bedroom, where you can just sit and watch the herons on the nearby spit of land that is a protected wildlife preserve.”

Softening the formal rigor, most of the house is topped with lushly planted green roofs and terraces that offer distinct vistas, while the extensively glazed exterior is clad in handmade white brick on the lower levels and dark-stained wood above. The black and white palette is one that Leroy Street, which has designed dozens of waterfront homes in the Hamptons and elsewhere, has rarely used previously. “The dark wood, somewhat counterintuitively, blends into and dematerializes against the vegetation,” Hare says, noting how the house opens on all sides to tree-shaded terraces and courtyards.

Landscape architect Ed Hollander and his firm Hollander Design collaborated on the 1.7-acre property’s outdoor living spaces, while outfitting the gardens with what he calls “maritime habitat” plantings, a mix of native and non-native species “that are adapted to this sunny, salty, breezy environment.”

Everything in the house’s design was conceived around creating a seamless flow—visual and literal—between inside and outside. The main entry consists of a two-story glass-walled gallery that connects the two largest wings, providing views from the front walkway through the house to a rear garden planted with ginkgo trees and the bay beyond. “In the morning, the sun actually comes up through that ginkgo grove and reflects off the entry hall’s glass pendant lights,” notes Hollander. “Then in the evening, the lights become like starlight, these magic things that dance against the trees.”

Inside, the light-and-dark palette continues with the white artisanal brick, expanses of warm plaster, and pale oak and stone offset by inky-hued wood and blackened-steel accents. Throughout, the predominantly new and custom furnishings, overseen by Eleanor Donnelly of Stelle Lomont Rouhani Architects, stick to a sculptural, pared-down aesthetic, complemented by a contemporary art collection that includes works by Teresita Fernández, Sarah Morris, and Adam McEwen. Elevated details like bespoke integrated door pulls and closets with facings in faux-shagreen or mottled resin add subtle character.

The horseshoe-shaped main stairwell is one of few rounded architectural elements in the house. With floating treads and a minimalist oculus at the top, there’s a play of light, shadow, and form that shifts as one moves between floors.

Reaching the home’s dramatic second-story rooftop terrace requires taking a separate, private flight of stairs. A seductive spot for sunsets and stargazing, it features a seating area with a fire bowl surrounded by wispy native grasses and plants.

While a major appeal of this house is all of its intimate spaces for quiet escape, equally important was having versatile areas for gathering in larger groups. That might mean bringing two dozen guests together around the formal dining tables that can be combined and separated as needed or congregating in the various outdoor spaces around the expansive pool area.

Step Inside This Waterfront Hamptons Home

Hare notes that while the firm has designed plenty of residences for extended families, they have typically taken the form of compounds with separate structures. “This is the first one we've done that’s multigenerational and all in one building,” he says. “It was a big part of our mission—and, ultimately, the success of the house.”

ELLE Decor features director Stephen Wallis has spent three decades covering art, design, and culture. He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. His writing has appeared in numerous publications, including The New York Times, T magazine, WSJ magazine, Esquire, Town & Country, Veranda, and Cultured. He is the author of Fontainebleau, a history of the iconic Miami Beach hotel, and Jim Thompson: Beyond Silk, Beyond Thailand.

#6821. The Quiet Rise of DeMellier Handbags

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Why royals and fashion editors alike love this brand

The Margaux, the Birkin, the Baguette: All are familiar bag styles associated with major fashion houses. Very few handbag-first brands can break through and compete. Enter DeMellier, a British label favored by royals, street-style stars, and fashion editors alike for its understated designs that are big on functionality and eminently reasonable in price.

While many luxury labels are raising prices on handbags, DeMellier’s range hits that sweet spot. Most bag styles ring in between $500 and $600 and capping off well under $1,000. “We believe luxury should be priced with integrity,” says Mireia Llusia-Lindh, creative director and founder of DeMellier London. “This means offering luxury quality and ethical manufacturing without inflated prices by having lower, more reasonable margins than traditional luxury houses.”

Before we hit peak quiet luxury, DeMellier built its foundation on that timeless, minimalist look years ago. (Llusia-Lindh launched Milli Millu in 2010 and rebranded as DeMellier in 2017.) “It’s a philosophy we’ve held since the beginning,” says Llusia-Lindh. “I have always believed in creating pieces that will stand the test of time, avoiding large logos or unnecessary embellishments. For us, luxury is about creating something personal, meaningful, and enduring.”

DeMellier’s New York tote has a minimal, architectural silhouette and is one of the most popular styles from the brand. It’s a bit reminiscent of Celine’s Trapeze, and for Phoebe Philo fans who are nostalgic for the designer’s past artful designs for the label, it fills that void.

The New York tote, which comes in two sizes and different hues and textures, is popular among many fashion editors, including those at Bazaar. “DeMellier has been able to understand that great style and substance are more valuable than trends and fashion. The bags are so chic and provide luxury at a great price point,” says Harper’s Bazaar’s accessories director, Miguel Enamorado.

Plus, the bag can haul a lot: a laptop, a notebook, beauty essentials, headphones, and more. As a New Yorker, I can vouch that it’s aptly named, though it suits any harried commuter’s lifestyle. The long straps are critical when carrying it over a thick jacket or coat. It’s lightweight from the start, so it’s not a drag when filled to the brim. It often prompts compliments and inquiries from friends, curiously asking, “What is that bag?” For me, it instills a sense of confidence and assurance when carrying it.

Earlier this year, DeMellier launched a new counterpart to the New York, called the Brooklyn, which features a similar shape but is equipped with a few design changes, like an interior zipper and a foldover buckle flap.

And for fall, DeMellier introduced new silhouettes like the Sloane (in celebration of its first store opening in London’s Sloane Square) and the Icons 26 collection, a limited-edition capsule where five stylish tastemakers, from Taylor Hill to Romee Strijd, reimagined some of the brand’s signature styles.

DeMellier has a royal fan club too, including Kate Middleton, Meghan Markle, Pippa Middleton, and Princess Beatrice. The Princess of Wales has been spotted carrying the Nano Montreal, a micro toffee-colored bag, on more than one occasion, as well as the small Hudson in mocha suede.

The brand has struck a chord with women searching for elegant, professional handbags that can accompany them during whatever life may throw at them in a given day—an important meeting, a parent-teacher conference, a gym class, a rush to make a bus home, a coffee with friends. Llusia-Lindh emphasizes versatility, noting that she aims for her designs to “transition effortlessly between work, travel, and leisure.” Each bag is named after a metropolitan city, like New York, Tokyo, and Paris, or a more vacation-worthy spot like Capri and Santorini (where you’ll find a range of raffia totes and resort-friendly carryalls).

“DeMellier has demonstrated that they have a true understanding of what the modern handbag customer wants,” shares Marissa Galante Frank, Bloomingdale’s fashion director. “They continue to evolve their silhouettes, update their colorways and use playful new materials like shearling or pony hair. Their handbags have a sophisticated aesthetic at a price point that can appeal to a wide range of customers. Their belted tote styles are ideal for our customers who are looking for chic handbag options that can be worn from day to night.”

Although the brand is based in London, Llusia-Lindh was born in Barcelona and boasts an impressive resumé: an MBA from Harvard Business School and advisory posts at Burberry and LVMH. She is also a mother of three, so she is well-versed in the day-to-day needs of a working woman. “DeMellier reflects my personal values and has always aimed for something more, redefining luxury by putting meaning, female empowerment, ethical craftsmanship, and sustainability at its core,” she says. And at the end of the day, don’t we all just want a bag that gets us from point A to point B and helps us feel powerful getting there?

Below, shop DeMellier’s greatest hits and new fall offerings.

#6820. An Everyman’s Guide to Wearing a Necklace

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You already have a watch you like. This is the next step in your jewelry journey.

Men’s jewelry, I’ve learned, can be a slippery slope. I’ve been a watch guy since elementary school, if we’re generous enough to count a dual-sided compass/watch on a carabiner as a watch. In college I added a bracelet. I’ve been a ring guy for over ten years, thanks mostly to being married and partially to getting lunch drunk in Albuquerque, going ring shopping for my wife, and finding a sterling silver bear signet ring that I’ve worn nonstop since. I’ve had an earring for two years, thanks to a quick weekend in Miami and a well-timed Harrison Ford interview clip crossing my algorithm about getting his ear pierced in a Claire’s.

Recently, I’ve been curious about my personal final frontier of men’s jewelry—the necklace. The other listings above are all fairly low entry points, safe experiments in the world of men’s jewelry. The earring may be the exception, but this is 2026; one small silver hoop isn’t that daring, even in the Midwest. A necklace has never really been my thing. I haven’t felt the pull. That is, until this year.

For an unrelated project, I was breaking down some of the signature stylistic calling cards of Robert Redford. And there it was, across paparazzi pictures and movie stills and in memoriam roundups. In almost every photo, Redford has some kind of necklace, usually peeking from a precariously unbuttoned western shirt.

Further study of what we can safely call Hollywood’s golden age of off-duty style shows the same: McQueen and Newman and their contemporaries, swanning around on and off film, always wearing some sort of necklace or chain or pendant.

This is, of course, a common trap: to look at a famously handsome Hollywood star and think, yeah, all that’s separating me from THAT is a necklace. And sure, a good cheekbone and clavicle will get you pretty far in life. There’s no magic amulet here, nothing that will shazam you into leading-man looks. But there are a few lessons on styling from guys whose literal job it was to present themselves favorably to the world. And a few lessons on what necklaces to choose to help get there.

So, I put my professional online shopping style writing gig into overdrive, going deep into the Getty Images archives and confusing Google’s settings by repeatedly searching “Insert Hollywood Icon + shirtless” to glean some jewelry 101 tips from the professionals.

Instagram and (theoretically, since I don’t indulge) TikTok are full of maximalizing style trend chasers. Why wear one necklace when you can wear four? Why two rings when each finger could have two rings on it? Why dip your toes in the water when you can cannonball into the deep end? And sure, there’s some je ne sais quoi about just committing to something fully and completely out of the gate. But there’s also a lot of try-hard to it.

We’re channeling a more reserved, less #sponsoredpost era of dressing here that cares less about link clicks and more about how things look in the real world. So start simple and start with a chain. My favorite, and currently on daily rotation, is the Curb Chain from L.A.-based Bloedstone. Mine is sterling silver and in the inch length, which hits closer to the sternum than the neck. That’s the prescribed Hollywood A-lister way to wear it, but chart your own path if you want to. Miansai has a thicker 2mm Cuban chain that’s great too. And while we’re covering the M’s, Mejuri has one too. To my slight surprise, my home state watchmaker Shinola makes one, and less surprisingly so, Esquire-approved David Yurman has this giant one featuring a marketing campaign with Michael B. Jordan, who is picking up McQueen’s (and subsequently Brosnan’s) torch by adapting The Thomas Crown Affair.

There’s a whole vocabulary on link types and thickness and clasps out there. Don’t worry about that for now. Find something that calls to you and click add to cart. We’re starting slowly here.

Parting note. There are a lot of chains worn over T-shirts these days. To each their own, but the leading man’s move is to keep it under your shirt. If you want to show it off, just ditch the buttons.

A chain is simple, and sure, the greats wore them. But more often than not, they had something dangling from that chain. Medallion, pendant, a bottle opener in Cool Hand Luke. McQueen was often photographed, including in the pages of Esquire, wearing a St. Christopher medallion. Seeing as he’s the patron saint of travelers, St. Christopher medallions are having a bit of a cultural resurgence, partially because of their traditional association as a good-luck charm for travelers. There are some fun enamel ones on eBay, including these surfing-specific ones. Every once in a while, Nashville-based Americana experts Imogene + Willie will pull together a vintage edit of St. Christopher medallions that always sell out in around 24 hours. Speaking of Nashville, if you’re going to go new, go with Yearly Co. The gold jewelry experts have a 14k version of the iconography with a pitch-perfect 24-inch chain. I can attest to the quality—I gave my wife the brand’s diamond huggies and she wears them almost every day. As the grandson of a Protestant minister, I steer away from the saints out of genetic loyalty to Luther’s 95 Theses, but I recently picked up this Perseverance medallion from the brand, inspired by a vintage signet ring. It scratches the McQueen itch with flying colors.

Secondhand gold is also an option. Alloy Market recently came onto my radar, and I’m browsing it daily for myself and gifts for my wife. They’ve got a small but mighty collection of certified men’s items with pendants and medallions you can mix and match. All of the quality without any of the markup.

Don’t limit yourself to just a medallion, though. Get a little funky. Redford did for a while with this turquoise number that somehow looked even cooler hitting under the sternum. Shinola’s got an option there too. Bali-based Salty Dagger has an Odysseus-approved Greek mythology piece. And why not try a locket, like this one from Pattaraphan. Miansai has a great pendant. Get fancy with the Prevaut diamond ring deal. When in doubt, steal from the girls. I got this With Clarity birthstone necklace for my wife that I might take a swing at myself.

Peyote Bird High Desert Sterling Silver Necklace

Shinola 21mm Turquoise Inlay Tag Necklace

From the abbreviated/slightly bastardized Shakespeare: “All the world’s a stage and one man in his time plays many parts.” Jewelry can feel like a risk, even if you’re following some sort of contrived guide to styling and buying it. Sometimes we pretend until we’re comfortable actually being. Redford and McQueen and company were actors. Pretending was their legacy, and they were good at it. Take a leaf from their book and get dressed for the curtain call.

#6818. Everything We Know About ‘Black Doves’ Season 3

Black Doves, the sleek and sexy Netflix series about the complicated friendship between a spy played by Keira Knightley and an assassin played by Ben Whishaw, has returned for season 2. Already caught up? Not sure whether the long-term commitment is worth it? Here’s what we know about the odds that Black Doves will be renewed for season 3.

The first season brought Knightley’s character Helen, a spy married to a prominent conservative politician, back into action in the aftermath of her lover’s murder. The second season shoves her into the spotlight as her husband becomes prime minister. Meanwhile, in both seasons, Whishaw’s character Sam does his best to protect her while fighting his own demons, internally and externally. As various secret organizations and powers that be pull their strings to use them as pawns, the two of them can really only trust each other.

As of the season 2 release, Black Doves has not yet been renewed for season 3. The second season was announced before the first season dropped on Netflix. In theory, that’s concerning. But maybe the show was initially “ordered” for two seasons, and they announced it as an early renewal to create some well-deserved buzz before the premiere.

So far, season 2 reviews have been good. If the audiences follow, then we should (hopefully) get the good news soon.

No spoilers, but season 2 leaves a lot up in the air. Many of the characters are split up. Big secrets have been revealed. I wouldn’t be surprised if a third season went international again. Every story about a spy and/or assassin teaches us that you can’t escape your past.

However, since Black Doves is not based on a preexisting book series or film, it is difficult to decipher what happens next. That’s up to creator Joe Barton, who of course also created The Lazarus Project and recently adapted the play Amadeus to the small screen.

This is the thing. There were two years between Black Doves season 1 and season 2. That’s the problem with getting big movie stars to make television shows. Scheduling can be difficult.

But are we really going to have to wait until 2028 for Black Doves season 3? That would not be ideal. With all of the twists and turns of a show like this, you’ll need to do a total preseason series rewatch to remember what happened. If it is 2028, it’ll probably drop in November or December. That’s the one thing you can count on...this is a chilly series.

#6817. What Really Happened Between Sarah and John That Caused Their Divorce After ‘Love Is Blind’? A Much-Needed and Very Shocking Update

Love Is Blind Season 11 didn’t exactly go as planned for most of the engaged couples, with pretty much everyone breaking up before saying “I do.” But one pair managed to pull through! Sarah and John exchanged vows and got married (legally, I might add) during the Love Is Blind season finale.

“You are beautiful, you are brilliant, you are thoughtful, and I love you,” John said in his vows, while Sarah gushed “I love that you are sensitive and considerate and I find you funny.” Honestly, it was sweet!

The thing is...this finale was filmed over a year ago. Meaning, we’re in desperate need of an update on where Sarah and John stand today. Enter: the reunion! Netflix filmed the Love Is Blind reunion pretty recently, and there’s a shocking update on Sarah and John.

During an early reunion viewing on Tudum, John revealed he had an emotional affair about a year into the marriage, which led to their separation. "Unfortunately Sarah and I are no longer together, which is my fault,” John said. “We had a wonderful year of marriage, the two of us. Shared a lot of memories together. We had a wonderful year of marriage together, but unfortunately I made a mistake and there was a major breach of trust on my part. It's hard to talk about. There was a boys’ night I went on with a bunch of my friends and I just acted un-husbandly.”

He added that “nothing physical happened," and when pressed said “I texted one of the people we met out that night, and nothing happened, but she saw the texts and they were inappropriate for me to be sending as her husband. It was like three [texts], but they were bad.”

As for Sarah, she said the situation was “very sad,” that she'd struggled with “how John spoke to me” in the marriage, and that his behavior on the “boys’ night” was the "final thing that made me want to leave."

We don’t have information on where Sarah and John stand circa this moment, but it seems safe to assume they aren’t about to get back together given how the relationship ended.

#6816. Jesse Jo Stark on What It Means to Be a Muse

The singer’s new gothic country album, MUSE, releases on November 6

Jesse Jo Stark wrote her latest album MUSE in the wake of heartache. She left Los Angeles to find reprieve in the Colorado Rocky Mountains, where she found sanctuary among friends, a bottle of rosé, and a pink halter top and sweatpants combo. The melancholic album flowed out of her for three weeks, during which she transferred her emotions onto the page. “I am no longer holding on to a version of myself,” she tells Bazaar on a call from Los Angeles, on her way to a photoshoot. “There’s a freedom in moving forward.”

Stark keeps pressing on, no matter what. Outside of songwriting, she plays a key role in Chrome Hearts as the daughter of founder Richard Stark. She spent her formative years surrounded by musicians, artists, designers, and every kind of creative person imaginable, all of whom inspired her to pursue her own vision. That world endlessly inspires her to create, even when she feels low. “I’ve worn things that are absolutely horrible, and I’ve written songs that I don’t necessarily love,” she says, “but when you grow, you go back to a less constructed version of yourself.”

This more authentic version of herself fuels MUSE. She shed some of the macabre edge of her debut album 2022’s Doomed in favor of a softer reflection on her life, love, femininity, and the natural world that inspired these songs. But above all, Stark offers a reflection on her life being put on a pedestal, a narrative that deeply considers the women in her life, from friends to her mother, Laurie Lynn Stark. This 13-track gothic country album appropriately conveys Stark’s energy, with visuals described as “somewhat of a horror film, but I’m wearing a lace skirt.” She wrote on her Instagram, announcing the album, that the theme is “fragile but fierce femininity,” best to “enjoy with a glass of red.”

A few weeks before the release, Bazaar spoke to Stark about the women who inspired her, Napoleon’s poems to Josephine, writing songs in Colorado, and what it means to be a muse.

I was looking at a lot of mythology and history and the muses of rock and roll—women of rock and roll. If you break down the word itself, you get “used,” “amused,” “music.” I think women are admired and desired, but not always heard. For me, women have this pivotal point in their life—hopefully, at one point—where they regain their strength and voice, and this album was that for me. The muse, throughout history, could also have a negative connotation and feel lonely. But I felt like putting emphasis on the reciprocity of it—then owning that and allowing it to feel beautiful. It’s a powerful word. It felt fitting for this time in my life.

There’s so much serious crossover. Being able to develop the way I feel my best on stage and design what I wear enables me to embody that version of myself, to gather the strength to perform. Women like Daphne Guinness, Isabella Blow and Jane Birkin have really inspired me. I’ve always wanted to not just completely mirror something from the past, but recreate it as my own. I wear a loincloth on stage—it’s not that it hasn’t been done before, but the way I wear it or the way it’s designed, the accessories and tops that are paired with it... I do feel confident in me extending this already living thing, but making it my own.

When I was little, I always had an attitude about the two not going hand in hand. I think that was incorrect. They’re pretty much the same expression: one’s a vocal cord and playing guitar, and the other’s a boot that you walk in.

It’s weird. My last album was highly visual. There was a lot of fantasy involved, and this is very stripped back; [both] vocal production-wise and stylistically. I just did my first acoustic show, and I was wearing a vintage silk dress with a heel, and my hair was straight, and I had minimal makeup. Because there was such a rawness to it, especially where I recorded, which was amongst nature and very secluded. With this record, anytime I tried to put emphasis on creating a specific vibe, I rejected that.

The space and the quietness and the snow—I’ve never been one to be completely influenced by nature. I don’t love green or grass, but I love to sit in it and lie in it. I love being with animals and the snow and the sun. All these natural tones, I’m embracing the color brown like I haven’t before. It sounds so silly, but they represent a softness that I didn’t have for a while… The visuals have become somewhat of a horror film, but I’m wearing a lace skirt.

My main single from the record, “age of being honest,” has been visually stimulating and powerful. I can feel every emotion through that visual. It’s rawness—in the vocals and everything. “colorado” is the place that I literally went to and didn’t really know what was going to come from it. That set the tone for the rest of the record.

The shedding was happening as I was driving. The heartache was there, so it wasn’t difficult once I sat down to break through that human brick wall. I wanted to feel everything right away. I gotta be real, it was a white boa, a bottle of rosé, people I fuck with, the sun, and being in a really beautiful pink halter top and sweatpants half the time. I don’t think there was much going on except pain and not knowing where it was going and just sitting on the floor, living in a house with people for two weeks or a month. You can’t escape what you’re doing; I really leaned into that.

My dad had a lot to do with my love of music. I asked to play guitar at a young age. I was around a lot of rock and roll and glam—these really amazing artists who were way older than me. This desire to—and I was really shy—create something as beautiful as they had or stand as my parents could and make my own way. Music was always mine, so there was an independence to it. Finding yourself in writing, you don’t think you can write, and then you start writing poems. I loved Britney Spears. I loved the Spice Girls. But then my dad would play Merle Haggard, and he also let me listen to Christina Aguilera. I found punk rock when I was 13 because I fell in love with a punk boy. Then you somehow are, you’re mad at everything, and you found your voice in that way. It just started to become this whole thing, and then I started singing covers because I wasn’t ready to write. And then when you write, you really write your first song—to create something out of nothing is really funny because the fact that we think we get to do that is funny to me, but also so freeing and amazing. You never want to stop.

#6812. This 100-Year-Old Louisiana Home Got a New Plot of Land and a Colorful New Chapter

The expansive property also includes a pool house and carriage house.

Long before it became the backdrop for family dinners, afternoon swims, and spirited tennis matches, this century-old Colonial in Baton Rouge, Louisiana, had already lived a rather extraordinary life. In the late 1920s, the house was dismantled and relocated from Prairieville to its current address, where its distinctive red clay roof tiles—salvaged from Louisiana State University—have presided over a landscape of majestic live oaks ever since. Decades later, a young girl growing up down the street would admire the house, never imagining that one day she’d raise a family of her own beneath that very roof.

When she and her husband finally purchased the property, they knew that turning a beloved neighborhood landmark into a forever home for themselves and their three daughters would require a little imagination—and a lot of careful planning. Enter interior designer Anne Underwood, who was tasked with bringing the historic residence into the present while preserving the quirks that made it so beloved. Over several years, the project grew from a renovation of the original four-bedroom home into a sprawling family compound, complete with a carriage house, pool house, and landscaped grounds designed to make an ordinary weekend feel like a vacation.

Inside, Underwood embraced the home’s traditional bones without letting them stiffen the home (and the family’s) sunny disposition. Lemon-yellow checkerboard floors mingle with antique furnishings (an element Underwood says is a must for creating “a lived-in, collected-over-time feel that I find crucial to making a true home”), hand-painted finishes, and exuberant textiles. A whimsical chandelier welcomes guests into the living room, while a tucked-away banquette has become an unofficial headquarters for homework, puzzles, and art projects.

Outside, the property’s newest additions—including a pool house and carriage house—offer an equally playful proposition. Conceived as the estate’s crown jewel, the pool house features pale wood walls, a colorful handwoven light fixture, and a monumental wood-burning pizza oven. The homeowners’ relatives jokingly dubbed the expanding property their own private resort, a description Underwood happily embraces.

“So much was poured into this project to make it unique, joyful, and really functional for the clients,” says Underwood. “The warmth and care of each detailed layer creates a sense of ease, which is what facilitates the joyfulness that plays out inside and outside these walls.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Anne UnderwoodArchitect: LRK ArchitectsContractor: Bernhardt Normand ConstructionStyling: Lucy BammanLandscape Architect: ARBO Landscape Architects Location: Baton Rouge, LouisianaThe Space: A four-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom main house, with a carriage house and pool house.

With a simpler architectural structure, the pool house was a place where Underwood leaned into the family’s playful energy. “We selected the wall stain to look like the inside of a shell and wanted the furnishings to feel layered, colorful, vibrant—like a vacation but also very durable for wet swimsuits and sweaty tennis players!” she adds.

The first selection for the pool house was also its most exuberant: a 12-light PET Lamp fixture, with each colorful pendant and cord individually customized and positioned beneath the soaring vaulted ceiling. “Its colorful, playful nature was exactly what we wanted and is very effective for balancing light throughout the space with high vaulted ceilings,” says Underwood. Below, a generous sectional upholstered in indoor-outdoor fabric creates an inviting place to retreat after a swim.

With vibrant Moroccan mosaic flooring, blue zellige shower tile, and a vintage wooden mirror, the pool house bathroom could easily belong to a boutique hotel on some far-flung island. Wood siding and thoughtfully chosen antique details lend the space a relaxed, resort-inspired character that feels right at home beside the pool.

Tradition gets a playful twist in the home’s primary gathering space, where a hand-knotted rug and classic upholstered furnishings mingle with an exuberant chandelier by Stray Dog Designs.

“This house lacks a foyer, so we wanted the living room to be welcoming with plenty of seating and layers of color and accessories,” says Underwood. “While this is the more ‘formal’ living space, we selected a bright, whimsical light fixture to let guests know that these homeowners don’t take themselves (or their interiors) too seriously.”

A passageway between the living room and the home’s more casual quarters, the dining room needed to make an impression without feeling overly precious. Schumacher’s Weeping Pine wallpaper wraps the space in pattern, while pale blue trim, upholstered chairs, and vintage sconces sourced from Round Top lend the formal setting an unexpected sense of ease.

With the adjoining breakfast room to consider, Underwood gave the kitchen a quieter disposition. Existing cabinetry received a custom pale green stain, expertly applied by a local faux-finish artist, while paneled appliances and tonal counter stools create a seamless effect. The yellow-and-white checkerboard flooring continues underfoot, connecting the two spaces.

A powder room is as good a place as any to indulge a dramatic streak. “We wanted a bold escape for guests in the powder bath, and this incredible grasscloth wallpaper was the jumping-off point,” says Underwood. She complemented the Aux Abris pattern’s lush, atmospheric tone with a wood-grained antique chest repurposed as a marble-topped vanity. Vintage Murano glass sconces and a striped inlay mirror add a final dose of eccentricity.

In the office, Underwood leaned into the appeal of a well-appointed study, furnishing this hardworking space almost entirely with vintage and antique finds. Custom cabinetry painted in Benjamin Moore’s Templeton Gray conceals printers, files, and other less-than-picturesque necessities, while mohair-upholstered armchairs make the space equally suited to work and contemplation.

As it turns out, the inspiration for the primary suite was waiting just outside the window. “I started with the idea of bringing materials—like the patinated copper on all exterior lanterns and gutters—from the exterior to the interior,” says Underwood. “While the walls are painted a green/blue color reminiscent of the patina of weathered copper, the accents in the room are warm shades of camel and copper.”

A Gustavian antique secretary and dimensional papier-maché art from Gusto complete the room's quietly collected atmosphere.

The generously proportioned soaking tub in the primary bathroom has already lived several lives, first as a favorite bathing spot for the family’s three young daughters, and now as a welcome retreat for their parents. Underwood carried the bedroom’s blue-green palette into the bath, introducing blush-patterned wallpaper, graphic stone tile, and black accents to balance the room’s softer flourishes.

In daughter Emily’s room, Underwood chose to embrace the sloped ceiling, outfitting it with custom bed canopies that create an enveloping, almost storybook atmosphere. Layers of patterned bedding, including bespoke eiderdowns made in the UK, heighten the cozy effect, while chunky upholstered ottomans offer a welcome counterpoint to the room’s more feminine details.

Making sense of the home’s quirky upstairs architecture required some clever spatial maneuvering. Underwood divided one large bathroom into two separate spaces, each with its own dressing vanity. In daughter Olivia’s bath, Salvesen Graham floral wallpaper covers the sloped walls and ceiling, visually softening the room’s unusual angles. “The pattern play in the tile, wallpaper, and fabrics keeps the energy moving throughout the space,” she adds.

In the carriage house, Underwood continued the layered approach with velvet, chenille, and a generously proportioned sectional, creating a retreat where overnight guests can comfortably settle in for an afternoon—or an entire weekend.

The carriage house kitchen offers guests all the comforts of home, with a nostalgic twist. Cabinetry painted Benjamin Moore’s Mistletoe features vintage-inspired vent detailing, while a retro SMEG refrigerator reinforces the room’s charming, slightly old-fashioned character.

Underwood preserved the home’s original red clay roof tiles while refreshing its facade using Benjamin Moore’s White Dov on the siding and Kennebunkport Green on the shutters. An expanded bluestone sidewalk gives the front door prominence while mature live oaks lend the property a sense of permanence that no renovation could replicate.

Anne Underwood is the founder of Underwood Interiors. Known for her thoughtful approach to creating layered, Anne creates livable spaces that balance timeless tradition with a fresh, playful sensibility. With an appreciation for antiques, vintage furnishings, and artisanal craftsmanship, her interiors feel collected over time rather than decorated all at once. Her work celebrates color, pattern, and architectural character, with an emphasis on thoughtful details and everyday functionality. Whether reimagining a historic residence or designing a new space from the ground up, she believes the most successful interiors reflect the personalities of the people who call them home.

#6810. One of My Least Favorite Politicians Lost an Election This Week. I Can’t Stop Laughing About It.

This guy was an ur-Trumper before Trump, and I’m so glad we’re rid of him.

This one is for me. Pardon me while I bust a gut.

Kris Kobach of Kansas—and never has a phrase with three K’s been more apt—is one of the founding weasels of the modern voter-suppression effort. If there is an odious policy position concocted since 2008, Kobach has run the laboratory that produced it. Imaginary voter fraud? Kobach led the farcical election-integrity commission during Trump I that fell apart after a contentious 2017 hearing in New Hampshire at which Kobach’s contentions were disemboweled by Maine secretary of state Matthew Dunlap. Draconian immigration policies? As Kansas secretary of state, Kobach tried to initiate a “proof of citizenship” law that lined him up on the wrong side of the League of Women Voters. Outright racism? Kobach told a radio program that the Obama administration was not far from a call for an end to all prosecutions of African Americans. If there was an ur-Trumper before Trump, it was Kris Kobach.

I thought we were rid of him when he ran for governor in 2018 and lost. I thought we were rid of him when he ran for the Senate in 2020 and lost. I thought we were rid of him when he got tangled up with Steve Bannon’s Build the Wall scam. But he was like the shingles virus in the body politic. He got elected Kansas attorney general in 2022.

And, on Tuesday, and extending into Thursday afternoon, it looks as though Kris Kobach might have lost another election. May it be his last.

This Mississippi Senate Race Is Making Me Nervous

#6808. Why Wallpaper Is the Secret to Making Even the Smallest Bathroom Feel Special

A little pattern can turn into a true design moment.

When you’re working with limited space, it’s almost instinctive to tone down your design details to avoid feeling overwhelmed—but even small spaces need character. Our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston is a master class in excellent design, no matter the room size or purpose. This year’s top recommendation for a chic bath is wallcoverings.

When installed properly, wallcoverings are one of the most reliable ways to add an extra touch of flair to interiors. Here’s how the designers of our 2026 Whole Home used them to bring big personality to small spaces.

For many designers, powder rooms are the perfect opportunity to play. Designer Mark Kennamer, founder and principal designer of his eponymous Birmingham, Alabama–based firm, used wallcoverings to add bespoke character to a nearly monochromatic powder-pink scheme. In the guest bath, the Zoffany mauve moiré wallcovering lends a subtle shimmer and dimension, while its wood-like grain adds an unexpected, welcome layer of texture.

Anissa Zajac of House Seven Design outfitted the primary bathroom with a botanical-printed wallcovering by Sanderson that incorporated nearly every hue in her palette while adding a dose of whimsy. The pattern’s vertical lines play off the texture of the fluted vanity cabinets, making a bold statement without straying too far from the design scheme.

In the gallery bath, the team at R&R Interior Design 365 took inspiration from Charleston’s famed cobblestone roads. They opted for a blue-brick wallpaper by Perennials to channel the perfect level of city flair while keeping the feel warm, intimate, and inviting.

#6805. 27 Creative Ways Designers Hide TVs for a Stylish, Screen-Free Environment

These genius tricks keep it accessible yet out of sight.

For years, many designers and design-loving homeowners have felt that having the TV on display is a major faux pas. Even though most of us have one and use it daily, there’s something unappealing about having a big black box against the wall and breaking up the interior flow. It’s become even more of an eyesore now that analog spaces are becoming more popular.

While one solution is to invest in a TV that can disguise itself as artwork, those come at a high price; the other option is to learn how to hide the TV in a way that doesn’t feel obvious or tacky, and thankfully, designers have a lot of ideas.

We’ve also included plenty of inspiring photos and expert tips to guide you along the way. If your goal is to reduce the visual presence of technology in your home, you’re in the right place. Keep reading to find inventive ways to make any room feel screen-free—at least when the TV isn’t in use.

Of course, you can hide your television the same way you'd hide anything else you wouldn't want on full display at all times: tuck it into a cabinet. At least, that's what designer Kylie Buhagiar at Melbourne, Australia-based Golden did. "In the main living room, the TV is discreetly integrated into a wall-hung metal joinery box, with hinged doors that elegantly stack back to reveal the screen when needed, maintaining a clean and uncluttered aesthetic," Buhagiar says. "This thoughtful design transforms the joinery into a sculptural piece of furniture, allowing the space to effortlessly transition from a main living room to a calm sitting area, enhancing the home's rich yet comfortable atmosphere."

The whole point of concealing the TV is to hide it, but sometimes, the mechanism used to hide the screen just draws more attention to the area. Gillian Segal, an interior designer based in Vancouver, British Columbia, came up with a clever solution that basically looks like the wall. "This room functioned as both a formal living space and informal family room (complete with movie nights and lots of kids' TV shows), so we wanted a design that felt both elegant and approachable," Segal explains. "We concealed the TV behind a very simple millwork door system that folds open and closed. It was a cost-effective solution that allowed other elements in the room to shine."

It’s no secret that many designers find the Samsung Frame TV to be beautiful, but designer Ariel Okin takes it a step further by adding a literal frame to the Frame. This hidden-TV tactic ensures that it matches other elements in the space, such as the fireplace screen, thus hiding it in plain sight.

If you don’t want to think about your TV when it’s off, you can cleverly camouflage it. In this living room, designer Emily Del Bello painted a shadowy accent wall black and mounted the TV there—you wouldn't even notice it at first glance.

In a room as traditional and cohesive as this, you probably don't want a big black rectangle interrupting your aesthetic, so do what designer Lynne Uhalt did and hide your TV in a closet. This guest room is heavily inspired by European interiors and architecture, but Uhalt still wanted the guests to be able to watch TV and relax in bed. As a design-friendly solution, she put the TV in one of the closets, which still boasts enough room to enough to hold clothes.

Designer John Wooden maximized limited square footage in his clients' cottage by cleverly hiding several items, including placing their TV in a beautiful credenza. One of the simplest methods to hide your TV is by incorporating it into furniture that already complements the space. This wooden mid-century piece fits perfectly into the room, making it an ideal spot for a hidden TV.

In a maximalist, highly colorful space like this, designer Shazalynn Cavin-Winfrey couldn't see how a TV could work in the room, so she decided to hide it in the green cabinet in the back. However, the designer took it a step further and covered the glass with fabric that pulls hues from the rest of the room.

Everick Brown designed a statement wall encased in custom steel with walnut insets to create a “floating credenza” for a hidden TV. A console table behind the sofa helps separate the two living room zones.

Designed to look like abstract artwork, Kristin Fine hid the TV behind modern paneling above the fireplace. It suits the neutral tones nicely and maintains the refined and grown-up atmosphere while also ensuring that it's family-friendly.

This living room sitting area revolves around the corner fireplace. To ensure that the TV didn't take up too much valuable visual space, designer Denise McGaha invested in a Samsung Frame and disguised it as a photograph that complements the blue and grey color scheme.

This outdoor living room, designed by Amanda Lindroth, is the perfect place to entertain and unwind. Look closely at the pagoda-shaped cabinet above the fireplace, and you'll notice that it opens to reveal a hidden TV. It was based on a design from St. Michael's Mount castle in England.

What do you do in a busy kitchen where the children love TV and the mom doesn't—but she does want to keep an eye on said kids? Enter the Samsung Frame, once again making it all work in family homes with competing interests and preferences, like in April Tomlin Interiors designer Sarah Rogers's kitchen here.

A bi-fold panel painting by Stuart Coleman Budd hides a TV, but the goal was to be transparent. “Bronze hinges honestly express that it’s a movable screen—that this is true kinetic art,” says architect Ken Pursley of Pursley Dixon Architecture.

This formal living room, designed by Mark Cunningham, doubles as a more relaxed and casual hangout room. To ensure that it could do both well, Cunningham built a hidden TV into the back wall shelf that pops up when the occupants want to unwind with a good movie.

Don’t hide it, just make it one with your wall. This gallery wall of various-sized framed prints blends in with the Samsung Frame TV, which has a wood frame and a rotating display of art images. “None of my clients' guests realize it's a TV when they first walk into the room,” says Natalie Myer of Veneer Designs.

Custom paneling over a fireplace elevates the television area in this bedroom lounge zone by Studio McGee. Flush with the fireplace facade and further disguised by a custom mirror, the hidden TV definitely will not be ruining the chic decor scheme here.

Related Story: Shea McGee Just Dropped a Cottagecore Home Collab With a Major Fashion Brand

Designer CeCe Barfield Thompson hid a TV behind a retractable antique mirror for maximum glamour. “In an oak-paneled Manhattan library, I designed an antique mirror to hide the wall-mounted television,” Thompson says. “The mirror's lower panels retract like a garage door to reveal an entertainment system behind. This mechanism allowed us to create a room that was incredibly functional, without sacrificing an ounce of beauty!”

Related Story: This Manhattan Apartment Showcases an Abundance of Antiques and Contemporary Art

Here's another example of custom cabinetry with a hidden TV. A sliding door flush with the wood accent wall makes this media room designed by Heidi Caillier extra discreet and pretty. After all, the custom upholstery, drapery, and grasscloth wallpaper are too good to get lost in the entertainment system shuffle.

Designer Nina Farmer took the most elegant approach possible—hiding the TV in a custom mirrored cabinet atop the mantel. “The living room needed a concealed TV due to the formality of the architecture,” she says. “Hand-blown mirror was used on the doors so that the enclosure fit with the original 1850s marble mantel. It has concealed hinges and no pull, so when it’s closed, you would never know what’s behind it.”

If you can’t outright conceal your screen, the key is to avoid the “black hole” effect when it’s off. “I always try to blend them into the architecture of the house,” says designer Eche Martinez. “We recently completed a project in Belvedere, CA, where the homeowners were hesitant about installing a TV in their living room. To solve this, we decided that the best way to divert attention from the TV when it was not in use was to have an oversized, freestanding piece of art right next to it. Clients loved it, and above all, the piece looks great in the room."

#6804. Godzilla Minus Zero Is a Sequel Over 50 Years in the Making

The secret to the terrifying monster melee film? “I want the audience to feel that anxiety,” director Takashi Yamazaki tells Esquire.

However much Takashi Yamazaki loves Godzilla, he might love his rival more. In 2023, the filmmaker wowed the world with Godzilla Minus One, an emotionally stirring disaster epic that told a deeply human story about sacrifice, self-forgiveness, and the power of found families, against the backdrop of postwar Japan. At the 96th Academy Awards, Godzilla Minus One clinched the Oscar for Best Visual Effects, which not only righteously recognized its handsome design but lent credence to its rep as a prestige-level event film.

But many years ago, its director was just a seven-year-old boy eager to imagine his hometown wrecked by Ghidorah, one of Godzilla’s most prolific enemies. “In the theaters, fifty-five years ago, was the first time I saw Ghidorah,” Yamazaki tells Esquire via English interpreter. It was at a revival screening of the 1964 film Ghidorah, the Three-Headed Monster, when a school-aged Yamazaki saw a gold dragon with three heads stomp over Matsumoto City, where his family lived. But instead of glee, Yamazaki actually felt crushing ... disappointment.

“I was looking forward to seeing the utter destruction of my hometown by that kaiju,” Yamazaki says with a laugh. “They didn’t go as far as I wanted them to. So what I’ve done here is my childhood dream come true.”

On Friday, November 6, the King of the Monsters returns to the screen in Godzilla Minus Zero. Set in 1949, the sequel continues the story of Godzilla Minus One’s ex-kamikaze pilot Kōichi (Ryunosuke Kamiki). It’s been two years since Godzilla’s rise, and now he and his wife Noriko (Minami Hamabe) are raising their adopted daughter Akiko (Sae Nagatani). But when Ghidorah shows up, Kōichi regroups with his colleagues who’ve since formed the Large-Scale Disaster Response Bureau. While trying to stop Ghidorah’s terrible rampage, they cook up a desperate, if inevitable plan: Convince Godzilla to fight him.

Although Yamazaki spent the promotional cycle of Godzilla Minus One insisting that he had no plans for a sequel, the director had actually drummed up a story outline for Minus Zero. Yamazaki tells Esquire that he wanted to keep following the characters he introduced in his previous film to see how their traumatic circumstances could bring them closer together.

“In this series, Minus One and Minus Zero, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker says. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge. I wanted to see how they were going to deal with that. I love this family. I’m very attached to this family. I just wanted to see how they march forward.”

Below, Yamazaki reveals how surprised he was by the Western reception toward his first movie, why he included Ghidorah in the sequel, and whether a third movie is in the cards.

This interview has been edited for clarity, with Japanese-to-English translation performed by an interpreter.

ESQUIRE: Let’s rewind to the release of Godzilla Minus One in 2023. The movie was acclaimed outside Japan, particularly in the United States, while critics in Japan were less enthused. Did this response surprise you?

YAMAZAKI: The way Minus One was received in the United States was a surprise to me. We made Minus One without international audiences in mind. Step by step, it grew larger; first there was the L.A. premiere, which was received with a passionate reaction. That made me happy. Then the release came, and we started to see all of these comments and reaction clips on YouTube. That was surprising. I feel grateful because it was something I had not foreseen or imagined.

Why do you think there was such a stark difference towards Minus One from U.S. audiences versus Japan?

U.S. fans are so expressive. We tend to be more reserved. We don’t wear our excitement on our sleeves. I guess this is just how American people are versus Japanese. But to experience that kind of reception was wonderful. I remember watching American movies and imagining how great it would be to put a film out there and have that kind of reaction. That is what came true. So I’m very grateful.

When you first revealed Godzilla Minus Zero, it was with a teaser that showed Godzilla stomping into New York City. What compelled you to make this the very first look at the movie?

I wanted the first [look] of Minus Zero to be impactful. To have people wondering, “What the hell is going to happen?” Because the first film was so wonderfully and warmly received among American audiences, this was a way for me to communicate to the American audience, “Your country is part of the story.”

Between these two movies, you make the point that just because you aren’t related by blood or marriage doesn’t mean you can’t be a family. Where does this story come from? Do you have any similar relationships in your life?

This does not come from my personal experience, but it is something I touched on in a film I made about twenty years ago, Always: Sunset on Third Street. I feel that, if a parent and child are non-biological, sometimes their connection is stronger than biological parents and children. One of my inspirations is Shuhei Fujisawa. One of his novels is a story about a ronin who accepts a mother and child, albeit reluctantly at first, as his own family. I’ve been inspired and affected by that.

Godzilla Minus Zero continues the postwar period setting you introduced in the first film. What about Japan’s status in the immediate aftermath of World War II makes it fertile ground for the stories you want to tell with Godzilla?

With Godzilla Minus One, I attempted to tell the story of, What do people do when they are lost? When they are stripped of all means to fight? With this one, it’s a continuation of that story, about people who have to fight without anything to fight with when an even greater catastrophe than Minus One befalls them. I also wanted to tell the story of people not using weapons, but their brains. Their wits and wisdom. In this film, you see how they have to resort to means no human being should resort to. Trying to put an end to it all, but having to resort to taboo means.

Out of all of Godzilla’s other rivals, what excites you most about Ghidorah?

I was born in the Year of the Dragon, so it just so happens I love dragons. But it’s also the form factor of this kaiju. It straddles the line; if it were any weirder as a form, it would be really weird. But it’s cool visually. It’s a strange kaiju you can’t compare with anything. And it’s been Godzilla’s archnemesis for years, and the strongest nemesis of Godzilla. I don’t know how many more Godzilla films I will be invited to do, so I didn’t want to save Ghidorah for later.

Sometimes the spectacle of Godzilla fighting another monster undermines the dramatic weight he’s meant to carry. How did you strike the balance between maintaining Godzilla’s terrifying aura and the blockbuster thrills of watching him fight?

The way I approached this, in what we call a versus film, I wanted to portray how mankind relates to Godzilla and Ghidorah. Godzilla is a wild beast that humans have to deal with. Mankind harbors fear and awe in the face of Godzilla. But as I mentioned, they have to step into a taboo [solution], which mankind must never step into, to deal with this godlike beast. It is that fear and awe and conflict I wanted to depict. It was never my intention to have Godzilla as a friendly kaiju.

Godzilla has appeared in many different shades over the years. He’s been a monster, a folk hero, even a mascot. How do you, personally, see Godzilla? Is your depiction of him in your films reflective of how you think the monster should be?

I see Godzilla as a very angry god, or the wrath of God, because it is a metaphor for nuclear weapons and war. If we are going to tell a story set in the 1940s, after World War II, relying on Godzilla’s powers is tantamount to relying on nuclear weapons. Godzilla has that much of an immense power. There’s no way we can utilize his power without suffering repercussions. There is no happiness on the other side.

There have been a lot of Godzilla movies made in Hollywood lately. To your view, what are American filmmakers getting right or wrong?

I have no complaints about the different ways in which Godzilla is depicted. That’s actually one of the things I like about Godzilla. There’s room for avenues and perspectives. You also have Showa Era Godzilla that is quite funny. Then you have the Hollywood Godzilla, the monster battle films. They’re all wonderful. It’s great Godzilla can encompass all of these forms. I don’t intend at all to say, “This Godzilla is good” and “This Godzilla is not right.”

When Godzilla Minus One first released, there was so much talk about how it achieved so much with a limited budget. With Godzilla Minus Zero having more money than its predecessor, what were you able to accomplish on a technical level that wasn’t possible before?

We put all of that extra money into the visuals. I don’t want you looking at a movie, I want you in the story. There are a couple scenes where it’s longer takes, in which we show the kaiju coming and the people are scrambling, trying to escape but there’s nowhere to go. I want the audience to feel that anxiety. It is also the first Japanese film filmed for IMAX. So we were able to realize that immersive in-theater experience. It’s not a film, it’s an experience.

What are the chances of a black-and-white “Minus Color” version of Godzilla Minus Zero?

I don’t know. Personally, I want to make it. It’s of the times, isn’t it? It feels like a documentary. It’s something I want to do. I don’t know if I’ll do it yet.

You said during Minus One you didn’t plan on doing a sequel. So I’m asking now, after Minus Zero, do you have plans for a third movie?

It would be great if we could do a third film. But we have to have an offer before we embark on a new project. I’m also curious what other people might do with Godzilla in other films. Nothing is planned yet.

If there’s a third movie, do you imagine including other enemies from Godzilla’s history? I ask as a big Destroyah fan...

[Laughs]. I’ll refrain from any comments that might be misconstrued as huge news. But it would be interesting to have the option of a completely new kaiju appear. Already existing kaiju would be great, but new kaiju too.

You’re now working on your first English-language Hollywood movie, Grandgear. How are you adapting after a long career making movies in Japan?

It’s like I’m starting all over again. It makes me feel the way I felt when I was making my debut in Japan. I don’t really care about making films in Hollywood versus making films in Japan, as long as I can embark on a project that I truly want to make going forward. No matter the territory.

#6800. 7 Desert Boots That Can Survive the Winter

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Despite the name, these chukkas are built to be worn in all conditions.

Winter weather can get ugly. If it’s not wind and rain, it’s slushy sidewalks and unexpected snow flurries. You obviously need shoes that can handle a little precipitation, but the key phrase here is a little. You don’t want to stomp around in heavy winter boots, especially if we want to look nice. Dress shoes are a tough fit in winter, as are dress boots. Funny enough, between December and March, you know what our go-to footwear becomes? Desert boots. Technically chukka boots, but a few desert boots make the cut here.

But that’s something to note. The Esquire office does not use the term interchangeably—but the Internet does, so we’re going along with it. We know some of you—nerds, mostly—have stopped reading this article because you don’t think we know the difference between the two. We’re well aware of the square and rectangle-ism: All desert boots are chukkas, not all chukkas are desert boots.

If you’re not a nerd and want to learn something new, chukkas are ankle-high boots with open lacing and two or three pairs of eyelets, often made with leather uppers—leather or rubber soled. Desert boots are a subsection of this silhouette that typically features a suede upper (nubuck, sometimes) with lighter-colored, more flexible crepe soles. They are not as winterized—suede, especially, is known to take a beating from moisture—hence the name. The details vary here and there, but both styles can work for winter if you choose wisely.

We found seven pairs of chukka boots—yes, some of which are desert boots—with weather-friendly features, from water-resistant uppers to sturdy rubber soles. Most have both, so you can find a pair that looks sharp without leaving you entirely at the mercy of the winter weather forecast.

These chukka boots might as well be dress boots. They look sharp no matter what outfit you pair them with, and the ultra-refined, premium calfskin leather upper is naturally water-resistant; to a point, that is—we wouldn’t wear them in the pouring rain.

They’re very comfortable thanks to a soft, leather lining on the inside and open-cell foam insoles that provide supportive cushioning. And the flexible outsoles made from XL Extralight injection molding are a nice touch that give the boot some lightweight flexibility.

For a classic pair of chukka boots that come at a good price and have a wide range of colors and sizes, Thursday Boot Co. is the way to go. These chukkas come in a variety of colors and materials, but our favorite has to be the Mocha colorway in weather-safe suede. It’s rare to find a pair of suede shoes you can actually get wet without ruining, but these ones can brave the weather while still looking great. And with studded rubber outsoles, you have some extra traction and durability. If you want even more traction and durability in the soles, try one of the Rugged & Resilient styles with anti-slip wedge rubber outsoles.

Here’s another suede option you can actually wear during the winter; Sabah treats the suede upper of this boot to be water resistant, but you can still add a suede treatment for extra protection if you want to. These boots are handmade in Monte San Giusto, Italy, and you can trust that they were made with lots of skill and care. The insole and midsole are made from vegetable tanned leather, so they end up forming to your feet once you wear them, making them extra comfortable.

If you like the look of a gum-sole sneaker, you might like how it looks on a boot, too. With these desert boots, SeaVees blends the classic gum-sole look with the more rugged desert boot style, and the result is a versatile boot that can withstand pretty much any weather. With water-resistant rugged oiled leather uppers and natural rubber soles, these guys can get you through quite a lot.

These are going to be the least weather-ready on this list. You don’t want to take them out in a snowstorm, but damn do they look good. Imagine these worn to a holiday party, worn with a nice pair of chinos and fair-isle sweater.

The suede upper is a true not-water-resistant suede. But they do have durable rubber soles. The stitched moc-toe is a fun detail that makes these chukka boots stand out from the rest of the pack. Drake’s notes that these boots run small, so they recommend sizing up by a half-size when ordering online.

We’re starting to get pretty far away from the chukka heart, but you can see the chukka-shaped vamp and the ankle-y shape. Plus, when we think of rugged workwear boots, we think of Timberland. The upper is made from waterproof leather and has a TimberDry waterproof membrane for extra protection, and you can see that Gripstick rubber outsole gives you some distance from the ground and provides great traction as well.

And finally, we can’t talk about chukka boots without mentioning Clarks. The original Clarks desert boot was designed in 1949 and inspired by the rough suede and crepe-soled boots worn by off-duty British soldiers during WWII (as well as the shoes worn by polo players in India and South African veldskoen shoes). Today, the brand’s original style is still going strong, and you can get a pair for under $100.
