#6775. Casio’s New Mechanical Field Watch Is a Game-Changer

The affordable titanium timepiece puts the Japanese brand squarely in the consideration set for budding collectors.

Since forever, Casio has dominated the market for affordable digital and quartz watches. Now it's making a play for the wallet-friendly end of the mechanical watch market, territory traditionally dominated by fellow Japanese manufacturers Seiko, Citizen, and Orient.

The new CMK-100 is Casio's first automatic watch to carry its mainline branding, following the introduction of mechanical models under its Edifice label last year. And on paper, it's a pretty compelling proposition.

Inspired by classic military field watches—with more than a passing resemblance to the Rolex Explorer and the Tudor Ranger—the CMK-100 has a 38mm titanium case, sapphire crystal, screw-down crown, and 100 meters of water resistance. Its textured dial features luminous Arabic numerals, while a Japanese automatic movement provides a 42-hour power reserve. The movement's manufacturer hasn't been disclosed, although its specifications suggest it could be a Miyota 8215, a calibre used by Timex, Spinnaker, and Invicta, among others.

There are five versions: one on a titanium bracelet, two on nylon straps, and two more on Italian leather straps—including a rather jazzy brown-dial model with a rose gold-colored case. Prices range from $330 to $440.

For Seiko, which has built a substantial business selling affordable mechanical watches through collections such as the Seiko 5 Sports, Casio's arrival in this category represents an interesting challenge. Titanium cases and sapphire crystals are hardly standard equipment at this price, and Casio already has an enormous following among younger watch buyers.

Whether those customers will embrace a mechanical from the home of G-Shock remains to be seen. But for anyone shopping for a good-looking, affordable automatic watch, there's now another Japanese name in the running for your attention.

#6773. Flattering and Low-Maintenance, the Midi Bob With Bangs Is the Simple Way to Update Your Hairstyle

It’s the noncommittal way to embrace shorter lengths.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Short haircuts such as a pixie crop or a jaw-skimming bob never fail to make an impact, creating a beauty mood that feels instantly fresh while bringing out the very best of your natural hair texture.

But if you’re not ready to take the plunge or fancy a more gradual change, take a leaf out of Taylor Swift’s book with a “midi” bob and a fringe to add structure and interest. Swift spent a majority of the last few years with long hair, so getting incremental trims is not only a great way to remove any dry or split ends, but it also helps ease you into a shorter haircut.

“The midi bob sits in that sweet spot between a classic bob and longer hair. It usually falls somewhere between the jawline and collarbone, which gives it a softer, more lived-in feel than a sharper, shorter cut,” says Luke Hersheson, hairstylist and CEO of Hershesons. “What I like about it is that it doesn’t feel too ‘done’ or too precious. Adding a fringe instantly brings another dimension to the shape. It frames the face, breaks up the length, and gives the cut more personality. It’s often that little detail that takes a simple bob and makes it feel individual.”

Hairstylist and founder of Arkive Headcare, Adam Reed, agrees, adding: “It’s the balance between something beautifully classic and something that feels incredibly modern. The bob is one of those haircuts that continually reinvents itself, and I think the midi length is particularly appealing because it doesn’t feel too restrictive.”

The best news about a midi bob with fringe is also the versatility. You can still have it up or down, and it can be easily tailored to every hair type and curl pattern. “The fringe can be adapted too,” adds Hersheson. “Whether that’s a curtain fringe, a softer bottleneck shape, or something fuller, it’s really about finding the version that works with your natural texture rather than fighting against it.”

As with all haircuts, the main consideration should be to understand how your hair naturally behaves. “A fringe sits front and center, so things like cowlicks, growth patterns, and texture matter,” says Hersheson.

He adds: “You also need to think about the relationship between the fringe and the rest of the cut. A bob can sometimes feel quite graphic, so I often prefer a fringe that has a bit of softness and movement through it. It keeps the overall look modern and wearable.”

If you’re new to fringes, try a longer curtain fringe or a soft side-swept style as they are more versatile, shares Reed. Fringes do require some maintenance and regular trimming, so tell your hairdresser to tailor it to your lifestyle. “My biggest piece of advice is to bring references, but allow your hairdresser to interpret them for your hair rather than trying to recreate somebody else’s fringe exactly,” Reed tells ELLE.

There is no one-size-fits-all when it comes to hair, but Hersheson shares his main tips to ensure a polished yet relaxed finish, no matter your hair type.

Curly hair: Don’t try to force curls into a bob shape they don’t want to be. Work with the natural movement and focus on creating a strong silhouette. The Almost Everything Cream and a bit of diffused drying will usually give you a much better result than over-styling. Fringes on curly hair can look brilliant when they’re slightly looser and less perfect.

Thick hair: It’s all about using the weight of your hair to your advantage. Internal layers can stop the shape from becoming triangular while still keeping that fullness people love. When styling, don’t over-smooth it. A little texture is what makes a midi bob feel modern.

Straight hair: This hair type spotlights the silhouette of a sharper bob, but if your hair is prone to looking flat, add texture through the ends or use a texture spray to take away that freshly styled finish. The goal is for it to look like you’ve lived in it for a day or two.

Fine hair: A midi bob can actually make fine hair appear much thicker because you’re concentrating the weight into a stronger shape. Keep products light and avoid anything too heavy around the roots. A soft fringe can create the illusion of fullness around the front and make the entire haircut feel more substantial.

#6772. Seiko’s Affordable Hideo Kojima Watches Are a Gamer's Dream—but There's a Catch

The Japanese brand has teamed up with Kojima Productions to celebrate its 10th anniversary with two limited-edition timepieces.

If you're a fan of Hideo Kojima's video games, you might want to take a look at Seiko's later collaboration. The watchmaker has teamed up with Kojima Productions, the studio behind Death Stranding, to release two limited-edition watches celebrating its 10th anniversary, both exclusive to Japan.

And, in keeping with Kojima's rather distinctive creative universe, these aren't your typical Seikos.

The new Ludens watches come in two colourways, black and white, with just 300 of each available. Both take inspiration from Ludens, the futuristic, spacesuit-wearing mascot of Kojima Productions, whose illustration takes pride and place on the dial alongside the studio's motto From Sapiens to Ludens.

The white edition features a silver-and-white dial paired with a patterned white strap, while the black version offers a darker alternative. Turn either watch over and you'll find an engraved illustration of Ludens on the case back, alongside the Seiko logo and an individual serial number.

As for what's inside, both watches are powered by Seiko's battery-operated 6N01 quartz movement, which offers an accuracy of approximately 15 seconds per month and a battery life of around three years. There's also a stainless steel case, Hardlex crystal, and everyday water resistance.

The black edition is priced at ¥55,000 (approximately $350), while the white version costs ¥57,600 (around $365). The latter also comes with a ticket to Kojima Productions' Beyond the Strand 10th-anniversary exhibition, taking place at Tokyo's Azabudai Hills Gallery until December 20.

Pre-orders opened in Japan on October 30 through Lawson and HMV&BOOKS, with reservations running until December 14, unless all 300 pieces sell out first.

But here's the catch. Despite being available to pre-order now, neither watch is expected to arrive until September 30 2027. That's almost eleven months of waiting for a watch that's already rather difficult to get hold of, particularly if you're shopping from outside Japan.

Still, for diehard Kojima fans, a limited-edition Seiko celebrating a decade of one of gaming's most recognizable studios might just be worth the wait.

#6771. Pastel Hues and Big Jewelry: Decoding The Gilded Age Season 4’s Fashion

The new season of The Gilded Age uses fashion as a powerful storytelling method.

Everyone’s favorite HBO period drama about New York high society at the turn of the 20th century is back for a fourth season. In The Gilded Age, all the main characters (played by Carrie Coon, Christine Baranski, Audra McDonald, Cynthia Nixon, and Morgan Spector) are back. But this time, their outfits are just a little bit different. As old and new money continue to clash, the gowns, jewelry, and glamour speak louder than ever before. The outfits also show where the characters are mentally, more than ever. Within the first episode, we’ve already seen marriage, birth, and daring tensions, all dramatically dressed and detailed.

For the series, costume designer Kasia Walicka-Maimone takes inspiration from the real changing fashion of New York City from the 1870s to the 1890s: a time when trends and silhouettes were rapidly evolving. Walicka-Maimone and her team look to paintings, photography, and fashion plates from the period to inform the grand gowns and refined suiting. “We are seeing more artistic license in the dress designs this season, with traditional elements mixed with modern ones like the sleeveless top Marian wore to the after-wedding party downtown at Webster Hall,” Elizabeth L. Block, author of the Gilded Age Fashion book and newsletter, tells ELLE. “The juxtapositions are fun, maybe even more so than the one-to-one matchups with actual Gilded Age dresses we saw in the earlier seasons.”

Even if we’re early in the season, it’s fascinating to see color tell such a story through clothing. Socialite sisters Agnes van Rhijn (Christine Baranski) and Ada (Cynthia Nixon) are at the center of the show, and in the new season, their looks reflect a slightly more joyful representation of their characters. Think: embroidered details and luscious hues. “Aunt Ada is loosening up,” adds Block. “She is fully out of mourning for her husband, and we are seeing her in lighter color combinations like pink and green. For a while last season she was all buttoned up in black and deep blues.” Gleaming buttons and lace trims allude to the fact that these sisters have access to the best of the best textiles.

On the same wavelength, Peggy (Denée Benton) and her mother, Mrs. Scott (Audra McDonald), demonstrate through their clothing that they have money, but most importantly, taste. There’s a short conversation about Brussels bobbin lace that is quite revealing, however. (Consuelo Vanderbilt famously wore a Brussels lace veil for her glorious 1895 wedding to the Duke of Marlborough.) Their highly ornate outfits with richly detailed trims and embellishments are unrivaled.

Still, sisters Agnes and Ada are more toned down than someone like Bertha Russell (Carrie Coon) and her railroad tycoon husband George Russell (Morgan Spector) or Peggy and her family, who have all always used clothing as armor and a communication tool. Despite seeing Bertha dressed down early in the episode during Gladys’s (Taissa Farmiga) giving birth, she later comes to fruition with diamond tiaras, puffed and beaded sleeves, and embellished full-skirted ball gowns.

A major fashion moment comes when Enid Winterton (Kelley Curran) and Oscar van Rhijn (Blake Ritson) get married. Enid wears an opulent gown embroidered with flowers in a soft shade of pinkish lavender, with matching gloves and an ornate pink floral headpiece to match. As Block says, it’s a “pinky-lavender dress for a lavender marriage,” and “The ensemble immediately reads as incongruous but elegant and playful, like the two characters, who are now fan favorites.” With a flash of pale green and a long train, the look is a nod to the stereotype of the idea of new money being flashier than old money. Enid’s wedding look is the equivalent of a fashion debut for her in a way, and later on, we see her in a pink silk trimmed with sumptuous fur, accented with a pearl necklace and earrings.

We also see Marian Brook (Louisa Jacobson) emerge even more as an example of a young woman who’s interested in dressing differently. Her vivid mint green and black striped gown with draped details was incredibly visually striking and felt new and bold, while her matching hat felt like a nod to the kind of more mature, old-money style of her aunts. She also wears a blue lace gown with velvet shoulder details and matching opera gloves, revealing more skin than the old-money characters in the show.

If you’re a jewelry (or accessories) lover, it’s the season to watch. Enid’s spectacular diamond necklace and whimsical approach to hats covered in bows and feathers are gasp-worthy and definitely among the most entertaining parts of the show. In The Gilded Age season 4, the fashion is vital to the drama.

#6770. Martine Rose Is Balenciaga’s New Creative Director—and the First Woman to Lead the House

The London designer consulted on menswear at the brand under Demna’s tenure.

Balenciaga is entering a new era with a familiar face. Martine Rose, the British-Jamaican designer who served as a menswear consultant under Demna at the brand, is officially taking over the post following Pierpaolo Piccioli’s departure. The news was confirmed by Balenciaga’s parent company, Kering, on Friday.

“It is a true privilege to be appointed Creative Director of Balenciaga, a House with an extraordinary heritage and a fearless creative spirit,” Rose said in a statement. “Balenciaga has been shaped by remarkable creative talents before me. I am committed to honoring this legacy and look forward to creating the next chapter in this rich and beautiful story.”

While Rose might be a new face to the mainstream, she’s something of an industry darling among those in the know. Born in South London, she launched her namesake menswear label in 2007, drawing on 1990s counterculture and youth scenes, hip-hop, reggae, and football. Her design philosophy challenges conventional ideas of masculinity and gives familiar wardrobe staples an unexpected twist: She might stretch a suit’s proportions or turn sportswear into something sharper and more formal.

Rose, however, is more than a cult designer. Her clothes have found fans in Hailey Bieber, Timothée Chalamet, and Rihanna. In 2021, she sold a 60 percent stake in her brand to London-based fashion accelerator Tomorrow and has sidestepped the traditional fashion calendar for more community-focused showings. Her appointment at Balenciaga brings her back to the house and is also history-making: She is the first woman to lead the brand creatively.

From 2015 to 2018, Rose was brought on by Demna as a menswear consultant, helping launch Balenciaga’s first menswear collections and shape the oversized proportions and brutalist, severe aesthetic that became a signature of his tenure. Her influence on contemporary menswear has earned her the moniker “Your Favorite Designer’s Favorite Designer” among insiders.

“Demna was so brave in giving me a real shot,” Rose told British Vogue in 2021. “Up until that point, I was basically squatting,” she said. “It gave me the chance to actually do something, to put it all back into my own label and make a proper jump forward. It also gave me confidence.”

“Balenciaga has always been a house of radical creativity, challenging conventions and redefining the boundaries of fashion,” Luca de Meo, CEO of Kering, explained. “Martine Rose embodies this spirit with a fresh, fearless, and deeply contemporary vision. Her connection to the new generation and her instinctive understanding of culture bring a new dimension to the house. Her appointment reflects our ambition to keep Balenciaga at the forefront of innovation, creativity, and culture.”

Rose will be based in Paris and begin her role on November 16; the date of her first show has not yet been announced.

#6769. According to Judi Dench, the Biggest James Bond Baddie Was Daniel Craig All Along

It turns out he was behind some of the last few Bond films' most shocking moves.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Daniel Craig’s much-celebrated time as 007 wasn’t just bad news for the world’s supervillains—the good guys dropped like flies, too.

After Craig joined the franchise in 2006’s Casino Royale, the legendary Judi Dench, who had played M since 1995’s GoldenEye, lasted just three films before kicking it. And as we all know—spoiler alert, just in case you haven’t seen the end of Craig’s 007 journey—Bond himself met an explosive end in No Time to Die. It turns out you can thank Craig for both plot twists.

Craig has been vocal about his desire to kill off 007 in the past, but in her new memoir, titled Is It Too Late to Make a Run for It?, Dame Judi Dench shared that he also had a hand in her own character's demise.

"I was taken to the Wolseley restaurant by Barbara [Broccoli], Michael [G. Wilson] and Sam [Mendes]—I don't think Daniel Craig was there—and they outlined the plot and told me they were going to bump me off," she revealed.

But the crucial new information is this: "Apparently, it was Daniel's idea to have me killed," Dame Dench revealed.

Ruthless stuff, but in fairness, it did result in one of the series's most impactful moments. Will the next Bond actor—which, of course, is an extremely hot conversation at the moment—get the same opportunity to decide his co-stars' fates? You would not have thought so. The next M, whoever it may be, can breathe a big sigh of relief.

#6768. How Club Kid Fashion Went From the Dance Floor to the Runway

The underground scene’s DIY, gender-bending style still shapes how we get dressed today.

For his fall/winter 2009 runway show, “The Horn of Plenty,” Alexander McQueen offered a satirical critique of fashion’s throwaway culture. Models navigated a runway strewn with glass and discarded props in houndstooth looks and silhouettes that mocked fashion’s excess. Their alabaster-white painted faces and exaggerated lips evoked Leigh Bowery, the Australian performance artist known for his audacious club looks. “It’s a sackable offense, this collection,” McQueen said post-show. “I want people to look at it and say, ‘What the hell is that?’”

Much like McQueen upended fashion with his confrontational approach to design, Club Kids—a name given to the scene’s most flamboyant clubgoers, Bowery included—did the same in cities like New York and London. The scene was rife with larger-than-life, sometimes debaucherous personalities expressing themselves through equally outsized clothes; no look was too outlandish, no party too wild. It was that DIY spirit, championed by the likes of Susanne Bartsch, a nightlife impresario, RuPaul, and the infamous Michael Alig, that caught on beyond the clubs, creating an inextricable connection between nightlife and fashion. “You didn’t have to wait for a designer or a magazine to tell you what was fashionable,” Bartsch tells ELLE. “You could create a look, walk into the room, and see what happened. The crowd became the show, and the dance floor became a runway with no fixed rules.”

“Susanne, when she moved to New York, she opened a boutique and was the first person to carry clothes by [John] Galliano and [Jean Paul] Gaultier, [really] introducing that type of style into the city,” explains Ryan Burke, a New York-based makeup artist. “And then she also threw these parties; it went really hand in hand.”

Bartsch’s legendary parties at Savage and the Copacabana brought together a true amalgamation of worlds, from downtown denizens to fashion heavyweights like Thierry Mugler and Gaultier. (Like McQueen, Gaultier referenced Bowery in a 1991 runway show.) It was not only an intermixing of punchy, confident aesthetics, but also the idea that fashion can be an escape and the ultimate form of self-expression. My club nights in the ’80s brought together artists, designers, photographers, performers, and kids creating their own looks,” Bartsch notes. “For someone who has felt judged or excluded, that can be powerful. The [dancefloor] gives you permission to try something on not just an outfit, but a version of yourself.”

Meanwhile, in London, the Blitz Kids channeled that same appetite for theatrical self-expression through their DIY, gender-bending looks, which would influence collections by Marc Jacobs (most famously, his grunge collection for Perry Ellis), McQueen, and Anna Sui.

The AIDS crisis took an enormous toll on the communities that made New York nightlife pulse. Then came New York’s tough-on-crime crackdown, which helped drive many of the most popular clubs out of business. Today, the Club Kids’ heyday has the glow of downtown legend: a moment when self-expression ruled the dance floor and anyone could make an entrance. Now, Burke notes that social media has changed how people create and share their looks, making the heyday of the Club Kids feel all the more singular. (In film, the scene’s legacy has been in 2003’s Chloë Sevigny-starring Party Monster and is now slyly resurfacing in Jordan Firstman’s new film, Club Kid, where he plays a club promoter whose life is upended by the arrival of the 10-year-old son he never knew he had.)

“People are really crafty, really crafty online; they create, document it, take it off,” he says. “That’s cool too; any kind of way people want to express themselves is great, but there was something that was really magical about seeing people out. They tried more interesting things that might not work or might not look that good, but they tried it.”

The Club Kid spirit still reverberates in the underground scenes of New York, London, and beyond—and at the highest levels of fashion. You just need to know where to look.

During the recent spring/summer 2027 season, when many designers celebrated the “joy of getting dressed,” the club was never too far away. At Junya Watanabe, pearls and punk hardware were stacked atop catsuits with the carefree abandon of the original club kids, who built their looks from punk boutiques and thrift-store finds. Diesel got hot and heavy: Glenn Martens’s swan song for the brand unfolded amid a crowd of performers kissing and touching, while models wore barely-there denim and clothes that looked hastily thrown on, finished with smudged red lipstick, as if they’d been smooching on the dance floor. And August Barron showed a starlet-inspired lineup of glitzy “going out” dresses, sandals with neon heels, and haphazardly layered separates.

It’s easy to see why fashion keeps returning to the club: Nowhere else makes getting dressed feel quite so alive. As Bartsch puts it: “For me, the club spirit isn’t one particular look, it’s freedom, imagination and the confidence to express yourself without asking permission.”

#6767. The Denim Trends You’ll Be Seeing All Winter Long

These four styles are about to dominate the street style on your FYP.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Let’s talk denim. You probably already have a solid rotation of jeans in your closet (and if you don’t, what in the world have you been wearing this fall?), but it never hurts to add to it. And you’re going to want to add to it, especially because this winter’s styles are going to be so good.

We’re not just talking low-rise or bootcut; we’re getting specific with it. Don’t worry, there’s a trend out there for everyone. If you’re feeling bold, you might find yourself taking an interest in the animal-print barrel-leg styles (Mango’s Bambi jeans have been ever-popular this season) that we saw striding into runway shows this fall. Or, perhaps, Chloé’s ultra-pleated denim has the dramatic flair that you’ve been coveting. On the other hand, if you’re looking for a subtle style to bring into your wardrobe folds, an inconspicuously ripped pair or a baggy low-rise cut might be the newness you needed.

We’ve found the four denim trends set to take over this winter, so get ahead of the game and start shopping for a pair today. With a novelty jacket and a square-toe boot, your trend-forward outfit will be complete.

We’re not talking full 2000s shredded jeans; rather, we’re seeing a slight distressing that adds a casual worn-in feel to a new pair. In bootcut, straight, relaxed, and wide-leg silhouettes, this subtle rip is taking over the streets this season.

Both low-rise and baggy denim aren’t anything new, but we’re seeing both trends combined this winter, creating a slouchy, nonchalant vibe. We would recommend pairing them with a cropped puffer or fitted jacket so that the bagginess doesn’t overwhelm your figure.

Chloé put an avant-garde spin on pleated pants, going for a drop-waist denim with a voluminous leg. We’re digging the exaggerated pleats, and we’ve found similarly dramatic designs across price points (in case the $2,200 price tag isn’t speaking to you).

Take a ride on the wild ride with these animal-print barrel-leg pairs. We’ve been loving the prints—cheetah, zebra, cow, leopard, and so on—and the distinct barrel silhouette, so naturally, blending the two trends together is really working for us.

#6766. How Loyal Is I Play Rocky to Sylvester Stallone’s Real Story?

“This is what he went through,” lead actor Anthony Ippolito tells Esquire.

Before Rocky Balboa climbed the steps of the Philadelphia Museum of Art, and long before he ever came back to defeat Apollo Creed, the iconic boxer was just an idea in Sylvester Stallone’s head. The 30-year-old was basically homeless and struggling to make it as an actor. But he wrote a good script for a moving and honest sports drama—one that he would also reportedly demand to star in or it would never see the light of day.

That’s how much Stallone believed in his work, and now the story of his perseverance has been adapted into a full feature biopic titled I Play Rocky. The film, out in theaters on Friday, November 6, stars Anthony Ippolito (The Offer) as a young Stallone who is desperately trying to get Rocky in the right hands. Once he does, he can tell that the United Artists film studio wants it badly enough to let him play the lead, turning down offers of millions along the way just so he can secure himself a future in Hollywood.

While many biopics fudge the details, most of I Play Rocky is spot-on. He conceived the story after watching a 15-round bout in 1975 between heavyweight champion Muhammad Ali and Chuck Wepner. He turned down massive sums of cash as the studio tried to buy him out from starring in the film, and he was forced to temporarily sell his dog just to afford food for himself. It’s even true that he starred in a softcore porn film, The Party at Kitty and Stud's, where he was paid $200 for two days of work and full-frontal. As Stallone told Playboy in the late ‘70s, “It was either do that movie or rob someone because I was at the very end of my rope.”

If there’s anything I Play Rocky stretches the truth about, it’s that Stallone didn’t write the script in a mad flurry. “I let it incubate for ten months,” he told Forbes in 2017. But the rest—even the scene that shows Stallone punching frozen meat until his fists bleed because the meat hadn’t thawed yet—is all how it really happened.

Plus, Ippolito’s impression of Stallone is one of the best performances we’ve ever seen in a biopic. After seeing the film, Stallone even told Ippolito that it was like looking in a mirror. And as the actor told Esquire last month, his journey to land the role in I Play Rocky wasn’t that different from Stallone’s original pursuit.

“Nobody was asking me to audition for this,” Ippolito said. “I read an article that Peter Farrelly was doing this movie and I just thought, I’d love to try to get in a conversation for that. I sent in a tape that I recorded in my grandmother’s basement, because it was very much stuck in the ’70s and just felt period correct. Then, when I got my hands on the script, I devoured it and sent another tape. You feel sometimes like you’re sending your stuff into an abyss, especially when nobody’s asking for the thing in the first place.”

Eventually, Ippolito was called in and it all took off from there. “I just hope that the film resonates with people. Rocky was so impactful because it’s the underdog story, and this kind of angle is very much true to his real life. This is what he went through, and it’s pretty remarkable.”

#6764. MobLand Season 2, Episode 8 Takes a Devastating Turn

Conrad’s grip on reality is beginning to slip.

Last week I wrote a giddy hagiography of Frankie Cooper. It’s embarrassing how much I like him. I really should grow up, be a man, maintain some critical distance, and write in wry asides like a real English journalist.

But I can’t help it. He’s such an irresistibly murderous bastard that he makes me want to kill a cat just to see how it feels. (The lawyers have advised me to clarify that I don’t actually want to kill a cat; no cats were harmed in the making of this opinion.)

Well, Frankie, it’s been lovely. But there’s a new psychopath in town. Of course the Medusa had a vengeful son by the name of “Mad Dog”! Just out of prison for murder, he’s seen CCTV footage of Eddie, who was there when his daddy was killed, and he’s coming to the Cotswolds.

“I’ll find them, Daddy,” says Kelly, pouring a bottle of whisky over his father’s coffin.

But all that’s for episode 9. We’ve got the admin of episode 8 to sort through first.

Remember Harry’s words at the end of episode 7? “Is that Michael Cooper? At 3 a.m. you’re gonna get clapped.”

Well, Harry did indeed have Frankie Cooper’s boy killed. But not quite so easily as we were first led to believe. He wanted to fuck with Frankie in the process. Harry knew that Michael would call his dad, who has Newgate Prison sewn up tighter than a dodgy tailor’s pocket. Frankie therefore arranged for Michael to be moved to the solitary wing, where he would be safe.

But as Harry explains to Kevin: “What Frankie’s forgot is that a bent screw is a bent screw… So if the CM’s [custodial manager] gonna take a tenner, what’s he gonna do for a twenty? Or in this case, 200 grand in a black bag in the dead of night on a pissing-down Thursday.”

Harry then pays a couple of hired goons to string Michael up in his cell. Frankie is not going to be happy.

It’s time we gave Emmett J. Scanlan his flowers . He’s been ticking over in the background, doing a solid, likable job as the Harrigan dogsbody Paul. But he is changing now and emerging like a butterfly into the show’s comic sideline. There’s a real touch of the Baldricks about him, with a stroke of Hamlet’s gravedigger—the loyal dogsbody who gets lumbered with the shitty jobs and stays funny in spite of it all.

The poor sod thinks he’s in for the noose after the Sicily debacle of episode 7. He’s begging for his life, so desperate that he thinks VIP tickets to Rod Stewart might save him. But no. They were never going to kill Paul. It was merely a simple lampoon. They want him to cut up Donnie’s stinking body in a shipping container.

So Paul strips down to his leopard-print pants and, swinging his hips to the Rolling Stones’ “Mr Charm,” sets about chopping Donnie into vacuum-packable “bricks.” It’s all very Tom Cruise in Risky Business, but with more blood and less hairbrush. Long live Paul.

Eddie’s sinking deeper into the soup—and it’s all of his own doing. If only he weren’t—in Kevin’s words—such a “lazy, spoilt, entitled little cunt.” Maeve finds him in the sauna. He tells her their “situation” isn’t going to work.

“Maeve, I fear our relationship is sailing into choppy water. You’re 80. I’m barely legal.”

Maeve feigns surprise. “This approach, this ploy. It’s depraved. And deeply optimistic.”

Conrad, Eddie explains, is going to “piss his kegs.” Suddenly, so is Eddie, but not out of laughter. “Please don’t tell him; I’ll do anything.”

She agrees, but only if Eddie apologizes. On his knees. To her phone camera. It’s all fun and games for Maeve. I suspect we haven’t seen the last of that video.

Jan agrees to meet Harry. She wants to come clean, to pour out her heart. She tries to explain that she had a brief affair with a man who was using her to get to Harry and Conrad. But Harry already knows.

“That’s my job,” he tells her. “To anticipate danger. Control outcomes. Minimize unwanted consequences.”

She asks him about killing. About the nightmares. He says he doesn’t have any. You get the feeling Harry is all out of nightmares. Then he offers to help Jan and Gina run. Forever.

“I can make that happen. Tonight,” he tells her. “You never have to see me again.”

“What about you?” she asks. “Does no part of you want that?”

For a moment, Harry almost seems human. Almost.

It’s clear now: Conrad is losing his marbles. He wanders into the woods, calling out for his dead dogs. Then he stops, surrounded by swirling mists and squawking crows, and seems to realize just how far he’s slipping. He summons Kevin to the Cotswolds, to which the latter reluctantly submits. Conrad tells his son he’s seen a doctor. He wants to talk succession plans. Kevin, astonished at being asked such a question, reels furiously through the options.

First, Eddie: “A lazy, entitled psychopath who I wouldn’t trust to light the barbecue.”

Then Seraphina: “She would make a world-class businesswoman, but a kingpin? I don’t think she’s ever even touched a gun.”

And then, just as Kevin is about to pitch himself, Conrad reveals what’s really going on. “I see the dead,” he hisses with a haunted, hounded look, the air of the ancient mariner. “They come to me at night. I see them in the woods. All the bodies, in the clothes they died in, their wounds fresh. Standing there, staring at me. They’re in my head, they’re in my soul.”

It’s all very King Lear. They embrace and it’s lovely.

Kevin goes to get the old man a brandy to calm his nerves. When he returns, the fear is gone from Conrad’s eyes. In its place: pure steel. And a shotgun pointed at Kevin’s head.

“If you breathe a word of this to Bella, I’ll kill you both,” he growls.

Conrad is another man again. As though the whole confession were just a performance. But it wasn’t. It was a glimpse of the terrifying disintegration of a man who can no longer distinguish between his different selves.

Kevin can’t believe it. “Pull the trigger then,” he shouts snottily.

“Fuck off now, Kevin,” Conrad says. And, like a beaten dog (though of course, Conrad would never treat a dog in this way), off Kevin fucks.

Well, we’ve spent most of this episode wondering about Frankie’s response to the murder of his son. But here we are. Harry calls Frankie. He wants to reach a treaty. He’s killed Frankie’s son, and now he hopes Frankie will back down.

Here we see a side to Frankie we haven’t seen before: crying, blubbering, a human man in real pain. He’s looking through old photographs of Michael.

“He’s dead, Harry,” he says through snot and tears. “Why did you have to do that? I can’t explain it. The pain.”

“It’s the game,” says Harry, heavy as stone.

To which Frankie replies: “Anyway, I’m sure you understand. After all, Harry, you’re a father too. Ain’tcha?”

It’s a less-than-veiled threat; Frankie’s message: this war isn’t over until the babies are all dead. He’s going after Gina.

And so we end the episode in truly miserable style. A montage of sadness, soundtracked by Johnny Cash’s cover of “Ain’t No Grave”:

The Harrigans have made a lot of enemies over the past eighteen episodes. And now, at last, the dead are joining the queue.

#6763. Sweatpant Jeans Are the Most Controversial (and Comfortable) Denim Trend

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Level up your casual looks with these cozy and stylish picks.

We all remember the jeggings era—it was a traumatic one. But believe it or not, the same concept has come back around in a fresher, more stylish manner. Typically cut from cotton terry cloth or a lightweight Tencel blend, “sweatpant jeans” combine the appearance of real pants with the comfort of loungewear for a result that you really have to see to believe. Plus, most of our favorite styles are shoppable on Amazon.

Don’t let the jeggings of the early 2010s deter you; today’s versions look far more realistic. Take Rag & Bone’s viral Miramar silhouette, which kicked off the trend, as proof. With a faux zip fly, button closure, and rivets, it’ll fool even the most discerning eye—even Oprah loves them. They’re ELLE editor-approved, too. Associate fashion commerce editor Meg Donohue loves the drawstring iteration, seen below.

To help you navigate the category, we’ve rounded up nine of the best sweatpant jeans below. From long travel commutes to casual Fridays, these comfortable bottoms can truly handle it all.

Rag & Bone has made variations of its viral Miramar sweatpant jeans for years, and they remain the gold standard to this day. Printed to look like a pair of faded medium-wash jeans, this wide-leg style feels just right for the current season.

Prefer a slightly more polished style? Made with DL1961’s latest fabric innovation, a lightweight cotton-tencel blend, these easy-going baggy jeans are the perfect solution.

This striped version of Rag & Bone’s viral sweatpant jeans was recently featured in Oprah’s Favorite Things. Should you be seeking a cozy gift for someone stylish, this is a great pick.

Drawing inspiration from denim washes, these Z Supply pants offer an elevated take on traditional sweats. Snag the coordinating button-up overshirt (cut from the same plush French terry material) for the ultimate cozy travel outfit.

Cut from cotton-lyocell chambray, these drapey Favorite Daughter pants work particularly well in warmer weather. The elastic waistband and wide-leg silhouette make them comfortable enough for long commutes, too.

Gap’s French terry baggy jeans are another viral favorite, so naturally, we had to try them ourselves. “Paired with a biker jacket, leather flats, and a studded belt, these pants looked suspiciously like regular ol’ blue jeans—all while providing the soft, stretchy comfort of sweatpants,” commerce editor Lauren Gruber wrote in her review. “It’s a solid, affordable alternative that offers the illusion of denim with the right styling.”

Sizes: 2XS-2XL in Regular, Tall, and Petite

Colors: Soft Cornflower Blue, Tapestry Navy Blue Wash, Black Wash

It’s hard to believe these Faherty pants are made from stretchy cotton terry fabric and not traditional denim—unless you’re the one wearing them, of course. The patch pockets and zip fly are quite deceptive.

For sweatpants jeans that are elevated enough for the office or dinner, this new style from Frame won’t miss. A particularly refined take on the balloon pant trend, they feature an airy yet slim silhouette and flattering high waist that make them easy to style with everything from a silk cami to a crisp-button and blazer.

Darting at the knees and a slightly tapered hem give this relaxed-fit style a subtle barrel-leg silhouette. Grommet details at the waistband and a deep stone wash only add to its edgy, laidback appeal.

#6762. The Black Doves Season 2 Finale Has an Insane Cliffhanger

Let’s talk about Helen Webb and what it all means for season 3.

After a two-year wait, Netflix's Christmas crime caper Black Doves is finally back for its second outing. It's a messy delight that tests the loyalties of the central characters in ways the first season never dared and—both annoyingly and excitingly—ends on just as agonizing a cliffhanger.

In the first season, Elizabeth Reed (Sarah Lancashire) was something of a mystery: an ominous, witty controller of sorts who kept Helen Webb (Keira Knightley) and Sam Young (Ben Whishaw) on their toes. But this time around, Black Doves spends some time unravelling her past, as well as that of the titular intelligence agency: a backstory that informs and explains the major threat of the season, the German anarchist organization Kestrel.

Though the secrets reveal themselves slowly, a bombshell-filled (pun very much intended) finale crashes through a series of twists that—if you didn't see them coming (which I can't say I did, for some of them)—will really feel like a stab in the face. No, not the back, because unfortunately the answers were always lying right in front of us.

There's a lot to break down from that last episode, so read on for our recap of Black Doves season 2 and an explanation of the ending—including that twist that'll probably have a drastic impact on where we'll be at the beginning of season 3.

The penultimate episode ended with a bunch of crossed wires, conflicting contracts, and potential deaths. Eleanor and Williams have taken out paid hits on Laila and Sam, while Sam's final target in his prison-break contract with Kestrel is none other than Reed (after episode 4 revealed that she is in fact the mysterious “Wildflower” of the files she tried to take from Alexi Chernov).

The main action kicks off when the precious international peace summit that Wallace (Andrew Buchan) has spent the whole season planning gets upended before it even starts. Wallace and Helen's cab is intercepted and chased, while U. S. defense envoy Cecile Mason (Neve Campbell) and recently widowed Russian envoy Katia Chernov (Sylvia Hoeks) are captured by strange armed men.

When Patrick (Sam Riley), the Kestrel man who's been blackmailing Sam all season, escorts Reed back into the Black Doves office with a gun to her head, it quickly becomes clear that 'Wildflower', or Reed, is the only target Kestrel wants brought in alive. Or, at least, whoever's running it does.

And there's the twist: after a horrid little hostage situation in the office results in the deaths of quite a few senior Black Doves and Mason, Katia reveals that she is in fact the head of Kestrel and, in turn, Vita—the long-lost, abandoned daughter that Reed left in Berlin after killing her fake husband and running off (ouch).

Dani, the nuisance of a secretary Reed planted in Wallace's office to seduce him and potentially replace Helen, has ended up being recruited by Kestrel. But she doesn't last long after a game of grenade hot potato played by Eleanor, Williams, and Laila ends with Dani being caught in a lift with it. Even Reed's capable of mistakes.

Meanwhile, over in the car park where Helen and Wallace end up after fleeing, Mr. Prime Minister finally decides to question his wife about her past and her secrets. Obviously, she lies again—until the situation shifts a little.

Helen receives a series of ominous messages and threatening instructions, first over text, then through a discreet earpiece planted in her car. Katia, whom Helen very quickly realizes is not an ally, instructs her to kill Wallace and turn herself in to MI5. But what's the leverage, you ask?

Marie, the housekeeper who was teased as a Black Dove at the end of the first season, has in fact been working for Kestrel this whole time. If you're a babysitter to the Prime Minister's spy wife, who's been planted by a capitalist intelligence agency, but you're working for a German anarchist organization, does that make you a double or a triple agent? A nightmare for Mrs Webb, more than anything.

Helen tricks Katia over the phone by shooting past Wallace towards the window, explains (on a surface level) that the children are in danger and she needs to hijack the car, and darts off with Sam to rescue the children from Marie.

After confronting Marie, Helen shoots her in the head and flees the scene, while Reed reconciles (not quite) with her bleeding-out daughter in her last moments. Kestrel's down and all is well? Obviously not, because it's never that simple.

MI5 is even more suspicious of Helen than ever before, but Wallace is covering for her. Conteh, as one of the few surviving senior members of the Black Doves, restores Reed to her former standing.

Helen and Sam reconcile properly after their heated argument during the first Reed rescue mission of the season, and he reveals that he's going to leave the country once again. After they part, a strange man bumps into Helen and, BOOM, final twist! Patrick has escaped in perfect condition and Kestrel is still going strong. He leaves Helen with his card, more as a threat than anything else.

Reed and Helen agree to increase Helen's standing and role within the Black Doves, meaning that she'll be more than just an intel-grabber going forward, and putting her in a pretty good spot work-wise. However, things aren't quite so simple at home.

Wallace confronts Helen once again about her real name (Lily Morris), and the finale ends with what appears to be the beginning of a conversation about her past. But how much is Helen actually going to tell Wallace? There's only so much she can give away without completely compromising her position, but she'll have to say enough to regain his trust. We'll find out in two years, I suppose.

#6760. Olivia Rodrigo Embraces the Holiday Spirit on ‘Take Me Home (4 Christmas)’

With just 50 days left until Christmas, Olivia Rodrigo is getting into the holiday spirit early with the arrival of her first official festive single, “Take Me Home (4 Christmas).”

The track, released on November 6, sees the pop star celebrating both the holiday and the joy she gets from having the right person by her side to enjoy the festivities. In her own words, the song—written with her frequent collaborators, Dan Nigro and Amy Allen—captures “the excitement and nerves of spending the holidays with someone you love.”

Rodrigo turns a cheeky eye to holiday traditions on the track. Instead of simply embracing the festivities of the season, like Christmas carols and gift exchange, she sees it as an opportunity to get commitment out of the object of her affections. “Take me home to your parents’ house/ I want you to take me home/Show me serious commitment,” she sings on the chorus of the track.

The pop star also fantasizes about what future holidays with her partner could look like, and has several activities on her agenda for future Christmases: “Wanna get drunk with your mother and father/I’d be the coolest in-law daughter/Hungover brunch with you and your sister/Your exes got the summer, but boy, I get the winter,” Rodrigo adds.

In addition to dropping the song, Rodrigo also gave the single a Christmas-themed music video starring Sex and the City’s Cynthia Nixon and Saturday Night Live cast member Ben Marshall. In the video, Rodrigo and Marshall show up as each other’s date to a Christmas-themed party hosted by Nixon. Classic holiday activities—such as posing for photos in ugly Christmas sweaters, decorating gingerbread houses, playing charades, and sitting down for an enormous dinner—ensue, and the festivities get so wild that Marshall and a few other guests end up passing out until the next morning. Rodrigo, dressed in a sheer black négligé, is left to clean up the mess left behind.

“Take Me Home (4 Christmas)” isn’t the only Christmas track that the pop star has written. Rodrigo wrote a holiday track called “The Bels” when she was just 5 years old, and released a snippet from the song in 2021. “In honor of Christmas Eve may I present to you my very first Christmas song ‘The Bels’ written by 5 year old me lol. Happy holidays!!!!” she shared at the time.

On Dec. 17, 2022, “The Bels” arrived in full. Rodrigo’s fan hub, LiviesHQ, shared the full version of the track as the last surprise in her 12 days of Livmas.

#6759. Party Blazers, High-End Homewares, and All the New Launches Esquire’s Editors Loved This Week

#6758. Adria Arjona Toasts Her Women in Film Honor With Max Mara at the Chateau Marmont

The Onslaught actress brought ELLE along as she got ready for the meaningful evening.

On Wednesday evening, the Chateau Marmont was in full Hollywood mode as Max Mara gathered friends for a cocktail reception to toast Adria Arjona. The Onslaught actress was named this year’s Women in Film Max Mara Face of the Future, an award recognizing an actress’s achievements in film and television, community contributions, and embodiment of style. (Past honorees include Emily Blunt, Hailee Steinfeld, and Gemma Chan.) With the Women in Film Honors taking place the following night, the gathering offered an intimate start to a larger celebration of women shaping the screen industries.

For what Arjona called an “amazing honor,” she channeled minimal elegance in a sleek black Max Mara gown, its halter neckline bringing a touch of drama to the otherwise understated look. Her longtime partner, Jason Momoa, joined her for the evening, alongside guests including Jessica Alba, Shay Mitchell, Brittany Snow, Zoey Deutch, and Victoria Justice.

The timing feels fitting. Arjona’s recent work in Onslaught, where she plays an army veteran and single mother with PTSD, has expanded her range, and she’ll next appear in The Thomas Crown Affair in March. Away from the set, the Puerto Rican actress has made community work a long-standing commitment, supporting underserved women in downtown Los Angeles for more than a decade and children in Guatemala for 15 years.

From her beauty routine to the final look, plus a car ride filled with friends and family, Arjona brought ELLE behind the scenes as she prepared for the Chateau Marmont celebration.

#6757. Grandmacore Bags Are the Textural Treat Your Winter Wardrobe Needs

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Gather your crochet hooks and embroidery hoops and get ready to embrace fashion’s new era of cozy bags. Needlepoint, embroidery, and tapestry are taking over bags far and wide, from chic clutches to big, oversize carryalls, useful totes, and reimagined duffles. The trend kicked off about a year ago when Valentino launched its DeVain bag in November 2025 as part of its pre-fall 2026 collection under Alessandro Michele. With its beaded details, tapestry textiles, and embroidery, the bag is a love letter to a new kind of grandmacore with a dash of boho.

Much like the tassels that we’ve been seeing so much of lately, the grandmacore bag speaks volumes about tactile, touchable joy in an increasingly digital world. It’s reminiscent of a comfy couch with pillows, or a well-worn blanket that’s been passed down by generations. It’s instant, wearable craft.

Valentino isn’t the only one putting a focus on crafty, comfy bags. Just look at Dries Van Noten. One bag from the fall/winter 2026 collection in particular feels perfectly primed for the grandma-centric fashion universe: an east-west duffel-style tote replete with floral tapestry and a large snakeskin-effect patch. It feels like a wearable textural wonder with an inherent vintage sentiment; something that could have been picked up on travels in a foreign land decades ago and passed down through generations.

Elsewhere, Fendi recently reissued 20 archival versions of the Baguette under chief creative officer Maria Grazia Chiuri, most of them collaged in craft. Think: intense zebra-striped beadwork, mirror appliqué, and floral embroidery made by (and inspired by) South Asian craft. The mirror bag, for example, involves 138 hours of handwork with the Mumbai atelier Chanakya International. All of these bags is clear nods to craft in a world where fashion (and many other things) are lightning fast.

The grandma-ification of bags comes at the perfect time, too. Beyond offering a bit of slowed-down, tactile luxury, these kinds of accessories are also the perfect touch for winter and holiday dressing. There are many ways to wear the look. Silky pouches finished with embroidery by Conner Ives or feather-trimmed shoulder bags from Erdem included. Details like fringe and tassels add extra feeling. When all you see is the coat, let the bag do the talking.

#6756. The Samsung Frame TV Is 25% Off Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Black Friday be damned. This is as good a deal as you’ll see.

Back in the old days of Black Friday shopping, you had to wait outside a big-box store for to score a TV on sale. You had to bring a tent, a friend, and prepare for some sort of physical altercation. Now, thanks to Amazon’s lead-up deals, you can just shop online. We’ve lost something, but there wasn’t much to lose honestly. Samsung’s talked-about Frame TV on sale, without the fistfight. Thanksgiving is still weeks away, and the 65-inch size of the standard 2026 model is already 25 percent off.

You may notice Amazon keeps the sale price hidden until you add the TV to your cart, where it still doesn’t show the original full price—but compare Amazon’s prices with those on Samsung’s website, and you’ll see this early deal is very legit.

The standard Frame TV is great, but honestly, it’s worth spending a bit extra on the 2026 Pro model, which offers premium picture quality and sound. “When you need a TV that decorates your wall like a work of art, The Frame Pro is everything,” writes associate commerce editor Bryn Gelbert. “A glare-proof screen, thin bezel, and partnerships with the Met, MoMA, and a few high-profile artist estates make it the art TV to beat.”

Samsung’s Wireless One Connect box is another key distinction between the standard and Pro models. “This is the real game changer when it comes to aesthetics,” Gelbert notes in his comparison of popular art TVs. “The box and TV communicate wirelessly, so you don’t have to worry about stowing cables out of sight (or in the wall) to get the true Frame experience of passing your TV off as art when your unsuspecting neighbor stops by.”

You might assume older versions of the Frame TV offer significantly better discounts—but as of writing, the 2026 model is only about a hundred bucks more than the 2025 model, so it’s worth upgrading your setup with the newest tech available.

Samsung The Frame Pro TV (2025 Model, 65”)

Will Frame TV prices drop further during Amazon’s official Black Friday sale? Only time will tell—but based on price history during previous sales like Prime Day, we think it’s safe to shop now. The Pro model is already at its lowest price ever, and the standard model is only $50 above its all-time low. Shop early, and you can watch Thanksgiving football in crisp, clear 4K instead of on that piece of crap you’ve got now.

Of note: Amazon doesn’t have any markdowns on the decorative bezels, which are sold separately. Stick with the TV for now, then check back closer to Black Friday and Cyber Monday to see if Samsung drops new deals on Frame TV accessories.

#6755. 7 Best Ways to Care for Gray Hair in the Colder Months, From Boosting Hydration to Restoring Shine

Easy tricks to keep silver strands healthy and hydrated when the temperature drops.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

If red carpets and runways are anything to go by this autumn, silver strands are the trend du jour. Emma Thompson, Olivia Colman, and Sarah Jessica Parker are leading the former camp, each having debuted an elegant gray transformation in recent weeks, while shows such as Lanvin, Akrin, and Andreas Kronthaler for Vivienne Westwood spotlighted gray hair at Paris Fashion Week.

It seems, then, that there’s no better time to embrace gray and silver hair. But for anyone seeking to follow suit, it’s important to note that cooler temperatures can affect the typical upkeep involved. “Cold weather dries out gray hair, making it feel rough, brittle, and more prone to breakage and frizz,” says John Clark, senior colorist at Nicola Clarke salon. “This is because the cold, low-humidity air pulls moisture from the hair, while indoor heating further dries it out.”

Since gray hair indicates a structural change in the hair (as it’s caused by a loss of melanin in the strands), it’s often more prone to dryness, regardless of the weather. Dr. Aamna Adel, founder of Rhute Haircare, explains: “Gray or white hair often has fewer natural lipids and a rougher cuticle, which means it can feel drier and coarser. It also reflects light differently, so it can look dull or wiry.”

She adds that both the texture of the hair and the scalp can change when temperatures drop; flakiness and irritation can be common consequences.

Thankfully, there is a range of easy ways to restore both back into top form. With winter firmly on the horizon, discover the seven swaps and changes you can make to your gray hair routine to best care for it in the weeks ahead.

A healthy scalp will ensure that gray strands are stronger and denser, according to hairstylist and founder of Jo Nova, Joseph Yildiz.

Adds Dr. Adel: “Look for ceramides, fatty acids, shea butter, and panthenol to rebuild the lipid barrier; niacinamide to support scalp health; and antioxidants like vitamin E, stem cell extracts, and green tea extract to protect against oxidative stress, which accelerates graying and dryness. Amino acids and peptides can also help strengthen the hair shaft over time.”

As gray hair naturally feels coarser, the last thing you want is to strip it with shampoos that contain harsh sulfates. Opt for hydrating formulas or cleansing conditioners, and while the hair is damp, apply a leave-in conditioner.

Incorporate a silver shampoo and conditioner once a week. “It dulls any yellow or brassy tones and helps reflect the light, making hair look shiny and glossy,” says Clark.

Alongside this, a clarifying formula used once every two weeks is useful, too, especially if you tend to use a lot of styling products. “It will lightly cleanse any product buildup or mineral deposits in the hair. Sometimes white hair can appear yellow, and this solves that,” says Jo Cockle at John Frieda Salon. Always follow with a bond-building and moisturizing hair mask to keep it strong and soft.

Philip Kingsley Pure Blonde/Silver Shampoo

K18 Full-Size Leave-In Molecular Repair Hair Mask

Avoid stepping outside into the cold right after air-drying your hair. “This can be incredibly damaging to hair that is already prone to dryness. The water in your hair can expand in freezing temperatures, which can lift the hair’s cuticle and lead to split ends,” says Hollie Rose Clarke, hairstylist and Living Proof ambassador.

Instead, she recommends using a hydrating product to help keep hair soft and conditioned while air-drying. Clarke is a fan of Living Proof Air-Dry Styler, which contains hemisqualane for moisture that won’t weigh down fine strands. You can also experiment with a nourishing hair oil such as Moroccanoil’s Treatment, which is brimming with omega-6 for deep hydration.

In the evenings, Dr. Adel recommends wearing a silk or satin-lined scarf or bonnet to prevent friction and static as you sleep.

Replenish lost moisture with cocooning hair masks and treatments. Clark recommends incorporating keratin-based formulas to restore strength, while Clarke name-checks amino acids to increase hair’s hydrophobicity (this ultimately helps to lock in moisture), and hemisqualane to seal the cuticles so locks appear silkier.

Sam McKnight Hair Rich Nourish Conditioner

Ideally, try not to overstyle your hair with heated tools. “This can leave hair broken, dry, and even discolored,” says Clark. But, if you must use them (and most of us do prefer to), shield your strands with a heat protector, even if you’re just running a hair dryer through it. Look for one with hydrating, frizz-managing qualities to ease flyaways.

Wella Ultimate Smooth Miracle Hair Oil Serum

There’s a reason why some may find their grays looking lackluster. “Gray hair is more porous, meaning there is less natural protection from environmental factors (such as urban pollution) than with pigmented hair. This can be what causes a lack of shine or discoloration,” Clarke explains.

Modern shield formulas deliver hydration and protection against both heat tools and environmental damage.

Oribe Invisible Defense Universal Protection Spray

Beauty Works Solaré UV Leave-In Conditioning Mist

As a finishing touch, a small amount of hair oil will add extra shine to your grays, but a glossing treatment delivers luster that lasts. At-home glosses (which are often applied pre-conditioner or in place of it, left for a few minutes, then washed out) promise instant results. You can also use a semipermanent conditioning tint such as Shrine Drop It to revive the color of your grays.

#6754. Trump Has Blamed Everyone for the GOP’s Midterm Meltdown—Except Himself and the Worst Member of His Staff

Maybe the president shouldn’t have told everyone to pretend he was on the ballot.

(Permanent Musical Accompaniment to the Last Post of the Week from the Blog's Favourite Living Canadian)

Wake up, little Susie. The gravy train went off the tracks. From CNN:

So, the president is a) in a rage because his party got annihilated after he bounced around the country telling everyone to pretend he was on the ballot, which millions of Americans cheerfully did, and b) his solution is to put the least popular primate in his employ in charge of the White House. He, of course, was not "among those" Trump blamed for the loss. He never is.

Probably gone. However, the Republican reservoir of otherwise unemployable whackadoos remains bottomless. Meet Anthony Constantino, congresscritter-elect from that part of New York we like to call Baja Canada. Here he is, celebrating his victory.

Ladies and gentlemen, your 2028 Republican National Convention keynote speaker.

Ever since the Great Day of Reckoning dawned, I've been watching a number of interviews with the various new faces that have emerged. Specifically, I listened to James Talarico in Texas and Dr. Annie Andrews in South Carolina. and both of them laid out their wish list for congressional reform. Both of them want a ban on congressional stock trading, an improvement supported by an estimated 125 percent of the American population. They both also expressed support for ... wait for it ... term limits.

Whoa, there, rookies. Kept that baby in your pockets, didn't you?

As is well known around the shebeen, term limits are the third worst idea in American politics. (The first two are a balanced budget amendment to the Constitution and the elected judiciary.) They are a poisoned placebo. You end up with legislators led around by the nose by battalions of lobbyists, who end up empowered as the legislature's institutional memory. And that's not to mention the fact that there ought to be no law or policy that tells me I can't vote for somebody for whom I want to vote, assuming they're not dead or incarcerated, and I'm willing to negotiate on the second one.

On the Supreme Court, I'm all in favor of them. Those are appointed positions, not elected ones, and, back in the day, justices regularly returned to private life and their old clients back home. For example, Justice Benjamin Curtis was on the Court for six years, long enough for him to write his classic dissent in Dred Scott v. Sandford. He then quit over money and because he hated Chief Justice Roger Taney. (Good plan.) He went back to Boston and his previous client list.

(In a very odd twist of fate, Curtis returned to Washington as lead counsel for President Andrew Johnson in the latter's impeachment trial before the Senate. He won that one.)

So, no, Senators-elect, stay away from this cheap snake oil. It will make a legislature gag.

#6753. From Caramelized Amber to Bombshell Blowouts, These Are Fall’s Biggest Hair Trends

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Hairstylists share the buzziest fall hair trends their clients can’t get enough of.

When dried leaves in varying hues of orange, red, and yellow crunch under our feet, it’s basically a bat signal that the best season is underway. Fall sits right in the middle of summer’s balmy rays and winter’s biting chill, with a refreshing briskness that won’t necessarily expedite reversion for curly hair going straight and won’t dry out freshly bleached and/or dyed strands. The options are endless in this kind of weather, which can make decision-making feel a bit overwhelming. Do you try a new color? A bob cut? Maybe a pixie? According to the hottest hair trends of the season, you can do it all.

The transition from summer to fall is an important one for your hair. “Hair can be a little more compromised or porous based on the environment, from swimming pools and sun in the summer. Going from fall to winter, hair treatments to encourage hydration and strength are the easiest way to get your hair back on track,” says Eric Marshall Wright, stylist and owner of Eric Marshall Salon in Nashville, Tennessee. With a proper hair routine at home, your hair becomes strong enough to withstand your next hair transformation—that’s where fall’s biggest hair trends come in.

If you’re itching for a new hairdo, we polled several hairstylists, including Wright, to see what they predict will be the most head-turning hairstyles of the season, straight from their very own salons. Whether you’re a balayage connoisseur or tempted to do a bob, allow this list of trends to inspire you ahead of your next appointment.

Here’s a subtle take on the popular balayage technique that works with your hair color, creating a soft gradient with lowlights and highlights. “These highlights are soft, tone-on-tone highlights with dimension—think of them as a soft whisper in an overstimulated world. They look underdone instead of overdone; it’s a delicate hair color that’s sustainable and grounded. The look is effortlessly cool, glossy, and glazed for a finish that’s organically imperfect,” says Leslie Shores, color specialist at Eric Marshall Salon. This shade is perfect for “anyone who prefers a timeless, sustainable color. It’s achieved with no bleach, lightener, balayage, or ammonia-free demi-permanent hair color. Ask your colorist for soft tone-on-tone color.”

Sometimes the road to blonde is far too intimidating from dark brown or black. Shores’ answer to that gives you a lift without veering too much into blonde territory. “Cocoa brondette,” she says, is “a rich brunette with soft ribbons of deep indulgent brown shades.” The color is reminiscent of a rich cup of hot cocoa, with swirls of light brown throughout. “This shade looks best on someone with an olive skin tone, and can be achieved with demi-permanent or permanent hair color or with balayage. Ask your colorist for an all-over color with ammonia-free color around a level seven, six, or five; they can also paint soft ribbons of balayage.”

While most of the world is obsessed with bobs and pixies, another percentage of the population still favors long locks. Dimitris Giannetos, celebrity hairstylist and Great Lengths ambassador, says long hair isn’t going anywhere. “Hair can take a long minute to grow, so I have the help of Great Lengths, especially the Linea Wefts, to achieve the perfect, lived-in long hair,” he shares. His favorite hair trend this season? “CEO blowout,” which looks polished “but secure enough to get a bit messy.” This looks even better when paired with a “quiet, lived-in color, with more wispy, secret highlights versus an obvious balayage,” he adds. Your tools to achieve this are simple: “I use three different shades of the Great Lengths Line Wefts. They have such a fine spine, which makes them easy to blend and fully match your client’s color with an extra twist. They are super flat on the scalp, so they blend together perfectly without unwanted volume on the sides. I always recommend razoring the wefts at the end to create the perfect blend,” Giannetos says.

Wright concurs, adding that layers can elevate long styles that “looks effortless and moves.” Placement matters when it comes to layering hair, depending on the style you wear most often. “Layers can be as short as collarbone so you can easily put it up, but this is more about wearing it down and long-styled. Think hippie boho or 60s and long like Pocahontas. The cut is accomplished by point cutting and dry cutting techniques to make it soft and lived-in, not overly aggressive but effortless layers,” Wright shares.

If you want a louder balayage, Shores encourages you to opt for “rich shades of buttery gold and copper throughout the hair that look warm, rich, cozy, and earthy.” The gradient of hues might immediately scream summer, but it offers a toasted finish that “adds exotic warmth, depth, and dimension to the hair that feels like golden hour. Ask your colorist to paint big balayage pieces mainly in the front of the hair to match the volume and layers. These can be foiled or painted on. It looks best on brunettes and someone with thicker hair with layers.”

Already blonde? Shores suggests “bright, airy white-blonde balayage highlights that are airy and dreamy placed perfectly, as they carry a lightness to them that make them look ethereal and serene.” This look works great for those who are already “bright platinum without the constant touch up. [It’s also] perfect also for grey blending. This look can be achieved by using bleach balayage or foilyage. Ask your colorist to use bleach balayage or foilyage to get as light as possible!” she adds.

“Thanks to Off Campus and [actress] Ella Bright, we’re gravitating towards this shoulder-length bob that is worn flipped, sleek, curled, or blown out,” Wright says. Pictured here, this bob is marked by an asymmetrical blunt cut and soft waves throughout. “The cut needs to have facial shape and neck width taken into consideration, as those will cause different results, as well as the natural density and hair diameter,” Wright adds.

With summer’s sticky weather behind us, fall’s coolness means those bangs you’ve been dying to get won’t get too stuck to your forehead. “Being in a humid environment, they can devastate you in the summer, as the humidity makes them fussy. A fringe or bang can make you look styled and undone at the same time, meaning you can just style your bangs and pull your hair up to look chic. I recommend a fuller style rather than wispy so they look intentional. Keep hairline, cowlicks, and recession in mind, as bangs can have a mind of their own; style them first, as they can be difficult to dry on their own. For a softer, fuller look, ask your stylist to point-cut the ends for softness,” Wright explains.

Davines This is a Dry Texturizing Finishing Spray

“For long, high-volume natural textures, go with Davines Dry Texturize. This enhances volume on the mid-shaft through ends, especially for those who like texture and volume on their ends. Clipping [the hair] back [is an easy way] to give high volume to a half-up/half-down look and secure it with a claw clip.”—Eric Marshall Wright

“Those with sleek or textured bobs, I personally would recommend the Davines Shimmering Mist, as it creates high shine with zero hold. This could really emphasize the texture of the bob whether it’s straight or blown out or done with waves.”—EMW

Davines Hair Refresher, Dry Cleansing Shampoo

“As far as bangs go, I highly recommend the Davines Hair Refresher because bangs can be tricky and you sometimes need to rewash them. It’s nice to have something that is powder base to refresh them, that way you’re not re-styling them or rewetting them—you’re just refreshing them.”—EMW

Featuring a fall-ready leopard design, Wright loves these clips for tightly securing long hair in half-up, half-down styles.

#6752. What Will James Bond’s New Era Actually Look Like?

Let’s pass the time until Amazon finally decides to tell us the identity of the new 007.

It’s hard not to get swept up in the James Bond speculation. Has Callum Turner landed the role, as reports suggest? Does Jack Lowden still have a shot? Or will they just give up, feed a JPEG of Sean Connery into ChatGPT, and see what it pumps out? It’s all a fun guessing game, really. But one factor is arguably more important than the man playing 007: the new media machine behind it all.

Daniel Craig's fifteen-year tenure churned through four directors—Martin Campbell, Marc Forster, Sam Mendes, and Cary Joji Fukunaga—who all operated under the watchful eye of Barbara Broccoli and Michael G. Wilson. Their family had held creative control since the film franchise's inception. That was, until the pair sold their share to Amazon last year. Bezos and co.'s first order of business was to hire Dune director Denis Villeneuve, arguably the franchise's first-ever auteur, alongside Peaky Blinders scribe Steven Knight—and it's their collective vision that will really define this new era.

Villeneuve is, in my mind, the philosopher king of modern blockbuster cinema. On top of the scale, flair and the star-studded casts that he brings to his grandest films, including the Dune trilogy, he can be trusted to pose challenging ethical and political questions. He'll no doubt bring that same lens to a franchise that has tried, with varying degrees of success, to address hot-button issues.

Ian Fleming's original novels, and the early films to an extent, position Bond as a near-unstoppable, ass-kicking international superspy serving "Queen and country" with little complaint; a wish-fulfilling antidote to the slow but terminal decline of the British Empire.

On-screen, that approach started to shift in the 1990s. Pierce Brosnan's smirking spy is mocked as a relic of the Cold War. Daniel Craig's grittier films took that further, reckoning with the character's increasingly outmoded presence in a culture that had moved beyond sexist wisecracks and silly gadgets. There was plenty of flag-waving along the way, but Broccoli's films nonetheless asked questions about Britain's standing on the world stage. They even touched on decolonization, albeit on the surface level.

Bringing a Canadian on board may have raised the eyebrows of those expecting Bond 26 to carry on with that kind of national introspection, especially in this current moment, as the exponential rise of the far-right that's swept across Europe in the past ten years has dug its claws deep into British culture. But Villeneuve's filmography is proof that he's up to that job, if he so chooses. Sicario was a refreshingly thoughtful take on a cartel thriller that allowed him to question the morality, stances, and intervention of the CIA in an international operation. Equally, Dune interrogates a messianic white savior in Paul Atreides and whether simply positioning oneself as a hero is enough to make yourself one.

Amazon's involvement is where this potentially gets complicated. The pressure is on for the tech giant to produce an explosion-heavy, action-packed money-spinning Bond blockbuster with an international, classic silver screen appeal: in other words, to not screw it up and get their money's worth. Will they be as interested in dealing with Britain's post-imperial neurosis as the Broccoli family were? Or will Bezos’s Bond be laser-focused on grander, more universal anxieties (if even that)?

If one person can stop that from happening, it’s Steven Knight. From big-screen projects like Spencer, his take on Princess Diana’s troubled years in the palace, through to series like Peaky Blinders, House of Guinness and SAS Rogue Heroes, questions of national and cultural identity are central to his writing. Plus, as you'd expect, he's a proper Bond fan.

“It's fantastic to be invited to do it—I can't wait to get started,” he told The Times last year. “I'm hoping that, being a Bond fan for so many years, it will be imbued into me and I will be able to produce something that's the same but different, and better, stronger and bolder.”

Hand-in-hand with Villeneuve, we could be in for something special.
